## [EvolveTrade: Self-Evolving LLM Trading Agents Refine Their Own Prompts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17632) ⭐️ 7.0/10

EvolveTrade introduces a self-evolving framework that treats a tool-using LLM trading agent's system prompt as a text-parameterized policy, which a separate Policy Agent revises after each update interval using accumulated decision traces and realized portfolio feedback while keeping the backbone LLM frozen. Experiments across multiple market regimes and two LLM backbones show the approach often improves Sharpe Ratio and Cumulative Return over fixed-policy LLM baselines. Most LLM trading agents rely on static, hand-written tool-use policies fixed before deployment, which limits their ability to adapt evidence gathering, tool invocation, and risk management as market regimes shift. Showing that the reusable procedure governing tool use can be self-improved without retraining the backbone LLM points to a practical path toward more robust and adaptive financial agents, with implications for broader agent-adaptation research. The framework keeps the backbone LLM fixed and only updates the system prompt policy, so gains come from procedure refinement rather than model retraining; behavioral analyses show self-evolved policies increase code-mediated analysis and activate regime-relevant computations, with case-level policy-to-return attributions tracing how allocation changes drive realized return differences. Improvements are reported in most, but not all, evaluated settings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: LLM trading agents combine market data, news, and executable analysis, but their behavior is typically governed by hand-written prompts that specify how to gather evidence, call tools, and manage risk. The Sharpe Ratio is a standard risk-adjusted performance metric defined as excess return over the risk-free rate divided by return volatility. Self-evolving agents are a growing research direction in which agents iteratively improve their own policies or procedures through experience rather than through gradient-based retraining.

## [Physics-Constrained Digital Twins Detect Stealthy Attacks on Pedestrian Counting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17635) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.17635) formalizes stealthy false data injection attacks against city-scale pedestrian counting systems and proposes a physics-constrained digital twin that detects them using a flow conservation residual and adaptive conformal calibration. Evaluated on six years of Melbourne data, the approach achieves an attack margin of 0.54 against a single compromised device, dropping to 0.19 when a third of the sensor fleet is compromised on a network where only 1.18% of walkable segments are metered. Pedestrian counting data increasingly feeds economic indicators, urban planning, and safety operations, yet existing digital twins treat incoming sensor streams as ground truth, leaving smart-city sensing vulnerable to manipulation. This work extends stealthy false data injection analysis—previously characterized mainly for power and water networks—to urban pedestrian sensing, where the observation map is far more rank deficient, and shows that physics-based conservation laws provide detection value beyond mere spatial locality. The twin estimates directed flows on the pedestrian street graph, assimilates counts through a learned graph-localized gain, and is trained against a flow conservation residual coupling metered and unmetered segments; detection combines the innovation with that residual, with the alarm threshold set by adaptive conformal calibration rather than manual tuning. Replacing the street graph with a distance graph collapses the attack margin to 0.09, demonstrating that the gain comes from the conservation law rather than locality, and the attack margin is defined as the relative reduction in worst-case corruption of the estimated flow field against a white-box adversary optimizing directly through the twin.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: A digital twin is a computational model that continuously synchronizes with a real-world physical system using live data, going beyond ordinary simulation. False data injection attacks corrupt sensor measurements to mislead state estimation, and 'stealthy' variants are crafted to evade bad-data detection; such attacks have been extensively studied in power grids but not in urban pedestrian sensing. Conformal prediction is a distribution-free uncertainty quantification framework that produces statistically valid prediction sets or intervals by calibrating nonconformity scores on labeled data, and here it is used to set detection thresholds with coverage guarantees.

## [Sixty-Society Study Confirms Evidence Masking Boosts Compositional Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17637) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A preregistered study with sixty four-cell modular systems sharing a frozen language-model backbone found that masking evidence improved accuracy on held-out two- and three-operation compositions by median paired differences of 0.846 and 0.859. All twelve preregistered pairs cleared the required margins, and the unmarked replication also passed. This provides strong preregistered evidence that restricting what a module can read can improve compositional generalization in modular systems, a key challenge for building AI that generalizes to novel combinations. The large effect sizes and public protocols could influence how researchers design modular architectures and training regimes. The study varied evidence masking, ownership markers, and replacement of foreign evidence with neutral filler across six initialization clusters, each with two data orders. No globally visible system passed the marker-following check, leaving the effect of usable role information unresolved, and the filler condition's decomposition criteria were inconclusive.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Compositional generalization is the ability of a machine learning system to handle novel combinations of known components, such as new sequences of operations. Modular systems with a frozen language-model backbone keep the pretrained language model fixed and train smaller modules that communicate through learned continuous packets, which can be tested for how well they generalize to unseen compositions.

## [CapMem Benchmark Tests Caption Memory for Egocentric Video QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17688) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced CapMem, a human-annotated benchmark for caption-based episodic memory in egocentric video, comprising 75 videos totaling 33.7 hours and 1,000 multiple-choice questions across 16 scenarios. On long videos over 20 minutes, full-coverage CaptionQA with 30-second and 60-second caption windows outperformed direct VideoQA for 10 of 12 and 8 of 12 models, respectively. This work shows that textual captions can serve as reusable, compact episodic memory, offering a practical alternative to bounded frame budgets and growing visual-token costs in wearable assistants. It could influence how memory-constrained vision-language systems are designed for long egocentric video understanding. A matched-frame control across six Qwen models retained mean accuracy gains of 3.22 and 2.55 points for the 30-second and 60-second caption windows, and a caption-guided retrieve-and-verify harness further improved accuracy by up to 5.3 points. The benchmark defines the Episodic Memory Video Caption QA task and spans 16 scenarios.

Vision-language models can answer questions about video but are limited by how many frames they can process and the cost of visual tokens, which makes long-video understanding difficult. CapMem investigates whether converting video into textual captions can provide a more efficient memory representation for question answering.

## [GraphEcho Benchmark Tests Whether LLM Graph Agents Confuse Repeated Paths with Real Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17695) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.17695) introduces GraphEcho, a benchmark that varies path counts and evidential origins while holding evidence content fixed, to test whether LLM graph agents mistake repeated encounters with the same graph paths for additional corroboration. The authors also propose provenance-aware post-training (PAPT), which reduces revisits and improves accuracy on synthetic tasks but covers fewer distinct sources, and on scientific claims reduces repetition while accuracy declines. This work exposes a gap between efficient exploration and effective evidence use in LLM agents, showing that an agent can learn to stop repeating itself while still overlooking information it needs. It matters for researchers and builders of graph-based and tool-using agents, because it offers a controlled way to evaluate not just what agents conclude but whether their exploration actually reaches distinct evidential sources. In controlled synthetic experiments, redundant supporting paths increased the share of repeated walks across all evaluated frozen agents, and judgment shifts were model-dependent. PAPT reduces revisits and improves synthetic accuracy but covers fewer distinct sources, and on scientific claims it continues to reduce repetition while accuracy declines, illustrating a trade-off between exploration efficiency and evidence coverage.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: LLM agents increasingly operate over graphs, following paths through nodes and edges to gather evidence for a conclusion. Evidence provenance refers to tracking where a piece of evidence came from and whether it is independent, complete, and unaltered, as opposed to simply logging activity. GraphEcho builds on this idea by asking whether agents treat structurally redundant paths — repeated walks that revisit the same sources — as if they were independent corroboration, and whether post-training can correct that behavior.

## [NVIDIA Releases NeMo Data Designer for Multimodal Synthetic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17699) ⭐️ 7.0/10

NVIDIA has introduced NeMo Data Designer (NDD), an open-source, general-purpose framework for multimodal synthetic data generation that uses a declarative, column-based configuration format covering text, code, structured outputs, images, embeddings, and statistical samplers. The framework is available on GitHub and NVIDIA's build platform, and it ships with a preview-and-revision workflow, a plugin system, and runtime dependency resolution with automatic retries. Synthetic data is becoming a core part of AI development, with Gartner projecting that 60% of AI development data would be synthetic, up from just 1% in 2021, so a standardized, inspectable framework from NVIDIA lowers the barrier for teams building training and evaluation datasets. Because NDD configurations are shareable artifacts and the plugin system is extensible, it could become a common substrate for reproducible dataset pipelines across enterprise and research settings. NDD's configuration is an inspectable artifact that supports workflow sharing and reproducibility, and the preview-and-revision loop lets users generate a small number of records, refine the specification, then rerun at full scale. At runtime the framework resolves dependencies, schedules calls to user-provided model endpoints, and retries failed requests; case studies cover structured, agentic, multimodal, and domain-specialized tasks, including datasets used in Nemotron model development and production enterprise deployments.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Synthetic data generation (SDG) uses models to create artificial datasets that mimic real-world data, which is useful when real data is scarce, sensitive, or expensive to label. Multimodal SDG extends this to multiple data types such as text, images, and code within a single dataset. Declarative configuration means users describe what each dataset column should contain rather than writing imperative generation code, which makes pipelines easier to inspect, share, and reproduce.

## [Compact Multimodal Imitation Policy Drives Collision-Free in CARLA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17757) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.17757) presents a 1.36-million-parameter multimodal imitation learning policy that drives autonomously for hours without collisions in the CARLA simulator, using five-frame histories of RGB images, LiDAR, vehicle telemetry, and lane waypoints to predict throttle, brake, and steering at 20 Hz. The policy was trained on 236,882 windows (about 3.3 hours of driving from 448 captures) and also transferred qualitatively to an unseen CARLA town with different road geometry. This work shows that a compact behavioral cloning policy can achieve meaningful closed-loop driving competence from offline demonstrations alone, which is important because behavioral cloning often suffers from compounding errors in closed-loop deployment. The released code, checkpoint, ONNX model, and data sample lower the barrier for reproducible research in autonomous driving imitation learning. The policy is trained on 236,882 windows representing about 3.3 hours of driving from 448 captures, and the authors report offline metrics while distinguishing measured results from qualitative closed-loop observations. They also observed recovery from large trajectory deviations but explicitly do not claim systematic recovery without controlled evaluation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: CARLA is an open-source simulator built for autonomous driving research, providing a modular API for training and validating driving systems. Behavioral cloning is a simple imitation learning technique that uses supervised learning on expert observation-action pairs to learn a policy, but it often generalizes poorly and fails to recover from errors in closed-loop settings. Multimodal imitation learning policies combine multiple input modalities, such as cameras and LiDAR, to improve perception and decision-making.

## [SAGE Turns Enterprise Guidelines into Governed Work Artifacts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17775) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SAGE introduces a governed multi-stage LLM pipeline that converts enterprise guideline documents into structured work artifacts, cutting turnaround from two to three days of manual effort to 20-100 minutes. On 120 documents it achieved a 96% document-level success rate with 3.2% hallucination, extracting 3,896 rules and producing 812 artifacts ready for human review. Enterprise guideline documents mixing narrative text, complex tables, and embedded images are a widespread pain point, and existing language and vision-language models only extract content without any governed workflow. SAGE's validation, consistency checking, and provenance tracking address the trust and auditability gaps that have blocked enterprise adoption of LLM-based document automation. The pipeline is organized around a shared versioned rule store with stable identifiers, schema-validated inter-stage contracts, and end-to-end provenance tracking; extracted rules pass deterministic structural validation and LLM-based semantic scoring before a consistency module removes duplicates, flags contradictions, and surfaces specification gaps. Only uncertain or flagged items reach reviewers while high-confidence outputs are auto-approved, and without governance the hallucination rate rises to 15.7%.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Enterprise guideline documents often combine narrative text, complex tables, and embedded images, and converting them into structured work artifacts has traditionally required two to three days of manual effort each. Language models and vision-language models can extract information from such documents, but they offer no governed workflow beyond extraction — no validation, no consistency checking, and no traceable artifact generation. SAGE is a multi-stage LLM pipeline designed to fill that governance gap by adding schema-validated contracts, a versioned rule store, and provenance tracking to the extraction process.

## [Paper Proposes Individuated Utility Functions for Heterogeneous Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17847) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces individuated utility functions conditioned on both the individual and their decision context, along with a multi-stage architecture for estimating them from multi-modal data. The framework is evaluated on a newly collected dataset of over 575,000 pairwise aesthetic judgments from 2,398 participants comparing automotive wheel designs, where individuated utility models substantially outperform universal utility models and foundation model baselines. This work challenges the universal-utility assumption in current RLHF and preference-learning methods, showing that annotator disagreement reflects meaningful preference heterogeneity rather than annotation noise. It could lead to reward models that explicitly account for whose preferences they represent, improving AI alignment and personalization in subjective domains. The approach draws on rational choice theory to model observed choices as arising from heterogeneous but internally consistent utility functions. The evaluation focuses on a subjective aesthetic domain (automotive wheel designs), and the paper emphasizes the importance of collecting annotator attributes to learn individuated utilities.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) is a central paradigm for training modern AI systems, where a model of human utility serves as a reward model to guide policy learning. Existing methods typically assume a single universal utility function shared across a population and treat disagreements between annotators as stochastic noise. Rational choice theory provides a framework for modeling human behavior as the outcome of rational actors weighing costs and benefits, which this paper uses to justify heterogeneous, individuated utility functions.

## [Pareto Atlas Maps LLM Inference Optimization Trade-offs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17863) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.17863) builds a cost, quality, and latency Pareto atlas by measuring 54 configurations of Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct on vLLM 0.12 across L4, A100, and H100 GPUs, then calibrating a simulator with cross-campaign drift below 1.5 percent. On the calibrated grid, 18 of 36 configurations reach the Pareto frontier, with combined optimizations reaching it more often (9 of 15) than single methods (9 of 21). LLM inference optimizations are usually reported on different models, GPUs, and metrics, making them hard to compare or combine; this atlas gives practitioners an actionable, constraint-driven guide for choosing configurations. It shows that quality testing can change the winners, and that the best choice depends on the deployment constraint and GPU, with H100 winning for tight latency and A100 for throughput and low cost at $0.106 per million tokens. Quality evaluation on 200 GSM8K questions shows AWQ 4-bit cuts per-token latency to 0.34x baseline on L4 but loses 5.9 percent strict accuracy, narrowly missing the 95 percent quality floor, while flexible answer extraction matches FP16 accuracy, suggesting the loss is formatting-related. FP8 weights retain 99.4 percent accuracy at 0.61–0.65x latency and appear in three of four regime winners, whereas a naive FP8 KV cache maintains throughput but answers none of the 200 questions correctly, and n-gram speculative decoding adds no benefit on this stack.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: vLLM is an open-source high-throughput inference and serving engine for large language models, originally developed at UC Berkeley's Sky Computing Lab and centered on PagedAttention for efficient key-value cache memory management. A Pareto frontier is a concept from multi-objective optimization describing the set of configurations where no objective can be improved without worsening another, which is useful when balancing cost, quality, and latency. Quantization methods such as AWQ and FP8 reduce model weight precision to cut memory and latency, but can degrade output quality if applied naively.

Researchers introduced ERPBench, a benchmark that evaluates screenshot-only computer-use agents on a live, reproducible ERP system and scores each task against ground-truth values in the underlying database rather than against on-screen appearance. They also released a production-grade harness that gates agent actions behind human approval, and found that strong general GUI performance does not transfer to enterprise reliability. Enterprise ERP systems run finance, procurement, inventory and customer operations for organizations worldwide, so unreliable agents can silently corrupt persistent business records. By showing that agents may save a form in up to 85% of runs but write the correct value in as few as 3%, ERPBench exposes a critical evaluation gap that could reshape how enterprise agents are tested and deployed. The benchmark evaluates six closed and open-source agents on a live ERP system, scores tasks against ground-truth database values, and includes a human-approval safety harness that ERPBench itself runs autonomously. It also characterizes failure modes specific to enterprise workflows, such as dense interfaces, coordinated multi-step interactions, and errors that alter persistent records instead of surfacing on screen.

Existing enterprise benchmarks for such agents rely on proprietary platforms or simulated approximations of ERP software, which limits reproducibility and fails to capture whether an agent actually changed the underlying business data correctly.

## [Paper Proposes Geometric Dynamical Memory for Personalized AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17969) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.17969v1) argues that long-horizon personalization should model memory as a user-specific dynamical state space with locally heterogeneous geometry, rather than as discrete records in a static latent space accessed by a single global similarity notion. Under this framework, memory access becomes trajectory-conditioned reconstruction of the user's current state, where interaction, feedback, and elapsed time induce trajectories through that space. This reframing could shift how personalization and long-term memory systems are designed, moving them from searchable record stores toward dynamical state estimation, which may improve handling of user drift, volatile preferences, and uncertainty. It matters for anyone building memory layers for assistants, recommenders, or agents that must stay coherent with a user over months or years. The paper treats geometry as a computational language rather than a literal claim about cognition, using it to capture stable versus volatile regions, variable-rate drift, heterogeneous neighborhoods, and uncertainty about the current user state. Profiles and isolated events remain useful as points, but the authors stress that the evidence is a temporal event stream, which creates a mismatch with the dominant searchable-record-set abstraction in data mining.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Dynamical state space models describe a system through a finite set of state variables whose values fully characterize its behavior over time in response to inputs, a standard tool in control theory and probabilistic graphical models. Recent machine learning work on Riemannian metric learning shows that global Euclidean distances often fail to capture complex data structure, motivating locally heterogeneous geometries. Trajectory-conditioned reconstruction, meanwhile, is an established idea in generative and physical modeling, where a conditioning trajectory guides estimation of a target state.

## [Confidence-Gated Hybrid Beats LLM-Only Pipelines for Emotion Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17977) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper compares three deployment strategies for dialogue-contextual emotion recognition in conversation (ERC): a low-cost stacked ensemble, off-the-shelf LLM prompting with GPT-4o-mini, and a confidence-gated hybrid that escalates only the ensemble's least-confident predictions to the LLM. On IEMOCAP the ensemble beats every LLM configuration (0.595 vs. 0.460-0.536 weighted F1, p < 0.0001), but the ranking reverses on MELD and CMU-MOSI, and the hybrid Pareto-dominates both pure systems on all three datasets (0.620, 0.643, 0.824 weighted F1) at roughly $10-85 per million utterances versus $99-170 for LLM-only. This gives contact-center-as-a-service (CCaaS) and conversational-AI teams a concrete, auditable recipe for deciding when to spend on LLM inference versus cheaper models, since cost and latency matter as much as accuracy in production. It also shows that neither pure approach is a safe default across datasets, so a hybrid routing policy is the more robust deployment choice. The ensemble uses sentence embeddings, windowed context, and RandomForest/XGBoost/logistic-regression stacking, achieving sub-10ms latency, and its confidence is well-calibrated and tends to be under-confident rather than over-confident. Escalated turns disproportionately follow an emotion or sentiment shift, giving operators an interpretable routing signal, and the pattern holds across three datasets and two LLM providers.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Emotion recognition in conversation (ERC) is a production capability behind agent-assist prompts, escalation routing, and post-call analytics in CCaaS platforms. Stacked ensembles combine predictions from multiple base models into a stronger meta-model, while LLM prompting uses a large model directly with zero-shot, few-shot, or chain-of-thought instructions. Confidence-gated cascading is an established pattern in general ML systems where a cheap model handles most traffic and an expensive one is called only on hard cases; this paper's contribution is showing it transfers cleanly to dialogue-contextual ERC.

## [Contiguity Beats Importance for Budgeted KV-Cache Repair After Edits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17983) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.17983) formulates in-place KV-cache repair after document edits as a budgeted recomputation problem and compares training-free position-selection policies on a factual RAG benchmark with matched direct and derived edits. Across three model families, a contiguous edit-local window recovers at least 0.94 of the post-edit answer margin and substantially outperforms attention-based, KV-deviation, and structural selectors, while running 13-21 times faster than full re-prefill. KV-cache reuse is central to lowering inference cost in retrieval-augmented generation and agentic systems, but stale caches after edits can silently corrupt model behavior. This work shows that a simple, unconditional edit-local repair policy is both effective and cheap, offering a practical recipe for engineers maintaining long-lived cached contexts. The edit-local advantage depends on adjacency and largely disappears when the answer-bearing text moves downstream; mechanistic analysis shows that position sets effective under clean-state transplantation can fail under actual recomputation because scattered positions inherit surrounding staleness. Because answer-relevant edits almost always corrupt model behavior and failure severity is hard to predict, the authors support unconditional edit-local repair when dependent text remains adjacent to the edit.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: In transformer-based LLMs, the KV cache stores key and value tensors for previously processed tokens so they need not be recomputed, which is essential for efficient long-context serving. Because causal self-attention lets each token attend to all preceding tokens, a local edit can propagate and make downstream cached states stale, so systems must decide which positions to recompute. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) supplies external documents to the model, and when those documents or user state change, the cache must be repaired to keep answers consistent.

## [TuiML: A Machine-Learning Library Built for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17984) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers released TuiML, a self-contained machine-learning library on arXiv (2609.17984) that provides native algorithms across supervised, unsupervised, time-series, data handling, tuning, and evaluation tasks, with every component self-described through machine-readable metadata and parameter schemas. It validates, seeds, and traces every call, exports sessions as runnable notebooks, and drives MCP, agent-framework adapters, a Python API, a CLI, and local model serving from a single specification layer. Existing libraries like scikit-learn and Weka were designed for human programmers, so agents must recall APIs from memory and write code, which hides what a library offers, delays errors until runtime, and loses experimental state between turns. TuiML addresses this gap by making machine learning searchable, composable, and reproducible for agents, potentially influencing how agent-native tooling is built. Benchmarks show TuiML remains predictively competitive with scikit-learn and Weka, and data and models never leave the machine since everything runs locally. It is open source with documentation at tuiml.ai, but it is an early arXiv paper without proven adoption or community discussion yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: The Model Context Protocol (MCP) is an open standard introduced by Anthropic in November 2024 that lets AI applications connect to external data sources, tools, and workflows, often compared to a USB-C port for AI. Weka and scikit-learn are long-established machine-learning libraries built for human users, and TuiML's design assumes agents—not people—are the primary consumers of ML tooling.

## [RideWay Benchmark Measures Efficiency of Tool-Using Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17985) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced RideWay, an efficiency-centered benchmark for ride-hailing agents in a stateful tool-calling environment, along with Efficiency Utility, a success-gated metric that discounts successful trajectories for excess tool calls and user-facing turns relative to task-specific reference effort. Across 58 tasks and 24 models, human paired preferences calibrate the penalties, with the fitted penalty for excess turns about twice that for excess tool calls. Most agent evaluations only check whether a task was completed, but in interactive service settings a successful agent can still frustrate users through repeated questions or redundant searches. RideWay makes interaction efficiency measurable alongside task success, giving the AI agent community a practical tool to compare models on user experience, not just correctness. On task-disjoint held-out preferences, Efficiency Utility achieves 78.7% accuracy overall: 90.6% when trajectories differ in turns, but only chance-level accuracy when they differ solely in tool calls, the axis on which human annotators agree least. This exposes the boundary of count-based tool-use evaluation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Tool-using language agents are LLM-based systems that can call external APIs or functions to accomplish tasks, and they are typically evaluated with benchmarks that only check final task success. In stateful tool-calling environments, each action can modify persistent state, so redundant calls or extra dialogue turns carry real cost and user friction. RideWay builds on this setting by adding an efficiency dimension calibrated against human preferences, rather than relying on raw counts alone.

## [EffiRAG Cuts Graph RAG Cost 57% While Beating LightRAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18099) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18099) introduces EffiRAG, a graph-based retrieval-augmented generation system that uses the graph only to locate relevant passages and then generates answers from the original text. On the UltraDomain benchmark of 120 open-ended questions across four domains, EffiRAG produced the preferred answer on 93 questions versus LightRAG-hybrid's 7 (20 splits), while cutting total system cost by 57%, from USD 0.952 to USD 0.408. Graph-based RAG systems such as Microsoft GraphRAG and LightRAG can answer multi-document questions but require many LLM calls during ingestion, raising the question of whether quality gains justify the cost. EffiRAG's results suggest that graph structure can be used more cheaply—pricing structure explicitly and preserving source evidence—which matters for teams deploying RAG at scale where ingestion cost dominates. EffiRAG keeps graph construction and query processing lightweight by generating answers from original passages rather than graph-derived summaries, and at 10 and 20 documents per domain it adds a lightweight, non-LLM filter to skip low-salience chunks, costing 4.2x and 4.5x less than LightRAG-hybrid respectively. The cost accounting includes LLM calls during both ingestion and querying, and the paper argues graph-based RAG should be evaluated on both answer quality and cost.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) lets a large language model retrieve relevant external documents before answering, improving factuality over relying on training data alone. Graph-based RAG, exemplified by Microsoft GraphRAG and LightRAG, builds a knowledge graph of entities and relationships from the corpus and uses it to retrieve information spanning many documents. Building such graphs typically requires many LLM calls during ingestion, which is expensive, so researchers are exploring how to reduce that cost without losing answer quality.

## [Portfolio Optimization for Agentic AI Workflows Under Compute Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18126) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18126) formulates a workflow portfolio problem for agentic AI, jointly optimizing run size and allocation across multiple workflows to balance performance gains from variety against compute costs and selection errors. It introduces an odds-lift index to summarize selector quality, derives sharp bounds on the value of workflow variety, and develops exact formulations, LP relaxations, randomized rounding, and a dual-based ellipsoid method with a pricing oracle for large implicit workflow classes. As agentic AI systems increasingly run multiple reasoning workflows per task, this work provides a principled framework for deciding how many executions to run and which workflows to allocate compute to, potentially reducing operational expense while improving accuracy. It bridges AI systems engineering and operations research, offering theoretical guarantees and practical algorithms relevant to deploying LLM-based agents at scale. The framework is evaluated on three datasets—ABCD, Schema-Guided Dialogue, and HotpotQA—where portfolio optimization improves held-out selector accuracy by 3.1, 7.5, and 0.9 percentage points over the best standalone workflow, respectively. Dual-guided workflow generation adds 3.5 points on ABCD and 24.1 on HotpotQA, with no additional gain on Schema-Guided Dialogue, and under a weak condition the method achieves near-optimal solutions with polynomially many oracle calls.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Agentic AI systems often approach the same task through multiple workflows that differ in reasoning strategy, verification structure, and compute cost. A naive deployment policy picks the workflow with the highest average performance, but this can be suboptimal because different workflows may succeed on different instances. The paper studies a portfolio-and-selector paradigm where a firm runs multiple workflow executions and selects the final answer after observing their outputs, trading off the chance of uncovering correct answers against extra compute and plausible distractors that complicate selection.

## [ContrAgent: Contract-Based Temporal Supervision for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18128) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers present ContrAgent, a contract-based framework that uses linear temporal logic over finite traces (LTLf) to symbolically supervise LLM agent behavior. Each assume-guarantee contract is compiled into a deterministic finite automaton (DFA) that both gates agent actions online and evaluates recorded traces offline, matching state-of-the-art LLM-judge and rule-based guardrails across four benchmarks while producing deterministic, reproducible verdicts and orders-of-magnitude lower per-call latency in online mode. Current safeguards for tool-using LLM agents either grade trajectories post hoc with stochastic LLM judges or block unsafe actions one call at a time, and no single deterministic artifact supports both roles. ContrAgent fills this gap by providing a reusable, model-independent contract library that enables deterministic verification and enforcement of safety properties, which is significant for AI safety and reliable agentic AI deployment. ContrAgent formalizes agent behavior as a trace over a fixed set of checkable predicates and specifies required behaviors using assume-guarantee contracts in LTLf. The contract library is maintained independently of the agent's model and can be applied across different agents within the same task domain, though the approach requires that relevant behaviors be expressible as checkable predicates.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: LLM agents augmented with tools can automate complex multi-step tasks such as web navigation, code generation, and workflow orchestration, but hallucinations, distributional instability, adversarial manipulation, and the irreversible consequences of certain tool calls can lead to harmful outcomes. Linear temporal logic over finite traces (LTLf) is a temporal logic whose semantics are adapted for finite words, enabling precise specification of terminating system behaviors. Assume-guarantee contracts are a modular formal-methods technique that divides responsibilities among components by specifying what a component must fulfill under assumptions about its environment. Deterministic finite automata (DFAs) are finite-state machines that accept or reject input strings deterministically, making them suitable for reproducible verification.

## [WFM: A Wiki Foundation Model for Agentic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18182) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18182) proposes WFM, a Wiki Foundation Model that replaces sparse graph knowledge representations with an "LLM Wiki" format combining dense document contexts and markdown files with multi-layered topological linkages. The work introduces a Wiki Graph schema, query-conditioned attentive aggregation with attention variance regularization, and an NCCL boundary exchange protocol that reportedly achieves 10.5x training acceleration on distributed clusters across five long-term agent memory and multi-hop reasoning benchmarks. Sparse graph representations have long constrained the semantic density and machine readability needed for complex agentic workflows, so shifting to an agent-native wiki representation could improve long-term memory and retrieval-augmented generation for real-world agents. If the reported 10.5x training speedup holds, it could also make large-scale commercial deployment of such knowledge systems more practical. The approach formalizes a Wiki Graph schema that maintains explicit topologies alongside continuous semantics, and uses an infrastructural NCCL boundary exchange protocol that hoists static partition indices and leverages fixed-shape GPU-to-GPU collectives to bypass CPU serialization and memory copy overheads. However, the abstract is truncated and no community discussion is available, so independent verification of the benchmark results and deployment claims is still lacking.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is a technique that lets large language models pull relevant text from external sources before generating a response, supplementing the parametric knowledge stored in their weights with non-parametric knowledge kept in an external datastore. Graphs have been a popular way to provide structured evidence for such retrieval, but their sparse nature limits how much dense semantic context they can carry. LLM Wiki is an emerging agent-native representation that compiles knowledge at ingest time by pairing dense document contexts with markdown files containing multi-layered topological linkages, aiming to improve representation rather than just retrieval.

Researchers posted arXiv preprint 2609.18249 introducing Re2A, a framework that formulates situated conversational recommendation (SCR) as a structured reason-then-align process. Re2A combines rubric-based preference reasoning, which uses automated rubrics to produce explicit preference states, with preference-conditioned optimization that aligns responses to both user preference satisfaction and situation consistency, and it reportedly outperforms state-of-the-art methods on two SCR datasets. Situated conversational recommendation is a hard, underexplored problem because assistants must jointly reason over dialogue history, co-observed scenes, and in-scene item attributes. A structured reason-then-align recipe with explicit preference states could influence future work in conversational AI and recommender systems, though as an unvalidated preprint its immediate impact remains moderate. Re2A's two components are rubric-based preference reasoning, which uses automated rubrics to guide the model toward explicit preference states, and preference-conditioned optimization, which aligns generation with the dual objectives of user preference satisfaction and situation consistency. The authors report extensive experiments on two SCR datasets and have released code at https://github.com/DongdingLin/Re2A.

Rubrics are natural-language criteria or checklists used to guide and evaluate model judgments, and preference-conditioned optimization refers to training or inference that adapts model behavior to a specified preference signal. Re2A applies both ideas to SCR, aiming to make the assistant's understanding of user preferences explicit before generating a recommendation.

## [REPAIR: Self-Evolving Data Augmentation Fixes Long-Tail Confusion in Scientific Retrievers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18262) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (arXiv:2609.18262) introduces REPAIR, a self-evolving data augmentation framework for scientific dense retrievers that iteratively synthesizes training data by cycling through diagnosis of long-tail concepts, API-guided evidence expansion, and differentiation via hard negative mining. The authors report that REPAIR significantly outperforms 19 strong baselines across nine materials science and biomedical benchmarks. Scientific retrieval is bottlenecked by rare, long-tail concepts and high fact-sensitivity, which limits both dense retrievers and hallucination-prone LLM augmentation. By grounding training data in verified facts and targeting knowledge gaps, REPAIR points to a practical path for more robust retrieval in specialized domains such as materials science and biomedicine. REPAIR's pipeline combines three stages: diagnosing long-tail concepts where the retriever fails, expanding evidence through API-guided retrieval, and mining hard negatives to force fine-grained distinctions. Evaluation covers nine materials science and biomedical benchmarks against 19 baselines, though as an arXiv preprint it has not yet undergone peer review.

Hard negative mining is a long-standing technique that selects negatives similar to positives but not actually relevant, providing stronger training signals for fine-grained discrimination. The long tail refers to the many rare items in a distribution that receive little training signal, a persistent problem for specialized scientific corpora.

Researchers released BENCHCOMPASS, a payment-domain benchmark built from typed evidence packs, LLM-based quality checks, attack variants, and expert admission, with an expert-reviewed Pro benchmark and a lower-assurance Normal pool. Across 16 model variants, the best frontier model scored 89.6% on Open Context-Grounded Reasoning and 81.7% under attacked inputs, while a representative 32B open-weight model reached 69.8% and 42.6%. Payment operations are critical financial infrastructure where rules change quickly and evidence is fragmented, yet existing benchmarks cannot isolate whether failures stem from missing payment-rule knowledge, poor use of supplied evidence, or brittleness under imperfect harness inputs. The benchmark covers payment knowledge, context-grounded scenario reasoning, and Attacked Open robustness, and it remains unsaturated since even the best frontier model does not reach ceiling performance. The construction pipeline reserves final item admission for domain experts, and benchmark data and code are available at https://github.com/ant-intl/BenchCompass.

An evaluation harness is the software infrastructure surrounding a large language model that standardizes prompts, scoring, and sampling so benchmark results are comparable across models. ## [Paper Proposes Independence-Graded Audit Protocol for Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18272) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18272v1) proposes grading audit independence for agentic AI along three orthogonal axes—principal, substrate, and evidence—rather than treating it as a binary. It formalizes the model by transplanting the beta-factor common-cause failure model from reliability engineering and provides a seven-step protocol whose outputs a third party can verify. As agentic AI systems increasingly act as both audit subjects and auditors, a binary notion of independence can mask shared-model, shared-toolchain, and self-reported-evidence failures. A graded, verifiable protocol could give regulators, procurement teams, and third-party auditors a practical way to quantify how much an "independent" audit actually shows. The paper aggregates the three axes by the weakest link and applies the same rubric when the auditor is itself an agent; a Monte Carlo study of a conventional internal audit of an agent (a real audit team, a second agent, and provider logs) surfaced only 5.9% of the faults it could in principle see and none at all in half the fault classes. It also maps the triple to the EU AI Act as amended, ISO/IEC 42006, UK public-sector risk-management guidance, and audit-regulator practice.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Agentic AI systems plan, invoke tools, and act with limited human supervision, which makes their assurance harder than that of traditional input-response models. Audit independence is the foundation of assurance, but in AI it is usually treated as a yes/no property. The beta-factor model is a long-standing reliability-engineering method for quantifying common-cause failures—cases where shared causes make redundant components fail together—and the paper repurposes it to capture shared foundation models, toolchains, or guardrails between auditor and auditee.

## [Energy-Memory Characterization of Agentic AI Across Edge-Cloud Continuum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18283) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces agentic-eCAL, a metric that generalizes the Energy Cost of AI Lifecycle (eCAL) to directed multi-agent workflows by combining a two-rate single-call energy model (compute-bound prefill and memory-bound decode) with 7-layer OSI data transport. Validated on hundreds of GPU configurations using NVIDIA A100 and H100, 16 open-weight models, and 8 orchestration topologies, the study finds that inter-agent text transport accounts for only 0.25% of total workflow energy across 5G RAN, metro, and optical links. This work fills a gap in existing AI lifecycle metrics that evaluate only isolated single-model inferences or ignore multi-agent execution graphs entirely, giving network operators a foundational model for deciding where agent teams should physically reside across edge-cloud tiers. As 6G networks aim for greater energy efficiency and embed agentic AI for autonomous operations, such placement guidance is critical for sustainable lifecycle orchestration. The key finding is that the dominant energy cost of distribution is often not the transmission of inter-agent text itself, but the additional inference and context processing induced by that communication. The metric is grounded in hundreds of GPU benchmark configurations on NVIDIA A100 and H100, 16 open-weight models, and 8 orchestration topologies, though the 0.25% transport figure is specific to the tested 5G RAN, metro, and optical link scenarios.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: The edge-cloud continuum describes a spectrum of computing resources, from tiny devices at the edge to centralized data centers in the cloud, working together to run and orchestrate AI workloads. Agentic AI workflows are systems where large language models execute multi-step reasoning, invoke diagnostic tools, retrieve domain knowledge, and coordinate across agent teams. 6G is the proposed sixth generation of mobile communications, expected to be commercially deployed in the early 2030s, with goals of higher data rates, lower latency, and greater energy efficiency than 5G, and with AI expected to help manage networks and optimize services.

## [Systematic Mapping of Chess Research on Humans, Engines, and LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18286) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18286) presents a systematic mapping study of recent chess research, covering human players, classical engines, neural and reinforcement-learning systems, LLMs, and hybrid approaches, organized into 84 core study families classified by agent type, strategic-reasoning stages, and evaluation dimensions. The map shows the literature is heavily concentrated on situation assessment, evaluation, and action selection, while explicit planning, explanation, metacognition, and human-AI collaboration remain underexplored. Chess has long been a controlled testbed for studying search, expertise, and decision-making, and the rise of LLMs has renewed its relevance for probing strategic reasoning. This mapping helps AI, cognitive science, and LLM-reasoning researchers see where the field is crowded and where gaps remain, particularly around planning, faithful explanation, and genuine human-AI complementarity. The authors note that LLM research emphasizes state representation and generalization, whereas grounded explanation appears more often in hybrid systems that combine language models with engines, expert knowledge, or other external structures. They also flag two unresolved distinctions: hybrid systems differ in where and when heterogeneous capabilities combine, and evaluations showing improved human performance do not by themselves establish human-AI synergy.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: A systematic mapping study is a research method that categorizes and catalogues existing literature on a broad topic to provide an overview, identify gaps, and guide future work, rather than answering one specific question like a systematic review. Chess is a classic benchmark domain in AI because it offers clear rules, measurable outcomes, and a long history of both human expertise and engine superhuman play, from classical search engines to neural and reinforcement-learning systems such as AlphaZero-style approaches.

## [GuardEn Compiles Safety Rules into Executable Visual Compliance Checks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18328) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose GuardEn (Guarding by Safety Rule Entailment), an executable safeguard framework that decomposes safety policies into atomic propositions via Safety-Rule Compilation and instantiates them at test time with scene-graph-derived visual context through Scene-Grounded Execution. On SafetyVisionBench, GuardEn achieves an average improvement of 9.8 F1 points over the strongest baseline. This work targets two persistent weaknesses of training-based end-to-end safeguards — adaptability to evolving risk patterns and explainability over complex rules — by making safety policies programmable and rule-grounded. It could benefit AI safety researchers and deployers who need auditable, updatable visual compliance checks rather than opaque model judgments. The framework separates policy authoring from execution: Safety-Rule Compilation turns rules into atomic propositions modeled as executable code, while Scene-Grounded Execution binds those propositions to contextual visual information extracted from scene graphs. Evaluation uses SafetyVisionBench, which combines real-world regulatory sources with violation cases spanning food safety, construction safety, platform-content safety, and advertising safety.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models (LLMs) and vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly used as safety systems that reason about contextual and semantic risks rather than only matching simple risk patterns. However, most such safeguards are trained end-to-end, which makes them hard to update when rules change and hard to explain when they flag a violation. GuardEn instead treats safety policies as programs: rules are compiled into atomic propositions and executed against structured scene-graph representations of an image, so the reasoning trace follows the rule structure. Scene graphs are graph representations that encode objects, attributes, and their relationships in a visual scene.

## [CHASE Uses Counterfactuals to Stop Agent Harnesses Gaming Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18366) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Counterfactual Harness Search and Evolution (CHASE), a method that treats harness evolution as constraint generation over validity-preserving benchmark counterfactuals. After each Proposer update, a Challenger searches for an executable protocol transformation that destroys the harness's benchmark gain, while a validity firewall ensures task semantics are preserved. Evaluated on a synthetic benchmark and OfficeQA, CHASE retains strong released-benchmark gains while substantially reducing gain destruction under valid protocol changes. Automatic harness optimization can inflate benchmark scores through benchmark-wide shortcuts rather than genuine capability, undermining the reliability of agent evaluation. CHASE offers a formal framework and statistical guarantees for detecting and neutralizing such shortcuts, which could make agent leaderboards and safety evaluations more trustworthy. The method formalizes an exact shortcut-neutralized benchmark B_0 and links finite counterfactual archives to B_0 through statistical guarantees, while also characterizing sequential Challenger search. A confirmation set decides whether a counterfactual enters the finite archive, and the validity firewall checks that task semantics are preserved before a counterfactual is admitted.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: An agent harness is the runtime scaffolding around a language model that manages prompts, memory, retrieval, tools, and control code, and its design can affect task performance as much as model choice. Automatic harness optimization searches this space using a released benchmark to guide a Proposer, but task holdout only varies semantic tasks while leaving the benchmark protocol fixed, letting a "bad genius" Proposer exploit benchmark-wide shortcuts. Counterfactual reasoning, which asks what would happen under alternative conditions, is used here to generate validity-preserving protocol transformations that expose such shortcuts.

## [ChatGPT 5.2 Passes a Finnish-Language Turing Test](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18394) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A study conducted in Finland found that ChatGPT 5.2 passed a Turing Test administered in Finnish, contrary to the researchers' expectation that the model would perform worse in a language and cultural context underrepresented in LLM training data. The authors also introduce model-generated role prompting as a replicable technique for running comparative LLM-based Turing Tests with improved construct validity. The result challenges the assumption that LLMs are inherently weaker in low-resource languages and cultural contexts, suggesting that evaluation outcomes depend as much on human participants' cues and AI literacy as on model capability. Reframing the Turing Test as a comparative probe of credible social membership offers a new lens for studying the human-machine boundary across domains and cultures. A prominent source of error was participants' reliance on linguistic cues, especially colloquial Finnish, as markers of human authorship, meaning the model's success partly reflected test-takers' heuristics rather than pure intelligence. The authors argue the outcome reflects model capabilities, prompted identity, insider competence among human participants, and their AI literacy, so the method should be read as a comparative probe rather than a pass/fail intelligence verdict.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: The Turing Test, proposed by Alan Turing in his 1950 paper "Computing Machinery and Intelligence," asks whether a machine can imitate human dialogue well enough that a human evaluator cannot reliably distinguish it from a person. Because languages and cultural contexts are unevenly represented in LLM training data, researchers expected a Finnish-language test to be harder for the model than previously studied English-language US contexts. Construct validity, a concept borrowed from psychometrics, concerns whether an evaluation instrument actually measures the abstract property it claims to measure, such as intelligence or social competence.

## [HPOQuest: Training-Free Agent Improves Rare-Disease Diagnosis via Active Phenotype Acquisition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18431) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers posted arXiv preprint 2609.18431 introducing HPOQuest, a training-free framework that performs sequential phenotype acquisition for rare-disease diagnosis. Starting from a small set of observed phenotypes, it maintains a probabilistic disease ranking and iteratively selects informative follow-up questions, achieving gains of up to 30 percentage points at Recall@1 and 45 percentage points at Recall@5 across four benchmark cohorts. Rare diseases affect more than 300 million people worldwide, yet diagnosis is often delayed because patients initially present with sparse and heterogeneous phenotypes. A training-free agent that actively asks targeted follow-up questions could make phenotype-driven diagnostic support practical in real clinical workflows without requiring expensive model retraining. HPOQuest uses the Human Phenotype Ontology (HPO) as its phenotype vocabulary, updating the disease ranking only when phenotypes are confirmed while using all responses to update the candidate question set. The results are reported on four benchmark cohorts, but the work is an arXiv preprint that has not yet undergone peer review.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: The Human Phenotype Ontology (HPO) is a standardized vocabulary of over 13,000 phenotypic abnormality terms with more than 156,000 annotations to hereditary diseases, widely used for phenotype-driven differential diagnostics. Recall@1 and Recall@5 measure whether the correct disease appears in the top 1 or top 5 ranked candidates, which matters because clinicians typically review a short differential list rather than a single prediction.

## [WetRobo: A Transferable Robot Kit Letting Coding Agents Automate Biology Labs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18435) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers released WetRobo, a reproducible robot kit consisting of one robot arm, lab equipment (an incubator, a capped reagent bottle, and a Petri dish), existing arm-control code, recorded teleoperation demonstrations, and a general AGENTS.md skill file. Using OpenAI Codex (gpt-5.6-sol), the coding agent successfully performed three real-world tasks — lifting a Petri dish lid, removing a bottle cap, and opening an incubator door — and completed the cap task in both Lab X and Lab Y, whereas a VLA policy fine-tuned on Lab X demonstrations failed to transfer to Lab Y. This work suggests a practical alternative to training a separate vision-language-action policy for every laboratory: instead, distribute a standardized kit and let a coding agent adapt it on site. If validated, this could lower the barrier for individual wet-lab researchers to delegate repetitive manipulation tasks to robots, accelerating laboratory automation across biology and chemistry. WetRobo requires no local teleoperation data collection or neural network training: the experimentalist simply provides natural-language tasks, and the coding agent observes the local lab and writes and executes programs, calling external tools when adaptation is needed. The kit, demonstrations, and evolved programs are open-sourced at https://github.com/tsudalab/WetRobo, though the results come from an arXiv preprint that has not yet undergone peer review.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language-action (VLA) policies are models that unify visual perception, language understanding, and motor control to let robots generalize across tasks, but they are typically trained on large demonstration datasets and can lose performance when the operating environment changes. Teleoperation — a human remotely controlling the robot to record demonstrations — is the dominant way to collect such training data, but it is labor-intensive and does not scale well across labs. Coding agents, by contrast, are AI systems that can read instructions, inspect their environment, and write and run code; AGENTS.md is a lightweight open convention for giving such agents project-specific skills and instructions.

## [VLM Verbalized Confidence Found Trajectory-Independent, New Metric Proposed](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18453) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18453) shows that verbalized confidence in vision-language models is largely trajectory-independent, meaning models can self-correct, arrive at a wrong answer, and still report high confidence. The authors propose the Trajectory-Grounding Score (TGS) in two forms—TGS-self and TGS-pair—along with TGS-Bench, a model-agnostic suite spanning 10 benchmarks with controlled good/bad trajectory pairs. This finding exposes a blind spot in current calibration evaluation: existing metrics like ECE and AUROC cannot detect this disconnect, and conventional calibration rankings diverge from trajectory-grounding rankings. It matters for AI reliability and trustworthiness, especially in high-stakes settings where users rely on model confidence to judge answer correctness. The paper examines the issue through three lenses—content variation, token masking, and the model's own hesitation markers—and finds that calibration training can paradoxically worsen the disconnect. TGS-self compares confidence with and without access to the model's own trajectory, while TGS-pair tests whether the model assigns higher confidence to correct trajectories than to flawed ones along the vision, reasoning, and answer axes.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) jointly interpret images and text, extending large language models to multimodal tasks; examples include GPT-4V, Gemini, Claude 3 Opus, LLaVA, and InstructBLIP. Confidence calibration aims to make a model's stated confidence match its actual accuracy, and metrics like Expected Calibration Error (ECE) and AUROC are standard tools for measuring this. The paper argues that these metrics miss a key failure mode: confidence that does not track the quality of the reasoning trajectory that produced the answer.

## [LGM Uses Sparse Autoencoders to Build Latent Memory Graphs for Personalized Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18461) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18461v1) introduces LGM, a neuro-symbolic framework that shifts long-term memory disentanglement for personalized agents into a continuous latent space. Instead of persisting fixed, query-agnostic graphs, LGM uses a sparse autoencoder to map historical interactions into latent memory nodes and sparse concept activations, dynamically synthesizing query-aware relational edge weights that a graph encoder then uses for non-linear message passing. Current structured memory frameworks rely on static, query-agnostic graphs that fail to capture context-dependent relations, and raw textual memories are entangled and noisy, making fine-grained personalization computationally prohibitive. LGM's query-conditioned latent graph approach could improve cross-session reasoning and both explicit and implicit preference capture for personalized agents, an increasingly important capability as agent memory research grows. The framework has two main components: a tailored latent graph construction with a sparse autoencoder that disentangles memory traces into sparse concept activations, and a graph encoder that treats the query embedding as a conditioning preference to direct non-linear message passing across a task-specific latent subgraph. Experiments on long-term personalization benchmarks reportedly show LGM significantly outperforming state-of-the-art baselines, though the abstract is truncated and no code or dataset details are provided.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Neuro-symbolic AI combines neural networks with symbolic reasoning to build more robust and interpretable systems. Sparse autoencoders are autoencoder variants inspired by the sparse coding hypothesis in neuroscience, where most code entries are near zero, yielding interpretable feature activations. Latent graph memory frameworks such as LatentGraphMem learn graph-structured memory in latent space with explicit subgraph retrieval, addressing the brittleness of explicit graph memories and the opacity of purely implicit ones. LGM builds on these ideas by making the latent graph query-aware and disentangled for personalization.

## [First Token Matters: Safety Collapse in Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18471) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.18471) identifies a localized safety vulnerability in Large Reasoning Models called Onset Refusal Collapse (ORC), where the refusal-related signal drops sharply at the very first generated token under harmful queries. The authors propose SafeToken, a lightweight inference-time intervention that injects a learned continuous safety anchor at reasoning onset and updates only a single token embedding. This work offers a mechanistic explanation for why safety alignment in reasoning models degrades, shifting the focus from retraining to a targeted, low-cost fix at inference time. If validated, such single-token interventions could make deployed reasoning models safer without sacrificing their problem-solving abilities. SafeToken updates only one token embedding yet reportedly mitigates ORC, improves safety on harmful-query benchmarks, and largely preserves reasoning utility. The paper is an arXiv preprint without peer review, and the intervention is applied at inference time rather than through additional training or preference optimization.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) are LLMs that generate extended chains of thought to solve complex problems, but their safety alignment can weaken when they handle harmful requests. Safety alignment refers to training techniques that make models refuse dangerous queries, and inference-time interventions modify model behavior during generation without changing core parameters. Token-level positional analysis examines how the model's internal signals evolve across each generated token to locate where safety failures originate.

## [Cunning Questions Training Boosts LLM Safety Vigilance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18515) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.18515) introduces 'cunning questions' — prompts that are not necessarily safety-related but contain misleading premises, atypical reasoning, or subtle inconsistencies — as a training signal to cultivate vigilance in large language models. Experiments show that Cunning training improves robustness to out-of-distribution jailbreak attacks and, when added to an existing state-of-the-art safety alignment pipeline, reduces mean attack success rate across nine backbone–benchmark combinations from 17.40% to 15.05%. Aligned models often fail when harmful intent is hidden inside seemingly benign contexts, so this work suggests a general-purpose training signal that transfers from non-safety reasoning traps to safety-critical scenarios. If the effect generalizes, it could complement conventional safety alignment and raise the bar for jailbreak defenses across deployed LLMs. The paper reports that Cunning training also strengthens subsequent safety fine-tuning, and trace analysis after matched safety fine-tuning suggests safety judgments are more likely to govern responses before harmful planning begins. A conditional theoretical analysis further characterizes when invariance learned from cunning data can transfer to safety-related inputs, though the work remains an unpeer-reviewed preprint.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Safety alignment typically teaches LLMs to recognize and refuse harmful requests, often via supervised fine-tuning or preference-based methods such as RLHF and DPO. However, attackers exploit out-of-distribution inputs — prompts that fall outside the safety training distribution — to bypass these defenses, a class of attacks known as jailbreaking. Adversarial training is a proactive defense that exposes models to adversarial examples during training to build inherent resilience, and this paper extends that idea by using misleading-premise questions rather than explicit attack prompts.

## [LLM In-Context Learning in Multi-Agent Games: Reasoning or Extrapolation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18591) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18591) disentangles recursive reasoning from statistical extrapolation in LLM in-context learning by testing agents in multi-agent incomplete-information public goods games. When the statistical structure of historical feedback is disrupted, the benefits of longer context largely vanish, degrading decision quality to the no-context baseline, an effect sharply amplified by stronger strategic interdependence. The finding suggests that in strategic environments, LLM in-context learning may rely on pattern extrapolation rather than genuine recursive belief reasoning, challenging assumptions about LLM strategic capabilities. This has implications for deploying LLM agents in negotiation, market, and other interdependent decision-making settings where robust reasoning is expected. The study uses a public goods game and evaluates decision quality against a history-independent rational expectations equilibrium (REE) benchmark, introducing REE as a diagnostic tool for distinguishing reasoning from extrapolation. The authors also provide a reusable framework for probing the boundaries of LLM reasoning in recursive belief tasks.

**Background**: In-context learning (ICL) is the ability of large language models to perform new tasks simply by conditioning on examples or interaction history in the prompt, without updating model parameters. Multi-agent incomplete-information games require recursive belief reasoning, where each agent reasons about others' beliefs and actions. Rational expectations equilibrium (REE) is a benchmark from economics in which agents' expectations are model-consistent and fully use available information.

Researchers propose MAGER, a multi-agent genetic evolution framework that automatically discovers meta-paths to compress complex propagation graphs into informative subgraphs for LLM-based fake news detection. The framework also introduces a graph in-context learning strategy that retrieves semantically and structurally similar demonstrations, and experiments show it substantially improves frozen LLMs as standalone detectors in data-efficient settings. This work bridges graph-based propagation modeling and LLM reasoning, potentially enabling accurate fake news detection with far fewer labeled examples and better generalization than supervised GNNs. It could benefit platforms and researchers who need data-efficient, structure-aware veracity reasoning without fine-tuning large models. MAGER addresses modality mismatch and information overload by evolving meta-paths that select informative subgraphs, allowing frozen LLMs to perform structure-aware reasoning in zero-shot and few-shot settings. ## [Six LLMs Tested on Translating Natural Language Goals to PDDL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18731) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18731) empirically evaluates six state-of-the-art LLMs on translating informal natural language testing goals written by video game testers into well-formed PDDL targets for classical planning. All models exceeded 92% correctness, with Gemini 2.5 Flash reaching 96% accuracy and the fewest false positives, while GPT-4.1 was the fastest; the authors also release a carefully designed prompt template. This work shows that LLMs can serve as a practical bridge between human intent and formal planning pipelines, potentially letting non-experts specify planning goals without learning PDDL syntax. It offers concrete guidance for AI planning and LLM practitioners on model selection, prompt design, and the remaining failure modes. The evaluation covers correctness, speed, and error tendencies on real-world, domain-specific benchmarks, and the authors attribute occasional failures to language ambiguity and limitations in domain representation. The prompt template was refined through iterative experimentation to maximize both accuracy and response coherence.

Classical planning assumes a fully known, deterministic environment, and writing goals directly in PDDL is difficult for non-experts, which motivates using LLMs to translate informal natural language into formal PDDL targets.

## [Clue Game Testbed Evaluates Tool-Augmented LLM Deductive Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18736) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18736) implements a text-based, multi-agent version of the board game Clue as an environment to evaluate multi-step deductive reasoning in LLMs, instantiating six agents from GPT-4o-mini and Gemini-2.5-Flash (three per model family) across repeated games. It introduces a tool-augmented approach where a structured possibility matrix converts implicit game state from reasoning logs into an explicit representation of remaining possibilities, encoding extended-turn memory and deductive constraints to offload these tasks from the agent. Logically consistent deductive reasoning over extended interactions remains a key weakness of current LLMs, and this work provides a reusable multi-agent testbed plus baseline results that could guide future reasoning-enhancement research. The tool-augmentation design shows how external structured representations can compensate for LLM limitations in maintaining consistency and updating beliefs under new constraints, with implications for any agentic system requiring long-horizon logical inference. The environment uses turn-based gameplay where agents must infer hidden information from sequences of observations, maintain consistency across turns, and reason over an evolving set of logical constraints; the possibility matrix explicitly encodes extended-turn memory and deductive constraints, offloading these from the agent. Baselines are established with three agents per model family (GPT-4o-mini and Gemini-2.5-Flash) across repeated games, and the tool-augmented approach is compared against these baselines to assess reasoning quality and task success.

A new arXiv paper evaluates a hosted retrieval service against a governed system for version- and scope-aware question answering over roughly 73,000 normative documents, using a stratified sample of 200 benchmark questions with a gold source document for each. The governed system, which resolves version and scope through explicit rules before generation, scored 97.7 overall versus 88.1 for the hosted service, a gap of 9.6 points computed from unrounded means. The result challenges the common "upload the documents and ask" default by showing that explicit version and scope resolution materially improves answer quality on normative corpora. Because the governed configuration has operated as a commercial product since January 2026 with 1,126 registered users and roughly 100,000 calls per workday by mid-April 2026, the findings carry direct production relevance for enterprises building compliance and policy QA systems. The evaluation uses a released sampling rule that reads no system outputs or scores, and the question set, answer text for both systems, scores, and reproduction scripts are public. Named customer organizations include Zhipu AI and Lecheng Health, though the abstract is truncated, limiting full assessment of novelty and results.

## [CERA-MoA Co-Evolves Routing and Agent Policies in Mixture-of-Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18779) ⭐️ 7.0/10

CERA-MoA introduces an iterative reinforcement learning framework in which a dynamic router and independent agent policies co-evolve within a Mixture-of-Agents (MoA) system. It uses a predictive familiarity estimator built on mid-layer hidden states to score agent competence without full rollouts, and a cumulative-threshold adaptive routing mechanism that activates only a minimal tailored subset of agents. Most MoA systems treat query routing and agent fine-tuning as separate processes, so routing strategies cannot adapt as agents improve during post-training. By jointly optimizing both, CERA-MoA enables synergistic data-driven specialization and reportedly outperforms state-of-the-art static-agent routing and fixed-workflow fine-tuning baselines across multiple domains. The familiarity estimator avoids the overhead of full rollouts by reading mid-layer hidden states, and the cumulative-threshold routing dynamically selects a minimal agent subset to balance task performance against efficiency. Training samples are proactively allocated to agents based on their evolving competence, which promotes capability differentiation among agents.

**Background**: Mixture-of-Agents (MoA) is an architecture in which multiple LLM agents are organized into layers and collaborate or compete to solve complex tasks that a single model may struggle with. Routing in such systems decides which agents handle a given query, while fine-tuning adapts each agent's policy; traditionally these two are optimized separately. Co-evolving reinforcement learning refers to frameworks where multiple components, such as policies and routers, are jointly adapted through intertwined learning processes rather than in a static environment.
