## [Edited Facts Are Suppressed, Not Erased in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18985) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18985) introduces a linear trace probe showing that after successful knowledge editing on GPT-2-XL, the original fact remains linearly decodable from hidden states, with probe accuracies of 0.96 for ROME, 0.86 for constrained fine-tuning, and 0.79 for the memory-based editor GRACE, against a chance level of 0.50. Critically, GRACE changes zero base-model weights, yet the original object is still recoverable, proving the residual trace is not merely an incomplete weight update. This finding challenges the assumption behind current knowledge-editing benchmarks, which certify only local behavioral correctness and ignore how much of the original fact remains internally recoverable. It has direct implications for AI safety and interpretability, since suppressed knowledge could potentially resurface under relearning or adversarial prompting, and it suggests evaluation should measure residual knowledge rather than just generation success. The study applied three mechanistically distinct editors to 50 CounterFact edits on GPT-2-XL, with all edits reaching 100% generation-based success, yet the linear probe still decoded the original object well above chance. The authors also report a relearning-savings instrument that did not behave reliably in their setting, which they treat as a negative methodological result rather than evidence, and they release code and data.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Knowledge editing aims to update specific facts in a large language model without retraining, using methods such as ROME (which modifies feed-forward weights via rank-one updates), constrained fine-tuning, or GRACE (which stores edits in an external memory codebook and intercepts queries during the forward pass). Benchmarks like CounterFact typically measure whether the edited model outputs the new fact on near-edit prompts, but they do not measure whether the original fact is still represented inside the network. This paper uses a linear probe on hidden states to directly test whether the original object remains decodable after editing.

## [Task-Weighted Charts Reveal Language Model Computation Needs 70-90% of Residual Stream Width](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18989) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces task-weighted charts, a method that fits low-dimensional coordinate systems against a chosen functional of a language model's representation under that functional's own metric. Across six models from three families (70M to 7B parameters), next-token prediction requires 70-90% of the residual stream's width to stay within 5% of intact perplexity, and variance profiles predict almost none of this functional importance. This work challenges the common assumption that activation variance indicates functional importance, showing that two directions can carry 90% of GPT-2's variance yet almost none of its function. The findings have direct implications for model interpretability and distillation, suggesting that compression methods based on variance or optimal linear compression may discard functionally critical dimensions. The required width is consumed by the rare tail of language, and dimensionality is per-functional: the model's own uncertainty reads from six coordinates while the full predictive distribution needs hundreds, growing with depth. When only a few dimensions can be kept, charts trained under the functional's metric preserve predictions better than variance-based or optimal linear compression.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: The residual stream is the central information highway in transformer language models, where each layer reads from and writes to a shared high-dimensional representation. Perplexity is the standard metric for language model quality, measuring how well a model predicts the next token. Dimensionality reduction techniques like PCA are often used to compress or interpret these representations, typically by keeping directions with the highest variance.

A new arXiv paper (2609.18991) introduces a five-task diagnostic experiment across physics and geometry benchmarks that isolates whether multimodal LLM failures stem from perception, reasoning, or both. The study finds that incorrect diagram interpretation degrades performance even on problems models solve correctly from text alone, and that accuracy generally rises when raw images are replaced with human-authored captions. Most scientific reasoning benchmarks treat perception and reasoning as one measurable process, so this framework offers a more precise way to diagnose where multimodal models actually break down. The finding that perception errors cascade into domain-specific reasoning failures could reshape how researchers design evaluations and training data for scientific AI. Recovery under corrected captions is high for some models, which separates perception-blocked failures from genuine reasoning bottlenecks; the type of reasoning error that follows a perception failure is domain-dependent, resolving into calculation errors in physics and conceptual misapplication in geometry. As a side discussion, InternS1-mini falls below the weakest model in the experiments on every task, with reasoning traces frequently truncating before completion.

## [Flag Game: A Toy Model for Mechanistic Swarm Interpretability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19124) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce the Flag Game, a toy model in which a hidden country flag defines ground truth and bounded agents each observe only a private crop while exchanging beliefs and weighing social evidence. The model reproduces non-monotonic scaling of performance with population size, accuracy gains from social-awareness prompting and team diversity, and a transition from collective belief collapse at small populations to collective belief polarization at larger ones. As AI agents increasingly coordinate in swarms, understanding how false beliefs spread and how collective behavior emerges is a critical safety concern. This work takes a first step toward mechanistic swarm interpretability, offering tools that could inform collective alignment and the design of multi-agent systems. The paper introduces social circuit attribution to predict which agent and which view matter most to collective dynamics, verified through causal interventions on agents, but notes that intervention efficacy decreases as population grows. It therefore develops a statistical mechanical theory for larger populations that matches the empirical phase diagram.

Multi-agent systems, where many AI agents interact and share information, can exhibit emergent collective behaviors that are hard to predict from individual agents alone. The Flag Game is a deliberately simple testbed that isolates how individual observations and social communication combine to produce collective beliefs.

A new arXiv paper (2609.19128) extends the SwiftSage dual-process language agent with two modular cognitive extensions: an Adaptive Memory Module (AMM) for salience-gated episodic storage and trigger-driven retrieval, and a Self-Reflection Module (SRM) for bounded execution-time validation and corrective intervention. Across four controlled ablations on ScienceWorld, the full system achieved the best mean final score of 64.62, a 43.17% success rate, and 19.33 successful-step efficiency, with SRM identified as the strongest standalone contributor. This work provides concrete evidence that execution-time control, rather than memory alone, is the dominant bottleneck for language agents in long-horizon interactive tasks, suggesting that self-reflection mechanisms may be the highest-leverage addition for improving agent robustness. The modular, feature-flagged design also offers a reusable experimental methodology for isolating the contributions of individual cognitive components in agent architectures. Both modules are implemented as feature-flagged extensions over the same execution substrate, enabling clean controlled ablations across baseline, baseline+AMM, baseline+SRM, and full-system configurations. The results indicate that episodic memory becomes most useful only after the runtime loop is stabilized, and the abstract does not report comparisons against non-SwiftSage baselines or discuss computational overhead.

## [Paper Argues Schema Linking Is Unnecessary for Modern LLM Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.07702) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2408.07702v2) empirically shows that the latest generation of large language models can generate accurate SQL even when large numbers of irrelevant tables and columns are present, so its pipeline entirely forgoes schema linking whenever the schema fits within the model's context window. Instead of filtering context, it adopts augmentation, selection, and correction techniques, and the resulting approach ranks first on the BIRD benchmark with 71.83% accuracy. Schema linking has long been treated as a critical, error-prone step in Text-to-SQL pipelines, and imperfect linking often drops columns the query actually needs. If modern LLMs can reliably handle full schemas within their context window, practitioners could simplify their pipelines and avoid a major source of failures, potentially reshaping how production Text-to-SQL systems are built. The approach only skips schema linking when the entire schema fits within the model's context window, so it does not directly address very large or multi-database schemas that exceed that limit. The paper's headline result is 71.83% accuracy on the BIRD benchmark, achieved by combining augmentation, selection, and correction rather than by filtering schema elements.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Text-to-SQL is the task of converting a natural-language question into an executable SQL query over a relational database. A typical pipeline first performs schema linking, which retrieves the relevant tables and columns for the user's query while discarding irrelevant ones, so the language model sees a smaller, focused schema. The context window of an LLM is the maximum amount of tokenized text it can process at once, and recent models have greatly expanded this limit, making it feasible to feed entire schemas into the prompt.

## [DISCERN Certifies Model Updates by Auditing Only Disagreements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17560) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces DISCERN, a sequential two-tier protocol that formalizes update promotion as certified paired risk-difference auditing, exploiting a support identity that the risk difference between two models lives only on inputs where they disagree. A zero-label tier certifies benign updates from unlabeled traffic alone, while an audited tier labels only sampled disagreements through an anytime-valid confidence sequence, with proven finite-sample validity and matching label-complexity bounds of order rho^2/eps^2. Because exploiting free disagreement provably saves a factor of 1/rho over any pairing-blind auditor, the protocol can dramatically reduce labeling costs for validating retrained, fine-tuned, quantized, or silently swapped production models. The guarantee composes across an unbounded sequence of promotions from a single error budget, making it practical for continuous deployment and post-market monitoring. The audited tier remains valid at every stopping time and under any label-routing rule, even an adversarial judge, and each audit emits a machine-checkable evidence record. Across 14,000+ replayed audit streams over 785 update pairs, including LoRA fine-tunes of language models up to 1.4B parameters, miscoverage is 0.0002 against a nominal 5%, power is 0.986 with zero false alarms, and 56% of benign updates certify with zero labels.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Production models are constantly updated through retraining, fine-tuning, quantization, or vendor swaps, and each update risks degrading performance relative to the model it replaces. Certified paired risk-difference auditing aims to statistically verify that a new model is not worse than the old one, but naive approaches require labeling many examples. Anytime-valid confidence sequences allow continuous monitoring with valid error guarantees at any stopping time, while label complexity from active learning quantifies how many labeled examples are needed to reach a target performance.

## [Grokking as Distributed Spectral Recoding, Not a Module Switch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17571) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.17571) introduces Transition Games—behavior-aligned exact activation games with paired non-generalizing controls—to locate where a Transformer shifts from memorization to generalization. It finds distributed utility gain tied to a prospective block-0 attention bias, with selected degree-two modes accounting for 67–92% of its addition contrast, and block-1 MLP mediating more effect than all other tested downstream paths in 12/12 pairs. This challenges the widely cited "MLP memorizes, attention generalizes" hypothesis, showing that prediction actually reverses (-.331 bits/example at the memory anchor; 0/12 in the predicted direction). For mechanistic interpretability and AI safety research, it reframes grokking as spectral recoding of an existing distributed circuit rather than a discrete module switch, which could change how researchers search for generalization mechanisms. The study also reports that routing onset, global rank collapse, and a prime-invariant architecture ridge all fail as explanations, and a disjoint exact path study confirms block-1 MLP's dominant mediating role. These negative results are notable because they rule out several popular candidate mechanisms for grokking.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Grokking is the phenomenon where a neural network suddenly generalizes long after it has already overfit its training data. Mechanistic interpretability aims to reverse-engineer the specific computations and circuits inside neural networks rather than treating them as black boxes. This paper applies that lens to Transformers, using activation-level games and Fourier-style spectral analysis to ask where in the network the memorization-to-generalization transition is functionally expressed.

## [PhD Lecture Notes on Physics-Informed Neural Networks and Neural Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17638) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A set of lecture notes from the PhD course "Physics Informed Neural Network" taught at the University of Bozen/Bolzano during the 2025/2026 academic year has been posted to arXiv (2609.17638). The notes cover PINNs and Neural Operators, including from-scratch PyTorch implementations and advanced libraries such as NVIDIA PhysicsNeMo, along with recent topics like Mixture-of-Models, Fourier Neural Operators, and Physics-Informed Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (PIKANs). These notes provide a consolidated, up-to-date educational resource that bridges theory and practice for researchers and practitioners applying deep learning to scientific computing. By covering both foundational methods and emerging architectures like PIKANs and Mixture-of-Models, they help lower the barrier to entry for real-world engineering, physics, and petroleum reservoir problems. The course emphasizes hands-on implementation, teaching PINNs and Neural Operators both from scratch in PyTorch and via NVIDIA PhysicsNeMo, an open-source framework for physics-ML. It also introduces Fourier Neural Operators, which learn resolution-invariant solution operators for PDEs, and PIKANs, which combine physics-informed training with Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) are neural networks that embed physical laws, typically expressed as partial differential equations (PDEs), into the training process as a regularization mechanism, allowing them to learn from limited data. Neural Operators extend this idea by learning mappings between infinite-dimensional function spaces, so they can approximate the solution operators of PDEs and generalize across different discretizations without retraining. Fourier Neural Operators are a prominent neural operator architecture that uses Fourier transforms to learn resolution-invariant solution operators, often achieving large speedups over traditional numerical solvers. Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) are a newer neural network design that replaces fixed activation functions on edges with learnable spline-based functions, and PIKANs apply this design within a physics-informed framework.

## [SARA Turns Sparse User Rationales into Scalable Recommendation Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17639) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces SARA (Scaling Articulated Rationales), an industrial framework that elicits and curates articulated user rationales (AURs) from 240 million Kuaishou Live users, producing the SARA-HQ dataset, and then aligns a general-purpose MLLM into SARA-7B via large-scale SFT and Quality-Refining DPO. SARA-7B extends rationale generation from 86,564 AUR-covered authors to the full 10-million-author space, and the SARA-Ranker integrates generated positive and negative rationales into production ranking, with online A/B tests showing improved engagement and reduced negative feedback over a 30-day deployment. This work addresses a fundamental gap in recommendation systems: implicit signals like clicks and watch time reveal what users do, not why they like or dislike content, and SARA demonstrates that sparse natural-language rationales can be scaled into first-class textual signals for industrial recommendation. The successful 30-day production deployment at Kuaishou suggests that MLLM-based rationale generation and ranking integration can deliver measurable business value, potentially influencing how large-scale platforms model user preferences beyond behavioral data. SARA uses a data engine to elicit and curate AURs from 240M users, creating the quality-controlled, author-centric SARA-HQ dataset, and employs Quality-Refining DPO to improve rationale specificity, polarity consistency, and grounding. The framework integrates rationale-aware interaction modeling and rejection-memory modeling into production ranking, with daily refresh maintained for over 30 days, though the paper is an arXiv preprint without peer review or community discussion yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Modern recommendation systems primarily rely on implicit behaviors such as clicks, watch time, and negative feedback to infer user preferences, but these signals capture what users do rather than why they like or dislike content. Articulated user rationales (AURs) are users' natural-language explanations of their preferences, offering polarity-aware and reason-level textual signals, but they are naturally sparse, often low-quality, and cover only a small fraction of items, making them difficult to use in industrial systems. Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can process text and other modalities, and recent research has explored aligning them for recommendation tasks, but scaling sparse rationales into effective signals remains a challenge. Kuaishou Live is a large live-streaming platform in China, providing a real-world industrial setting with hundreds of millions of users for testing such a framework.

## [EvoSkill-GUI: Training-Free Skill Evolution for GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17653) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers from ZJU-REAL propose EvoSkill-GUI, a training-free framework in which each GUI agent skill is a structured multi-file package (retrieval metadata, executable plans, backup localization, failure-recovery rules, accessibility utilities, and failure cases) that is revised at deployment time through a reflect-revise-reuse loop. Across MobileWorld, AndroidWorld, and OSWorld, it improves multiple base models without any training, with maximum gains of +16.2%, +6.0%, and +10.5% respectively. Existing agent-skill frameworks treat skills as static artifacts produced before deployment, which breaks down on dynamic GUIs where pop-ups, delayed loads, and relocated widgets invalidate pre-fixed plans. By letting skills evolve from execution feedback at deployment time without retraining, EvoSkill-GUI offers a practical path to more reliable long-horizon GUI automation and reusable skill libraries that transfer to related tasks. The framework separates roles: the executor performs instant in-rollout revisions, an isolated critic diagnoses failed trajectories under strict information isolation, and the executor then edits specific skill files through a restricted tool interface. Gains largely saturate after about three rounds of skill evolution, and the approach outperforms repeated sampling under a comparable budget.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: GUI agents are large-model-driven systems that autonomously operate graphical user interfaces by clicking, typing, and navigating visual elements, much like a human user. Long-horizon tasks require many sequential steps, so small disruptions such as pop-ups or shifted widgets can derail a plan. Skill-based approaches aim to capture reusable procedural knowledge, but most prior work assumes skills are fixed once written rather than updated from real execution experience.

## [Three LLM Reliability Findings Converge on Calibrated Abstention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17686) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.17686) argues that three seemingly unrelated LLM reliability results—Yin et al. (2026) on reasoning RL collapsing tool-reliability representations, Suleymanov et al. (2026) on safety-constrained generation differences between large and small models, and Bastounis et al. (2024) on the recursion-theoretic inevitability of hallucination—all identify calibrated abstention as the missing capability. The authors propose four evaluation reforms: triple-scoring, abstention-rate reporting, capability-stratified evaluation, and mandatory calibration metrics. If calibrated abstention is indeed the common missing capability, then current benchmarks that assign zero reward to declining to answer are actively selecting against the very behavior needed for reliable and safe LLM deployment. This has direct implications for AI safety, reasoning systems, and how the industry measures model quality. The paper distinguishes three different sources of the gap—a capability gap, a policy gap, and a recursion-theoretic gap—and notes that while honesty post-training has narrowed the gap in deployed models, principled closure of the class Bastounis identifies requires a calibrated abstention function whose training signal is absent at the leaderboard level. The authors stress that benchmark reform is necessary but not sufficient to close the gap the theorem identifies.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Calibrated abstention refers to a model's ability to say "I don't know" when it lacks sufficient information or confidence, rather than producing a confident but wrong answer. Prior work such as the survey "Know Your Limits" and the Abstain-R1 paper has explored abstention methods and benchmarks, while the "Hallucination is Inevitable" line of research uses computability and diagonalization arguments to show that any consistent-reasoning system without an implicit "I don't know" function must hallucinate infinitely often on broad problem classes.

## [Draft Trees Boost Speculative Sampling for Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17691) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.17691) connects speculative sampling in diffusion models to relative entropy coding (REC) and introduces "draft trees," which replace the linear chain of lookahead drafts with a richer tree structure. Combined with greedy rejection sampling as the draft-target coupling, the method achieves up to 8.3% acceleration over the reflection maximal coupling baseline while preserving exact target samples. Diffusion models are computationally expensive at inference because each step requires many evaluations of a costly target network, so improving the acceptance rate per target evaluation directly reduces generation cost. This work relaxes a fundamental topological constraint of existing diffusion speculative samplers and could benefit researchers and practitioners working on efficient generative modeling. The key insight is that the lookahead draft need not form a chain graph, and the tree structure enriches the candidates considered per round, lowering the number of target function evaluations. The approach guarantees exact target samples via greedy rejection sampling, an REC algorithm, and experiments span diverse target and draft model pairs.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Speculative sampling, originally popular for accelerating large language model inference, uses a fast draft model to propose candidate states that a slower target model then accepts or rejects while preserving the target distribution. Extending this to diffusion models is challenging because they generate samples through continuous, vector-valued Markov chains rather than discrete tokens. Relative entropy coding (REC) is an information-theoretic framework that uses KL divergence to simulate a target distribution from a proposal, and greedy rejection sampling is an REC algorithm that accepts proposed samples as early as possible.

## [XConf Estimates LLM Confidence from Retrieved Past Experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17708) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.17708) proposes XConf (eXperiential Confidence), a confidence estimator that retrieves similar past graded episodes and reads off their historical success rate instead of relying only on the current inference. Across nine benchmarks and four models, XConf beats or matches ten-sample self-consistency in AUROC on 23 of 24 comparisons with lower ECE and one-tenth the generation cost. Reliable confidence estimation is central to trustworthy deployment of language models, deciding what to ship, escalate, or retry. By shifting the basis of confidence from the current inference to accumulated experience, XConf offers a format-general, low-cost paradigm that could improve selective prediction and agent reliability. XConf requires no logit access or weight updates and costs only one answer generation; its Recall stage retrieves past episodes with similar tasks and stated confidence, while its Reflect stage has the model name its recurring failure mode and restate confidence. Used for selective prediction, abstaining on the 10% least-confident episodes raises delivered success rate by up to 8.7 points on agent tasks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models are often overconfident, with stated confidence exceeding actual accuracy, which makes calibration a key research problem. Existing confidence estimators typically introspect on the current inference, score token probabilities, or resample the same prompt, as in self-consistency. XConf instead borrows ideas from retrieval-augmented generation, storing the model's own graded past episodes—task, reflection, stated confidence, outcome, and lesson—and retrieving them for new tasks.

## [DRAG: Dynamic Retriever and Generator Selection for RAG Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17709) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.17709) systematically analyzes how retriever and generator complexity interact across factoid and multi-hop QA tasks, and introduces DRAG, a query-adaptive framework for jointly selecting retriever-generator configurations. It proposes DRAG_QPP, a training-free routing method using Query Performance Prediction signals and perplexity-based measures, and DRAG_SFT, a supervised approach that fine-tunes an LLM to predict configurations. Most RAG pipelines use fixed retriever and generator settings for every query, wasting compute on simple questions and under-serving complex ones. DRAG shows that jointly adapting both components yields a better effectiveness-efficiency trade-off, which could directly inform cost and latency optimization in real-world RAG deployments. The analysis finds that stronger retrieval generally yields larger gains than increased generation effort, but both show diminishing and non-monotonic returns, meaning higher-complexity configurations are not uniformly better. Across three LLM families and four QA benchmarks, DRAG_QPP matches strong static RAG baselines while substantially reducing inference latency, whereas DRAG_SFT consistently improves effectiveness over static and training-free adaptive baselines.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is a technique that lets large language models fetch relevant documents from an external knowledge base before generating an answer, improving accuracy and grounding. Multi-hop question answering requires combining evidence scattered across multiple documents through sequential reasoning steps, making it harder than single-hop factoid questions. Prior work has studied retrieval adaptivity and generation adaptivity separately, but their joint effect on end-to-end RAG performance has remained underexplored.

## [REVERSAL-BENCH Exposes a Reversibility Cliff in Reset-Free RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17745) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces REVERSAL-BENCH, a benchmark that controls environmental reversibility through a continuous parameter ρ ∈ [0, 1] and provides a ground-truth reset oracle for testing state recoverability across eight manipulation settings in five physics engines. Evaluating actor-critic, safe RL, and specialized reset-free methods shows a sharp 'reversibility cliff': as ρ increases, reset-free agents become permanently absorbed in irrecoverable states, while episodic agents keep learning steadily. This work identifies a fundamental failure mode of autonomous reinforcement learning: without external resets, any transition into an irrecoverable state permanently traps the agent and halts further learning. It gives the community a controlled benchmark and dataset to study this problem, which matters for real-world robot manipulation where events like pushing objects off a table cannot be undone. The benchmark spans eight manipulation settings across five physics engines and includes a large multi-simulator dataset labeled with recoverability. The authors also test a safety shield that intervenes before irreversible failures; recoverability can be predicted accurately, but active recovery mostly succeeds only when the agent can physically steer clear of the trap.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning typically relies on episodic training, where the environment is reset between attempts so the agent can start over. Reset-free RL aims to remove this human intervention so agents can train continuously and autonomously, as would be required for long-running robots. However, real physical environments contain irreversible events, and prior reset-free methods implicitly assume the environment is largely reversible. REVERSAL-BENCH makes reversibility an explicit, tunable axis to test that assumption.

## [CALOS: A Real-Time Lyapunov Safety Layer for Safe Quadrotor Deep RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17758) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced CALOS (Control-Affine Lyapunov On-manifold Safety), a runtime safety layer that enforces quadrotor attitude constraints without modifying the underlying reinforcement learning algorithm. It formulates four tilt-angle inequalities plus a Lyapunov descent condition as a single quadratic program solved exactly via active-set enumeration over the three-dimensional torque space, and in NVIDIA Isaac Lab it cut lateral tracking error by 55-60% versus an unconstrained PPO baseline while achieving zero attitude-constraint violations. Learned quadrotor policies typically offer no guarantee of respecting safety constraints during training or deployment, so a layer that guarantees safety without touching the learning algorithm addresses a critical gap in safe deep RL. Because it runs fast enough for thousands of parallel simulation environments, it fits the massively parallel training paradigm used by modern robotics RL, and it may be of immediate interest to safe RL and robotics researchers. The safety layer computes the minimum-norm correction to the policy's nominal torque output, and the quadratic program is solved exactly by enumerating active sets in the three-dimensional torque space, keeping computational cost low enough for real-time use across thousands of parallel environments. Restricting exploration to safe regions also accelerated training convergence and improved data efficiency without producing suboptimal policies, though the evaluation is limited to trajectory-tracking tasks in NVIDIA Isaac Lab.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Deep reinforcement learning has shown strong capability in quadrotor control, but a learned policy is a black box with no built-in guarantee that it will respect physical safety limits such as maximum tilt angle. A control Lyapunov function is a control-theoretic tool that certifies whether a system can be driven toward a desired state, and it can be combined with constraints in a quadratic program to compute safe control inputs. Active-set methods are a standard way to solve quadratic programs exactly by identifying which inequality constraints are active at the optimum, which is efficient for small problems like the three-dimensional torque space here. CALOS combines these ideas into a runtime layer that filters the policy's torque commands before they reach the quadrotor.

## [Causal Typing Framework Prevents Estimand Drift from AI-Generated Covariates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17772) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.17772) proposes a causal type discipline for sequential experiments that assigns explicit causal roles to AI-generated covariates before analysis. The framework comprises a versioned representation map, a causal role classifier, a claim-status filter, and an estimand lock that fixes a standardized proximal effect prior to incorporating generated features. As AI-generated covariates from notes, conversations, images, and wearables become common in experiments, unspecified roles can silently change the causal question and invalidate conclusions. This framework provides a principled way to preserve valid causal inference, affecting researchers and practitioners in causal inference, experimental design, and AI/ML who rely on generated representations. The paper applies the conditional-covariance characterization of compression bias to substitution of generated representations for design-relevant states, and introduces a standardized decomposition separating compression, conditional-law, and standardization drift. Simulations show refinement helps when it retains design-relevant information, while design erasure, leakage, and same-data marker selection can produce bias or undercoverage; cluster-level orthogonal estimators distinguish empirical and superpopulation targets under repeated sessions and missing outcomes.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Causal inference in sequential experiments aims to estimate the effect of a treatment while accounting for time-varying covariates and sequential decision-making. AI-generated covariates—features derived from unstructured data such as text, images, or sensor streams—can serve many causal roles (e.g., treatment version, pre-action state, mediator, outcome proxy), and treating them interchangeably can alter the estimand, a phenomenon called estimand drift. The paper builds on established identification assumptions and the conditional-covariance characterization of compression bias to formalize when generated covariates can be safely used.

## [Information Set Emulation: Causal Certificates for AI-Derived EHR Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17777) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces information set emulation, a framework that attaches typed evidence—source evidence, clinical and recording times, decision-time availability, representation version, proposed causal roles, and unresolved ambiguity—to AI-extracted EHR features under a locked target trial. It also proposes causal certificates that record auditable evidence for those roles, and shows that the squared Chebyshev radius of the compatible image equals the residual minimax mean squared error when the image is nonempty and compact. Predictive usefulness of AI-derived EHR features does not establish their admissibility for causal inference, and this framework provides a formal, auditable way to decide when reconstructed information can support a point causal claim versus when only compatible reporting is warranted. It could affect researchers and practitioners in clinical AI, causal inference, and EHR analysis who rely on machine-learned features for treatment-effect estimation. The paper gives identification and cross-fitted augmented inverse probability weighted estimation under explicit exchangeability, positivity, and nuisance-consistency conditions, distinguishing empirical from population targets. It also presents an EHR compression-drift identity separating frame presence, treatment assignment, and outcome observation, but all experiments are synthetic, including artificial simulations, a common-law finite-world example, and synthetic Phase 0 notes.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Target trial emulation is a causal inference approach that designs an observational analysis to mimic a hypothetical randomized trial, locking components such as treatment versions, time zero, outcome window, and target population. Electronic health records are routinely mined by AI and large language models to recover clinically meaningful features, but such features may carry recording biases, version drift, and ambiguity that undermine causal claims. Causal certificates are not proofs of truth; they are auditable records that a feature is admissible for a proposed causal role under a locked target trial.

## [Study Compares AI and Human Approaches to Solving Long-Standing Math Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17779) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.17779v1) compares public AI research accounts with human literature on 11 long-standing mathematical problems, using 58 human papers and 31 within-problem comparisons. Six validated text-based measures reveal that AI accounts emphasize problem resolution and cross-disciplinary connections, while human papers focus more on method explanation, assumption specification, and successor-question generation. This study provides empirical evidence that AI and human mathematicians have distinct research profiles, which could influence how AI research is evaluated and integrated into mathematical discovery. It suggests that assessing AI's contribution requires examining the organization of inquiry, not just whether a problem is solved. The analysis found no precise difference in generality between AI and human accounts, and the estimated directions remained unchanged when each mathematical problem was removed in turn. The six measures capture problem resolution, method articulation, uncertainty and boundary specification, successor-question generation, generality, and cross-disciplinary integration.

AI systems such as large language models have recently begun reporting solutions or advances on long-standing mathematical problems, raising questions about whether their research process mirrors that of human mathematicians. This study systematically compares the two by analyzing text-based features of AI accounts and human papers on the same set of problems.

## [SAiFE-gym: Scalable RL Environments for Concentrated Liquidity Market Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17788) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers released SAiFE_gym, a Python module offering a collection of simulation environments for studying trading problems in Constant Product Markets (CPMs) with Concentrated Liquidity (CL). The environments use a vectorized implementation to scale to high-dimensional Reinforcement Learning (RL) workflows, and the authors demonstrate their utility by evaluating RL agents under uncertainty in market parameters. Concentrated liquidity has become a core design in DeFi AMMs like Uniswap V3, but liquidity provision there is an active, sequential decision problem that is hard to study without realistic simulators. A scalable, vectorized RL environment lowers the barrier for researchers and practitioners to develop and benchmark automated market-making strategies, potentially improving capital efficiency and LP returns across DeFi. The module decomposes the microstructure of CPMs with CL into interactive components so researchers can capture various economic settings, and the vectorized approach is specifically optimized for high-dimensional RL workflows. The evaluation focuses on RL agent performance under uncertainty in market parameters, though the abstract does not specify which RL algorithms or liquidity ranges were tested.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Automated Market Makers (AMMs) are smart contracts that let users trade tokens against pooled liquidity instead of an order book; the constant product formula (x*y=k), used by Uniswap V2, is the simplest and most influential pricing rule. Concentrated liquidity, introduced by Uniswap V3, lets liquidity providers allocate capital within specific price ranges rather than across all prices, improving capital efficiency but turning liquidity provision into an active strategy. Reinforcement learning is increasingly applied to market-making problems, where agents learn sequential trading or liquidity policies from simulated market interactions.

## [K²SVD: Principled Koopman Representations with Kalman Inference for Time-Series Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17815) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced K²SVD, a method that explicitly learns the leading singular functions of the Koopman operator by optimizing a Hilbert-Schmidt objective, yielding a well-defined low-rank approximation with a latent space using less than 10% of the dimensions of prior work. The learned Koopman space is paired with a linear Gaussian state-space model and Kalman filtering for inference, mitigating noise accumulation during multi-step prediction and outperforming state-of-the-art methods with significantly faster prediction speeds and lower computational cost. Prior neural approaches to learning latent Koopman spaces often produced representations that were mathematically inconsistent with the operator-theoretic formulation and failed to capture the intrinsic low-rank structure of system dynamics. By providing a principled, interpretable low-rank approximation with efficient inference, K²SVD could improve dynamical systems modeling, time-series forecasting, and control applications where both accuracy and computational efficiency matter. The method optimizes a Hilbert-Schmidt objective to obtain a valid low-rank Koopman operator with an interpretable linear combination, and it uses a linear Gaussian state-space model with Kalman filtering to reduce noise accumulation in multi-step prediction. Empirical results show it outperforms state-of-the-art methods across multiple datasets while being faster and cheaper than previous efficiency-focused models, though the work is currently an arXiv preprint without peer review or community discussion.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: The Koopman operator is an infinite-dimensional linear operator that describes the evolution of observables of a nonlinear dynamical system, allowing nonlinear dynamics to be analyzed with linear methods. In practice, one seeks a finite-dimensional approximation, often called a Koopman space, and prior work used neural networks to learn such latent spaces, sometimes without guaranteeing mathematical validity. The Hilbert-Schmidt objective is a norm-based criterion for operators, while the Kalman filter is a recursive algorithm that estimates the state of a linear dynamic system from noisy measurements by alternating prediction and update steps.

## [Free Inference Dimension: A New Complexity Measure for Zero-Collision Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17816) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper formalizes the 'Free Inference' phenomenon in meta-reinforcement learning, where a value-mixture (VM) agent achieves near-optimal, zero-collision navigation without identifying the true environment, and introduces the Free Inference dimension dFI(S,N) as a combinatorial complexity measure. It proves dFI is strictly smaller than the VC-dimension and relates to the Natarajan dimension up to a path-length factor, with a PAC-style relaxation yielding generalization bounds. This work bridges meta-reinforcement learning and computational learning theory by providing a new complexity measure that explains when a mixture agent can succeed without identifying the environment, potentially guiding the design of more efficient navigation and decision-making agents. It also establishes theoretical connections to VC and Natarajan dimensions, offering a foundation for analyzing non-decomposable losses in sequential decision-making. The Free Inference regime persists up to a sharp density threshold, beyond which performance degrades and posterior-mode selection (PMS) becomes preferable; a hybrid strategy that averages until the first collision then switches to selection is shown to be optimal. The analysis includes a complementary PMS identification dimension and links to Littlestone-type dimensions, supported by grid-world experiments.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Solomonoff induction frames prediction as a mixture over computable hypotheses, typically leading to identification of the true environment. In meta-reinforcement learning, agents learn to adapt to new tasks, and complexity measures like the VC dimension and Natarajan dimension characterize the capacity of hypothesis classes. The Free Inference dimension extends these ideas to sequential navigation with nested constraint families, capturing the environmental complexity a value-mixture agent can handle while preserving trajectory coherence.

A new arXiv paper (2609.17824) introduces a decentralized object-centric control framework in which each humanoid robot is assigned a local attachment region on a shared object and learns gripperless bimanual pinching to pick up and transport objects of varying size, weight, and geometry. The authors report that policies trained only on single-robot pickup transfer nontrivially to cooperative multi-robot settings, while explicit multi-robot training further improves performance, with validation in simulation and sim-to-real transfer on real humanoid hardware. This work suggests a common control abstraction can span single-robot pickup, cooperative transport, and robot-to-robot handover without per-task redesign, which could simplify how heterogeneous humanoid teams are deployed in logistics and manufacturing. It also provides evidence that shared-object coupling creates coordination dynamics worth learning directly, informing future multi-agent robot learning research. The approach uses an attachment-based interface where each humanoid controls only its local region on the object, avoiding centralized coordination, and relies on gripperless bimanual pinching rather than dedicated end-effectors. Validation covers varying team sizes and object geometries in simulation, plus sim-to-real transfer, though the abstract does not quantify success rates or specify hardware platforms.

## [Procedural Pretraining Boosts Molecular Property Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17831) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.17831) introduces a three-stage training pipeline — procedural pretraining on abstract data, molecular pretraining on SMILES, and downstream fine-tuning — and shows that procedural pretraining reduces test error on Lipophilicity by 4.8% even after subsequent molecular pretraining. The gain is roughly 90% of the performance difference between a 250K-molecule baseline and the publicly released MoLFormer checkpoint pretrained on about 100M molecules. This suggests that transferable inductive biases for molecular learning can be acquired from abstract, procedurally generated data before any chemistry is seen, offering a complementary and potentially cheaper route to better performance when labeled molecular data are scarce. It also connects molecular property prediction to the broader trend of procedural pretraining already explored for language models. The benefit is strongest under downstream data scarcity, depends on the structure of the procedural data rather than only surface-level statistics, and does not increase monotonically with more procedural training — transfer typically peaks at an intermediate procedural budget and deteriorates as the model converges on the procedural task. The authors also find that much of the transferable information is localized in attention layers, while feed-forward layers can contribute to over-specialization.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Molecular property prediction models are often pretrained on large corpora of unlabeled molecules, typically represented as SMILES strings, because labeled downstream datasets are small. MoLFormer is a linear-attention transformer pretrained on up to 1.1 billion SMILES from PubChem and ZINC, and serves as a strong large-scale baseline. Procedural pretraining, previously studied for language models, uses abstract structured data such as sequence tasks, cellular automata, and graph reasoning to build algorithmic scaffolding before exposure to domain-specific data.

Researchers developed held-out ensembles based on NuCLR (Nuclear Co-Learned Representations), a multi-task model of nuclear data, to study charge radii and electric-quadrupole transition strengths. Out-of-fold validation shows shared representation improves over single-task learning, achieving a charge-radius RMS deviation of 0.0147 fm and a B(E2) RMS deviation of 0.192 e²b² across hundreds of nuclides. The study uses out-of-fold (OOF) validation and held-out ensembles to estimate expected prediction accuracy across the nuclear chart. ## [Lexara-RF: Reference-Free Metrics for Conversational Visual Analytics Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17842) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced Lexara-RF, a reference-free metric suite that evaluates conversational visual analytics (CVA) agents using only the prompt, data, and model response. It comprises 13 metrics that operationalize visualization design theory and Gricean cooperative principles as computable consistency, intent-alignment, and design validity checks, achieving alignment comparable to reference-based methods on a human-rated CVA test corpus. Reference-based benchmarks for multimodal LLM outputs are costly to author and cannot cover the full space of valid responses, making them impractical in production settings. Lexara-RF offers a scalable alternative that also localizes structurally grounded failures, which could benefit researchers and practitioners building and deploying CVA agents. The 13 metrics are grounded in visualization design theory and Gricean cooperative principles, and the framework reportedly outperforms surface-similarity NLG baselines while localizing failures with high accuracy. As an arXiv preprint, it has not yet undergone peer review and no community discussion is available.

Researchers introduced RoboVAD, a large-scale benchmark for video anomaly detection (VAD) in robotic arm manipulation videos, featuring cross-domain evaluation where certain actions and anomaly types are unseen during training. The benchmark defines cross-domain evaluation by holding out certain actions (tasks executed by the robotic arm) and anomaly types (mistakes made during tasks) from training, forcing models to handle unforeseen tasks and new anomalies. Even the best-performing method, a novel approach specifically adapted for robotic arm manipulation, remains below a micro-averaged frame-level AUC threshold of 70% in the most challenging evaluation setup.

## [PrimeScientist Allocates Research Effort Strategically in Autonomous Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17846) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (arXiv:2609.17846) introduces PrimeScientist, a framework that formulates strategic research effort allocation as a sequential decision problem and uses an executable plan tree to preserve competing plans and their outcomes across attempts. It employs an adaptive MCTS-based allocation policy that balances exploration and exploitation based on experimental feedback and remaining resources, improving average reward by 10.3% with 50.6% fewer research attempts than AutoResearch across 12 AI research tasks under the same budget. This work addresses a key bottleneck in autonomous research agents: they can propose more directions than resources allow, and each attempt consumes substantial resources. By making research effort allocation an explicit optimization target, it could influence future agent design and establish effective resource use as a core capability for autonomous scientific discovery at scale. The framework jointly determines research direction and resource investment across successive attempts, with remaining resources explicitly guiding the research policy. Evaluations span AI research, systems and code optimization, and machine learning engineering, showing improvements in both research quality and sample efficiency; however, as an arXiv preprint without community discussion yet, its impact remains unvalidated.

## [AfriSyCo: Assertive Framing and Verification Effects in African-Language QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17853) ⭐️ 7.0/10

AfriSyCo introduces a two-layer study of answer switching around African-language factual content, combining native-language follow-ups with a precommitted 2×2 cross-language factorial across 1,415 turn-1-correct observations from 100 source questions, seven open-weight checkpoints, and six languages. Assertive endorsement produced 29.3 percentage points more any-turn false-target selection than mention-plus-verification under native prompts, and in the factorial assertive framing increased target selection by 30.4 points (95% CI [28.4, 32.3]) while verification decreased it by 17.4 points. The findings show that prompt realization itself is part of the measurement problem, meaning multilingual evaluations of open-weight models may be systematically distorted by how follow-up questions are worded. This matters for AI safety, multilingual NLP, and evaluation research, since large effect sizes across languages and checkpoints suggest that reported accuracy for African-language factual QA can be inflated or deflated by framing alone. The assertive effect remains 34.8 points among 611 observations that were correct after option reordering, and magnitude varies sharply by wording and checkpoint: prompt-family effects span 20.1–42.5 points, a Twi/Qwen3 paraphrase shifts target selection from 70.8% to 4.2%, and checkpoint effects span 9.2–47.0 points. The study also notes that turn-1-correct denotes observed first-response accuracy, not demonstrated knowledge.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models are known to be sensitive to how prompts are phrased, a phenomenon related to framing and anchoring biases documented in human decision-making. Open-weight models are those whose trained parameters are publicly released, allowing researchers to run controlled evaluations across checkpoints. ## [Study Measures Same-Family Bias in LLM-as-Judge Panels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17857) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.17857) analyzes four open-weight model families (Llama 3.1, Qwen 2.5, Gemma 2, Yi 1.5) in a fully crossed pairwise design with 9,312 judgments, deriving a corrected estimator that holds the candidate family fixed. All four families show a positive same-family lift of 3.4–8.4 percentage points, with a global family-preference score of 0.067 (95% CI [0.053, 0.084], permutation p = 0.0002). LLM-as-judge panels are widely used to evaluate and rank models, so a systematic same-family preference bias means leaderboards and evaluation results may be skewed toward judges' own model families. The paper's corrected estimator and finding that panel composition changes 18.5% of pairwise outcomes have direct implications for how AI evaluation pipelines should be designed and audited. The naive per-family statistic is heavily confounded with candidate quality, correlating with Bradley-Terry ability at r = 0.95, so the authors derive a corrected estimator that fixes the candidate family. The effect survives panel-based quality controls, an independent human-consensus anchor, and a float16 replication; adding judge-side likelihood advantage attenuates the controlled coefficient by 61%, which the authors treat as descriptive rather than causal. Position bias is a separate failure mode, with 55.4% of AB/BA pairs reversing, a rate incompatible with a simple independent content-noise model.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: LLM-as-a-judge is a technique where a large language model scores or compares the outputs of other models, used as a cheaper and more scalable alternative to human annotation. Prior work has documented self-preference bias, where judges favor outputs from their own model or family, which can distort evaluations. The Bradley-Terry model is a standard statistical method for converting pairwise comparison outcomes into latent quality scores, and it is commonly used to build model leaderboards.

## [AI Assistance Leaves Detectable Temporal Fingerprint in Writing and Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17883) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.17883) analyzes keystroke-level data from three public corpora—CoAuthor (1,447 co-writing sessions), RealHumanEval (243 programmer records), and a pre-LLM CS1 corpus (5.1 million keystrokes)—to test whether AI assistance leaves a detectable temporal signature. The authors find that AI contributions arrive in bursts far outside the author's own baseline in both writing and programming (paired d_z = 1.13 and 3.54), and that classifiers using only observable temporal features can separate simulated wholesale delegation from authentic work with F1 ≥ 0.997. This research suggests that process data—not just final artifacts—could offer a more accurate and ethically defensible basis for academic integrity checks, potentially reshaping how educators and institutions detect AI misuse. It also indicates that ordinary collaboration with AI is hard to distinguish from unassisted work, meaning detection systems may flag only wholesale delegation rather than legitimate assistance. The study found that 93% of AI-inserted characters survived to writers' final documents, while only 14% of accepted code suggestions survived intact, revealing a medium-dependent divergence in engagement. Classifiers achieved near-perfect separation of simulated delegation from authentic work (F1 ≥ 0.997, with at most 0.5% of real work misclassified), but ordinary collaboration remained difficult to distinguish from unassisted work, and the authors note validation in authentic coursework is still pending.

The CoAuthor dataset captures interactions between 63 writers and GPT-3 across 1,445 English writing sessions, while RealHumanEval is a web interface for measuring how LLMs assist programmers through autocomplete or chat. ## [Continuous-Time Generation on Learned Data Manifolds via Score Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17901) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.17901) proposes framing time-dependent data generation as continuous-time evolution on a learned data manifold, using pretrained score-based models as geometric priors to learn a vector field that evolves data along score-induced interpolation paths. This enables generation at arbitrary timestamps and temporal super-resolution beyond the training data's discrete temporal grid, with simulation-free training via a regression objective and an extension to a probabilistic distribution over future trajectories. This work could significantly advance generative modeling for time-dependent data such as video, PDE-based spatiotemporal fields, and molecular dynamics, where existing methods are constrained to discrete temporal grids. By enabling temporal super-resolution and stochastic continuous-time dynamics, it may benefit researchers in generative modeling, dynamical systems, and scientific simulation. The method introduces an objective promoting path-relative transverse exponential stability, which admits a practical interpretation as denoising score matching transverse to the interpolation path, improving long-horizon rollout robustness. ## [EDCT-Bench Exposes Faithfulness Gaps in Vision-Language Model Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17953) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new paper introduces Explanation-Driven Counterfactual Testing (EDCT), an intervention-based protocol that extracts visual concepts cited in a VLM's natural language explanation, applies verified minimal edits to those concepts, and checks whether the model's answer and explanation remain consistent with the edited image. Using this protocol, the authors build EDCT-Bench, a benchmark spanning knowledge-intensive VQA (OK-VQA), safety-critical driving (DriveLM), and 3D spatial reasoning (3DSRBench), and find substantial faithfulness gaps across evaluated VLMs. The work addresses a critical gap in multimodal AI evaluation: VLM explanations can sound plausible while being unfaithful to the visual evidence they cite, which is dangerous in safety-critical settings such as driving. By providing a reusable protocol and benchmark, it gives researchers a practical tool for measuring explanation faithfulness and shows that EDCT-generated counterfactuals can serve as high-impact fine-tuning signals. EDCT relies on verified minimal edits to visual concepts, meaning the counterfactual changes are validated rather than arbitrary perturbations, which strengthens the causal link between the edit and the model's response. The benchmark covers three complementary domains, and the accompanying fine-tuning study suggests that training on EDCT counterfactuals improves model behavior, though the abstract does not report specific accuracy or faithfulness numbers.

**Background**: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) are AI systems that jointly interpret and generate information from images and text, extending text-only large language models; examples include GPT-4V, Gemini, Claude 3 Opus, and open-source models such as LLaVA. Many VLMs can produce Natural Language Explanations (NLEs), human-readable justifications for their decisions, but prior work shows such explanations can be post-hoc rationalizations that confabulate rather than faithfully reflect the model's internal reasoning. Counterfactual testing, inspired by counterfactual thinking in psychology, asks what a model would output if some input factor had been different, and is increasingly used to probe model behavior, fairness, and robustness.

## [Role Assignment in System Prompts Induces Sponsorship Bias in LLM Recommenders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17989) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.17989) shows that when an LLM shopping assistant is assigned a platform-oriented role in its system prompt, it significantly reduces the penalty it applies to sponsored listings and weakens the skepticism triggered by sponsorship disclosures. The effect replicates across multiple LLMs and reasoning depths, and a second study finds the gap between traveler-delegated and platform-delegated agents widens when the paid placement is attributed to the platform. This matters because sponsorship disclosure rules designed for human consumers may not protect users when an AI agent, not the consumer, evaluates the disclosure. It raises concrete governance and design questions for deployed shopping assistants, AI alignment, and consumer protection. The paper draws on the fiduciary concept of conflict of duty to argue that an agent's evaluation of a sponsored listing should not depend on which party deployed it. Stricter terminology such as "Sponsored" instead of "Promoted" lowers choice of paid listings but does not close the gap when the platform is named as principal.

## [SSM Recurrence Drives Token Consensus Like Attention, Paper Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17997) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.17997) analyzes selective state space models (SSMs) through a dynamical systems lens, modeling token evolution across layers as an ordinary differential equation. Using input-to-state stability arguments, it proves local exponential stability of consensus equilibria and characterizes their domain of attraction for time-varying weight matrices, showing that the recurrence at the core of SSMs aggregates tokens just as attention does in transformers. This work deepens the theoretical link between SSMs and transformers, suggesting the two architectures are more similar than previously thought and potentially enabling cross-pollination of interpretability and design techniques. It matters for researchers working on efficient alternatives to transformers, such as Mamba, who need to understand the fundamental dynamics governing these models. The analysis extends previous results by handling time-varying weight matrices, a setting not addressed before, and numerical experiments on a pretrained Mamba-2 model identify the output gate as the component regulating the extent of consensus, preventing tokens from fully collapsing to a single direction.

**Background**: Selective state space models (SSMs) like Mamba are recurrent architectures whose state updates depend on the current input, offering linear-time inference as an alternative to transformers' quadratic attention. In transformers, attention is known to drive token representations to cluster toward a single direction, a phenomenon called consensus. Recent work has shown SSM recurrence admits a formulation akin to linear attention, motivating the question of whether it produces similar consensus dynamics.

## [Newer Is Not Fairer: Gender Stereotyping Persists Across Stable Diffusion Generations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18007) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A large-scale arXiv study evaluated gender representation across 20 occupations, 5 prompt templates, and 4 Stable Diffusion generations (SD 1.5, SD 2.1, SDXL, SD 3 Medium), generating 8,000 images classified with DeepFace. It found that 76.4% of images show male subjects, and 57.6% of images for historically female-coded occupations also show male subjects, with bias worsening from SD 1.5 to SDXL before partially recovering in SD 3 Medium. The finding that newer text-to-image models are not automatically fairer challenges the common assumption that model iteration improves social biases, and it matters for anyone using these tools in professional or creative settings where occupational imagery shapes perception. It also highlights a persistent gap between model outputs and real U.S. workforce demographics, with women underrepresented by 20-46 percentage points on average. All nine significant tests survived Benjamini-Hochberg correction across 10 tests, and compared with U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics data, models drastically over-represent men in near gender-balanced occupations such as scientist (48% female in BLS vs. 82-99% male in outputs) and cleaner (46% female in BLS vs. 80-92% male in outputs). A preliminary comparison with GPT-image-1 on five occupations suggested lower bias than open-source models, but the effect was small (Cramer's V = 0.080) and exploratory, and no model achieved gender parity.

## [Physics-Informed Neural Network Speeds Up Solar Chromosphere Spectral Inversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18025) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced MLSI-PINN, a physics-informed neural network framework that accelerates multilayer spectral inversion (MLSI) of H-alpha 6562.8 A and Ca II 8542.1 A spectra by predicting MLSI parameters directly from observed line profiles and passing them through a differentiable forward model. Applied to Fast Imaging Solar Spectrograph (FISS) data from the Goode Solar Telescope, it achieved a mean pixel-wise Pearson correlation of 0.933 and processed a raster in about 5-15 seconds versus 3-5 minutes for conventional MLSI, a 12-60x speedup. This work could make large-scale chromospheric diagnostics practical, since conventional pixel-by-pixel nonlinear least-squares fitting is prohibitively expensive for modern imaging spectroscopy data sets. It also demonstrates a physics-informed machine learning approach that preserves analytic radiative-transfer interpretability, relevant to both solar physics and the broader PINN community. Training uses a two-stage strategy: first optimizing only spectral reconstruction loss, then fine-tuning with spectral consistency plus parameter-space supervision from conventional MLSI on a single reference image, eliminating the need for large precomputed training sets. The method was validated on both quiet-Sun and active-region observations, and reconstructed spectra closely match observed profiles and conventional MLSI fits.

**Background**: The solar chromosphere is diagnosed through strong absorption lines such as H-alpha and Ca II, whose shapes encode plasma dynamics and thermal structure. Multilayer spectral inversion models these lines with a finite number of radiative-transfer layers, but requires solving the radiative transfer equation repeatedly for each pixel. Physics-informed neural networks embed physical laws, often partial differential equations, into training as a regularization constraint, improving generalization with limited data.

## [Cost-Efficient LLMs Fail 38.4% of Algorithmic Coding Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18052) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv study evaluated three cost-efficient LLMs — Gemini Flash 3, GPT-5.4 mini, and Claude Haiku 4.5 — on 992 algorithmic problems implemented as Java Spring Boot service methods, producing 7,593 methods across eight model/tool/prompt configurations. Although structural conformance to the mandated signature and DTO specification was near-perfect, 38.4% of generated methods did not actually compute the value they returned, and only 12.9% of returned answers were correct. The results are a significant cautionary signal for enterprises adopting cost-efficient LLMs for automated code generation: code that compiles and matches the required interface can still be functionally wrong, meaning structural validation alone is insufficient for production trust. This affects engineering teams, AI coding tool vendors, and anyone relying on cheaper models to cut development costs. The study crossed four model and agentic coding tool combinations with two prompt variants, forbade iteration, and explicitly prohibited hardcoded answers; eight problem statements were withheld to test behavior on missing input. Conditioning on an eight-class outcome taxonomy revealed that response reliability and correctness are inversely related, with genuinely computing methods answering least often yet achieving only 19.3% correctness. Limitations include single generation runs per configuration, partial harness coverage, single-pass timing, syntactic classification, and probable corpus contamination.

## [LLMs Show Covert Dialect Bias Across Four English Varieties](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18068) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18068) adapts the matched-guise sociolinguistic paradigm to probe covert dialect bias in ten open-weight LLMs, using 260 meaning-matched sentence quadruples and log-probability scoring over housing-relevant adjectives. Across all ten models, African American Vernacular English (AAVE) and Nigerian Pidgin (NP) are consistently associated with more negative adjectives than Standard American English (SAE), with NP penalized most severely, while Nigerian Standard English (NSE) shifts from favored in formal tenant screening to penalized as social proximity increases. This work shows that alignment techniques which reduce explicit racial bias in generated text can leave covert attitudinal associations in internal probability distributions untouched, meaning LLMs deployed in high-stakes domains like housing screening may still discriminate. It also extends covert-bias research beyond AAVE to postcolonial Black African varieties (NSE and NP), revealing that bias operates along both racial identity and prestige dimensions. The study probes ten open-weight LLMs across three contexts of varying social proximity—tenant screening, neighbor acceptance, and roommate selection—and finds each dialect is penalized through distinct stereotype clusters rather than a generic non-standard category. NSE, which carries institutional prestige, is favored over SAE in formal tenant screening but increasingly penalized as social proximity grows, and the abstract alone lacks discussion of quality metrics.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: The matched-guise technique is a classic sociolinguistic experiment in which the same speaker produces identical content in different dialects or accents, so listeners' differing evaluations can be attributed to the variety itself rather than to the message. Log-probability scoring uses a language model's own probability estimates for word sequences to measure how strongly it associates a dialect with particular adjectives. AAVE is a distinct English variety with its own grammar and vocabulary spoken by many Black Americans, while Nigerian Standard English and Nigerian Pidgin are postcolonial varieties that have been largely absent from prior covert-bias evaluations.

The paper analyzes each approach's architecture, applications, and limitations, and examines how they complement one another. As foundation models increasingly merge with physical embodiments, this survey offers researchers and practitioners a structured map of a fast-moving field, helping them compare approaches and identify open problems. Its emphasis on data-efficient learning, sim-to-real transfer, edge-compatible architectures, and safety frameworks highlights where the embodied AI community needs to focus next. The survey describes how WFMs generate training data for VLAs and DPMs, RFMs enable cross-platform deployment of learned policies, and LBMs provide motion priors for natural behavior. It draws on examples from autonomous vehicles, industrial automation, healthcare robotics, and humanoid systems, and identifies promising research directions including data-efficient learning, sim-to-real transfer, edge-compatible architectures, and safety frameworks.

## [PentestChain: Zero-Cost Automated Pentesting via MCP and Free-Tier LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18120) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PentestChain introduces a ten-phase automated penetration testing framework that combines a curated deterministic exploit map with a cost-aware AI cascade—starting with a local Ollama qwen2.5-7b model, then free-tier OpenRouter and Cerebras models, and finally a rule-based fallback—all exposed through a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server with eleven tools. The paper reports zero measured paid-API cost per engagement and, on legacy targets measured so far, detected 26 services and enriched 34 CVEs. Continuous automated penetration testing has so far been affordable mainly to large organizations that can pay for premium frontier models like GPT-4, leaving smaller organizations—those that arguably need testing most—unable to sustain it. By treating US-dollar cost per engagement as a first-class evaluation metric and achieving zero paid-API cost, PentestChain could make ongoing security testing accessible to resource-constrained teams. The framework grounds a four-position threat model in the 2025 MCP incident record—including the CVE-2025-6514 remote-code-execution flaw in mcp-remote, the postmark-mcp supply-chain backdoor, and the tool-poisoning/rug-pull/line-jumping class—and contributes four mitigations. It also specifies a reproducible, containerised evaluation protocol using AutoPenBench, a Cybench subset, and the PentestGPT 182-sub-task benchmark, with more than 10 trials per configuration, pass-at-k, non-parametric significance tests, and direct same-testbed reproduction of the PentestGPT and PentestAgent baselines.

**Background**: The Model Context Protocol (MCP) is an open standard introduced by Anthropic in November 2024 that standardizes how AI systems like large language models connect to external tools, systems, and data sources; MCP servers are programs that expose specific capabilities through this protocol. Ollama is a tool for running large language models locally, while OpenRouter and Cerebras offer free-tier API access to hosted models. PentestChain combines these elements so that a small local model handles most work, with free cloud models and rule-based logic as backups, keeping the pipeline running without paid API calls.

## [DualSQL jointly optimizes schema linking and SQL generation with multi-agent RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18135) ⭐️ 7.0/10

DualSQL is a new Text-to-SQL system in which two agents — one for schema linking and one for SQL generation — share a single model backbone and the same agentic scaffold, allowing them to be jointly optimized with multi-agent reinforcement learning. The authors also introduce rollout guardrails to stabilize training and a new correctness metric called robust execution match (REX), with DualSQL-4B reaching 68.0% execution accuracy on the BIRD development set and DualSQL-8B reaching 71.1% after training on only 3,755 examples. Most state-of-the-art Text-to-SQL pipelines train separate models for schema linking and SQL generation, which prevents the two interrelated tasks from reinforcing each other. By showing that a single shared backbone with joint multi-agent RL can beat much larger single-model systems, DualSQL suggests a more parameter-efficient path to high-accuracy natural-language database querying. The system designs three database access tools to ground multi-step reasoning in actual database interactions, and the rollout guardrails are specifically intended to prevent model collapse during multi-agent RL training. The REX metric is used both to judge SQL correctness more accurately and to assign reward signals, and the headline results come from a relatively small training set of 3,755 examples.

**Background**: Text-to-SQL is the task of translating a natural-language question into an executable SQL query, and modern systems usually split it into schema linking (identifying the relevant tables and columns) and SQL generation (writing the query). Schema linking errors — wrong columns, wrong tables, or extra columns — are a major source of failure in these pipelines, which is why the component is more important than it first appears. Multi-agent reinforcement learning extends reinforcement learning to settings where several learning agents act in a shared environment, and execution accuracy (comparing query results rather than query text) is the standard way to evaluate Text-to-SQL systems such as those benchmarked on BIRD.
