## [MoRE: Sharing Expert Pools Across Layers in Mixture-of-Experts Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18176) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new paper proposes Mixture of Reused Experts (MoRE), a hybrid architecture that shares expert pools across groups of adjacent layers in Mixture-of-Experts models. Each layer keeps its own router but selects from a larger shared pool, and lightweight learnable depth embeddings let shared experts distinguish between layers; experiments across three model scales (114M–1.15B parameters) show consistently lower perplexity and stronger downstream performance than standard MoEs and state-of-the-art weight-sharing architectures at matched compute and parameter budgets. MoE models decouple capacity from compute cost but their memory footprint grows linearly with the number of experts, which limits deployment; MoRE reduces this footprint while improving language modeling quality, and it requires only minimal modifications to existing MoE implementations, making it easy to adopt. This could benefit researchers and engineers building large language models under tight memory budgets. The approach keeps a separate router per layer while sharing a larger expert pool across adjacent layers, expanding routing combinations without adding parameters; lightweight learnable depth embeddings condition each layer's input before routing so shared experts can tell layers apart. Validation covers three scales from 114M to 1.15B parameters at matched compute and parameter budgets, though the paper does not report results beyond 1.15B parameters.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) is a machine learning technique that uses multiple expert networks to divide a problem space, and in modern language models it lets a model hold many parameters while activating only a few experts per token, keeping computation low. However, because parameters grow linearly with the number of experts, MoE models have large memory footprints. Recurrent Transformers take the opposite approach, reusing the same layer weights across depth for parameter efficiency, but typically lack the capacity for competitive language modeling. MoRE combines both ideas by sharing expert pools across adjacent layers. Perplexity, the metric used for evaluation, measures how well a language model predicts the next word, with lower values indicating better predictions.

## [Detailed Procedural Traces Make LLM Overseers Over-Reject, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18204) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18204) audits five LLM overseers across 19 compliance tasks and 4,551 judgments, showing that more elaborate procedural traces shift the overseers' decision criterion toward rejection and increase false alarms even when disconfirming evidence is always visible. Option labels remove the stated reason for about 60% of false alarms but do not eliminate residual over-rejection, which grows with trace detail. The finding reframes the common worry that detailed traces make LLM judges gullible, showing instead that traces act as governance artifacts that bias overseers toward false alarms. This means organizations deploying LLM-as-a-judge pipelines for compliance auditing should evaluate overseers by their decision criterion and false-alarm behavior, not accuracy alone. Using signal detection theory, the study varies only trace detail and evidence labeling while keeping disconfirming evidence visible, and finds error detection stays near ceiling; human-validated reason coding shows about 60% of false alarms cite an inability to tie evidence to its option. Labels eliminate that stated reason, yet residual rejection of correct work persists in susceptible overseers and rises with trace detail.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Signal detection theory is a framework for analyzing decisions under uncertainty that separates sensitivity (how well an observer can distinguish signal from noise, measured as d′) from response bias (the decision criterion, i.e., willingness to say 'yes'). LLM-as-a-judge pipelines use one large language model to audit another's outputs, often alongside procedural traces of claimed steps, and are increasingly used in AI compliance auditing and governance.

## [APGEM adaptively selects quantum error mitigation for reinforcement learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18219) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced Adaptive Policy-Guided Error Mitigation (APGEM), a controller that online-selects among Zero-Noise Extrapolation (ZNE), Probabilistic Error Cancellation (PEC), Clifford Data Regression (CDR), and Readout Error Mitigation (REM) for quantum reinforcement learning. Evaluated on a Delhi-based Capacitated Vehicle Routing Problem (CVRP) testbed across five noise families and four severity levels, APGEM restored approximation ratios from 0.84-0.87 to 0.92-0.94 under high noise. This work shows that learning-aware, adaptive error mitigation can make noisy intermediate-scale quantum (NISQ) reinforcement learning more practical for real-world combinatorial optimization. It suggests a route to noise-resilient quantum policies that could outperform static mitigation strategies in logistics and routing applications. APGEM uses a fidelity-, entropy-, and cost-aware utility function with an epsilon-greedy rule over temporal-difference Q-scores to select mitigation techniques. The controller shifts from a CDR-dominated regime under short training horizons to a balanced deployment of all four techniques under longer horizons, indicating genuine regime-dependent selection.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Quantum reinforcement learning (QRL) represents policies as variational quantum circuits (VQCs), which can be attractive for combinatorial optimization but are degraded by decoherence on NISQ hardware. Conventional error mitigation techniques such as ZNE, PEC, CDR, and REM are typically applied statically, without considering the learning context. The Capacitated Vehicle Routing Problem (CVRP) is a classic combinatorial optimization task where vehicles with capacity limits must serve customers at minimum cost.

## [Quanta unifies dense, lexical, and graph retrieval in one Python library](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18248) ⭐️ 7.0/10

An arXiv preprint (arXiv:2609.18248v1) introduces Quanta, an open-source Python library that combines 4-bit quantized dense vector search, BM25 full-text retrieval, and knowledge-graph traversal behind a single retrieval API. It fuses results using weighted reciprocal rank fusion and treats the graph purely as a candidate expander rather than a relevance scorer. RAG pipelines today typically stitch together an ANN index, a full-text engine, a graph database, and a document store, each with its own deployment and failure modes. Quanta targets that integration pain point by offering one self-contained library, which could simplify hybrid retrieval stacks for developers building retrieval-augmented generation systems. Quanta makes two explicit design commitments: it combines signals via weighted reciprocal rank fusion instead of normalizing heterogeneous scores onto a shared range (which the authors argue is ill-posed because such normalizations are query-dependent), and it uses graph traversal only to widen the candidate pool, with newly admitted documents re-scored by the dense indexes under an identifier allowlist.

Reciprocal rank fusion (RRF) is a common technique that merges multiple ranked lists by summing 1/(rank + k) scores, avoiding the need to compare raw scores from different systems.

## [M²Tok: Multi-head Multi-codebook Action Tokenizer for VLA Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18259) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces M²Tok, a multi-head multi-codebook action tokenizer that decomposes latent action features into multiple heads and assigns each head an independent codebook for quantization. Evaluated on RoboTwin, Simpler-Env, and three zero-shot real-world tasks, it achieves lower reconstruction error and higher VLA success rates than prior tokenizers, with code released at github.com/cpaaax/M2Tok. The discretization bottleneck has limited the performance ceiling of vision-language-action models, so reducing reconstruction loss in action tokenization could directly improve robotic manipulation success rates. This work offers a practical structural improvement for researchers building autoregressive VLA systems that must map continuous control signals into discrete tokens. The two key mechanisms are head-wise decomposition, which lets specific heads implicitly align with distinct action dimensions, and per-head independent codebooks, whose combinatorial nature expands representational expressivity. Ablation studies confirm both mechanisms contribute to the gains, though the approach adds codebook parameters and quantization complexity compared with single-codebook tokenizers.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language-action (VLA) models are multimodal foundation models that take an image observation and a text instruction and directly output low-level robot actions, a direction pioneered by Google DeepMind's RT-2 in 2023. Because autoregressive language models operate on discrete tokens while raw robot actions are continuous, VLA systems such as RT-1, RT-2, and OpenVLA rely on action tokenizers to convert continuous signals into a fixed vocabulary of discrete tokens. Vector quantization, which maps continuous features to entries in a learned codebook, is the standard technique for this conversion, and multi-codebook vector quantization aggregates codewords from several codebooks to refine reconstruction error hierarchically.

A new arXiv paper benchmarks Graph-RAG against standard Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) on LatamQA, a culturally grounded multiple-choice dataset covering eight thematic categories about Latin America. Using graphs built end-to-end from Wikipedia articles with the KGGen extractor and queried via G-Retriever, the approach reduces the base LLM's error by 72% with a standard knowledge graph and 78% with a benchmark-aware variant, making it competitive with standard RAG. This work directly addresses the long-tail deficit of LLMs, where culturally specific facts about underrepresented regions like Latin America are too rare in pretraining data to be reliably memorized. It shows that structured knowledge graphs can match or complement RAG while offering tighter control over context, better explainability, and easier updatability, which matters for researchers and practitioners building culturally aware QA systems. The main setting builds graphs automatically from Wikipedia with KGGen without manual curation, and the gap to RAG narrows further as the graph is oriented toward task-relevant content. Notably, the trained projection transfers zero-shot to Portuguese without target-language fine-tuning, indicating multilingual reach.

## [AWED Metric and GRPO Post-Training Cut Streaming ASR Latency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18333) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces AWED, a word-level emission-delay metric defined relative to each word's acoustic end, and uses GRPO with a jointly rewarded objective to post-train a delayed-streams-modeling (DSM) recognizer at a single operating point (τ=6 frames). The resulting model beats both its supervised fine-tuning initialization and the Voxtral Realtime backbone, cutting WER by 30.8% relative at an 80 ms structural delay and 5.7% relative at 480 ms, while lowering median AWED from 1.17 s to 1.04 s. This shows that latency-rewarded reinforcement learning post-training can push the accuracy–latency Pareto frontier of streaming ASR forward without any architectural change, which matters for real-time voice agents and full-duplex dialogue systems where both transcription quality and commit speed are critical. It also challenges the common assumption that the structural delay τ of DSM is a good proxy for user-perceived latency. The paper argues that DSM's alignment-based supervision wastes latency because the same forced-aligned transcript is used at every τ, forcing the model to withhold words it could already commit. The joint reward scores transcription accuracy and measured delay together, and the model is trained at only one operating point (τ=6 frames) yet dominates across all evaluated lookahead budgets.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Streaming ASR must be judged jointly on what it transcribes and how quickly it commits each word. Delayed streams modeling (DSM) is the dominant paradigm for streaming large audio-language models, exposing a structural delay τ that bounds the decoder's lookahead. GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) is a reinforcement-learning algorithm that generates a group of candidate outputs per prompt, scores each, and rewards answers for beating the group average rather than an absolute target; it gained prominence after DeepSeek-R1. Voxtral Realtime is a natively streaming ASR model that matches offline transcription quality at sub-second latency.

## [Governor-Mediated Split Control Secures Adaptive Edge Security](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18338) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18338) proposes a split-control architecture in which an untrusted planner—including rule-based controllers, learned policies, and LLM-assisted agents—emits typed security intents that a deterministic governor validates against safety, resource, temporal-stability, and proportionality invariants before admitted actions are compiled into signed eBPF map updates. The authors formalize the trust-boundary problem, define three threat classes, and report an end-to-end prototype on a Raspberry Pi 5 testbed connected to a university 5G Test Network, showing microsecond-cost admission, rejection, and bounding of intents without disrupting protected-flow regularity. This work addresses a real gap between increasingly autonomous planners (especially LLM agents) and kernel-level enforcement, where a semantically wrong action can be faithfully executed by a substrate that cannot judge mission context. By showing that adaptive security does not need to trust the author of an action but only a mediation boundary that decides admissibility, it offers a practical pattern for secure edge systems design. The governor checks each intent against four invariant classes—safety, resource, temporal-stability, and proportionality—and binds admitted actions to signed receipts before compiling them into pre-installed eBPF map updates. Evaluation covers both rule-based and LLM-assisted planners on a Raspberry Pi 5 testbed connected to a university 5G Test Network, with admission decisions at microsecond cost; as a preprint, it has not yet undergone peer review or community validation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: eBPF (extended Berkeley Packet Filter) is a Linux kernel technology that lets sandboxed programs run in response to events and share state through maps, making it a common substrate for high-performance edge security enforcement. Adaptive edge security increasingly uses automated planners—rule-based controllers, learned policies, and LLM-assisted agents—to translate observations into enforcement actions, but once a planner can influence live policy state, syntactic validity alone is insufficient. This paper treats the boundary between planner output and kernel enforcement input as the primary security object, introducing a deterministic governor that mediates typed intents before they reach eBPF maps.

## [Semantic CSI Feedback: 8-Value Embedding Beats Full-Bandwidth Beam Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18368) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18368) proposes a semantic communication approach to CSI feedback in FDD massive MIMO, in which the UE transmits a learned semantic embedding optimized end-to-end for beam selection at the gNB rather than a compressed reconstruction of the channel. The authors report that an embedding of just d=8 real values derived from only 43 NR CSI-RS pilots in the angular-delay domain achieves the highest beam prediction accuracy, outperforming every method that has access to the full 512-subcarrier channel, including the reconstruction-oriented CsiNet baseline. This result challenges the long-standing assumption that CSI feedback should maximize channel reconstruction fidelity, suggesting that task-aware semantic feedback can dramatically cut feedback overhead while improving downstream performance. If it generalizes, it could reshape how 6G and future FDD massive MIMO systems design feedback links, shifting from signal reconstruction toward transmitting only task-relevant intent. The comparison spans two input domains and three observation scenarios, pitting reconstruction-oriented feedback (CsiNet) against task-aware semantic feedback; the key insight is that beam-relevant information is intrinsically low-dimensional, so the semantic encoder learns to discard reconstruction-irrelevant structure. The paper's claim rests on simulation-based beam prediction accuracy rather than deployed over-the-air trials, so practical generalization remains to be validated.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: In FDD massive MIMO, the downlink and uplink channels are not reciprocal, so the user equipment must estimate the downlink channel from CSI-RS (channel state information reference signal) pilots and feed that CSI back to the base station (gNB) so it can choose beams and precoders. Because the raw channel across hundreds of subcarriers and many antennas is far too large to feed back directly, classical schemes such as CsiNet use deep learning to compress and reconstruct the channel with minimal distortion. Semantic communication instead asks the transmitter to send only the information needed for the receiver's task, which is the principle this paper applies to beam selection.

Researchers introduced MuViS-C, described as the first multi-domain benchmark for evaluating the robustness of learning-based virtual sensing methods under sensor failures. ## [ActionPiece proposes physical rank consistency for action tokenizers in VLA models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18487) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.18487) introduces physical rank consistency (PRC), a metric that measures how well action tokenizers preserve local physical distance rankings after reconstruction, going beyond pointwise MSE. The authors also present ActionPiece, a tokenizer trained with joint supervision of representation learning and quantization that achieves 94.8% on LIBERO and 68.8% on unseen LIBERO-Plus under the same Qwen3-VL-4B policy training setup. Pointwise reconstruction metrics like MSE can hide the fact that compressed action tokens distort or even reverse the relative adjustments needed for different contexts, which directly harms policy behavior. PRC offers a vocabulary- and decoder-agnostic way to compare tokenizers, potentially improving how the robotics and multimodal learning community evaluates and designs action tokenization for autoregressive VLA models. ActionPiece preserves physical action relationships by supervising near-far ordering in both encoder and quantized feature distances, and by applying the same ordering to codeword assignment distributions; the resulting discrete tokens are executed through a frozen decoder. Beyond LIBERO and LIBERO-Plus, it reaches 71.9% on SimplerEnv and 51.5% across VLA-Arena L0-L2, and ablations show the two objectives jointly improve both PRC and policy success.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Autoregressive vision-language-action (VLA) models process visual observations and language instructions to generate discrete action tokens, which a decoder converts into executable robot commands. The action tokenizer is the bridge between continuous demonstrations and this discrete prediction process, so its fidelity is usually judged by pointwise reconstruction error such as MSE. However, small average errors do not guarantee that the relative ordering of action adjustments across different contexts is preserved, which is what PRC is designed to capture.

## [MiST Mid-Trains 8B and 32B LLMs for Cybersecurity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18496) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces MiST (Mid-trained Security Transformer), a suite of 8B and 32B models built on Qwen baselines that are adapted to cybersecurity through a mid-training stage using a compact, expert-vetted seed corpus turned into high-quality synthetic training data. The final checkpoints improve mean cybersecurity accuracy by +13.1 and +8.6 absolute percentage points (relative gains of +27.0% and +15.8%) over the corresponding Qwen baselines for 8B and 32B respectively. Cybersecurity combines high-stakes analysis with complex technical language, making it a challenging domain for general-purpose LLMs, and this work offers a practical, data-efficient recipe for domain adaptation that avoids large-scale continual pre-training over raw domain text. The results are relevant to both the AI and security communities, since MiST also provides a stronger initialization for downstream task-specific fine-tuning and reinforcement learning. Ablation results show the cybersecurity gains arise in the mid-training and supervised fine-tuning stages through a combination of synthetic data generation flows, rather than from continual pre-training on raw domain text. The approach relies on a compact, expert-vetted seed corpus rather than large volumes of scraped domain data, which is the key efficiency claim of the paper.

**Background**: Mid-training is an intermediate adaptation stage between general pre-training and task-specific post-training (such as supervised fine-tuning), often used to inject domain knowledge or refine data quality before final alignment. Synthetic data generation, where a strong LLM produces training examples from real seed material, has become a common technique for domains where real labeled data is scarce, expensive, or sensitive — as is often the case in cybersecurity. Qwen is a family of predominantly open-weight large language models developed by Alibaba Cloud, widely used as a starting point for further fine-tuning in the open-source community.

## [VoiceTrace-Bench: Hybrid Speech Retrieval by Content and Speaker](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18521) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced VoiceTrace-Bench, a benchmark for hybrid speech retrieval where each query pairs text describing what to retrieve with a reference speech utterance specifying who to retrieve. They also propose VoiceTrace, a two-stage framework comprising VoiceTrace-Emb for unified embeddings and VoiceTrace-Reranker for fine-grained query-candidate relevance estimation. Existing speech retrieval benchmarks focus mainly on what is said, ignoring who said it, so this work fills a gap for real-world scenarios like finding a specific person's remarks in meetings, lectures, or podcasts. It could influence future speech search systems and multimodal audio-language research by establishing a new evaluation task. VoiceTrace achieves state-of-the-art performance on established semantic speech retrieval benchmarks while substantially outperforming cascade-based approaches on VoiceTrace-Bench, showing it handles both conventional semantic retrieval and the new hybrid setting. The framework relies on audio-language models' joint audio-text modeling capabilities and uses a two-stage retrieve-then-rerank design.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Speech retrieval is the task of finding relevant spoken content, and it has become more important as audio from meetings, lectures, podcasts, and videos grows. Speaker recognition is the process of identifying who is speaking from voice characteristics, while audio-language models (ALMs) learn joint representations of audio and text. VoiceTrace-Bench combines these ideas by requiring a system to match both semantic content and speaker identity from heterogeneous query inputs.

## [Interpretable Deep Learning Surrogates for Wildfire Spread Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18555) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper trains four deep learning architectures—a patch-based U-Net, a transfer-learned ResNet-50, a physics-informed network, and a Swin-Unet transformer—on 10,584 wildfire spread simulations at 2m resolution for the Rectoret region in Catalonia, Spain. It finds that surface fuel load is the only terrain or vegetation variable with meaningful predictive strength (r = 0.27), and including it reduces prediction error by 21%. Physics-based wildfire simulators are interpretable but costly when scaled to large ensembles, so demonstrating that deep learning surrogates can reproduce them at a fraction of the cost could accelerate probabilistic fire risk assessment. The interpretability analysis also shows that different architectures learn fundamentally different features, which matters for trusting and deploying such models in environmental science. Convolutional models rely primarily on distance from the current fire front, while Swin-Unet assigns more weight to fuel and terrain—a pattern also observed in an unrelated wildfire dataset. When applied without retraining to a second region, Pedriza, all three convolutional models still predict fire spread but lose accuracy by a small yet systematic margin.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Wildfire spread is traditionally predicted with physics-based simulators that are physically interpretable but whose computational cost grows with each additional ensemble member. Deep learning surrogate models are data-driven approximations that emulate such high-fidelity simulators using neural networks, accelerating simulation by orders of magnitude. U-Net is a convolutional neural network originally developed for image segmentation, while Swin-Unet adapts the Swin Transformer's hierarchical window attention into a U-Net-like pure transformer.

## [On-the-Fly Homography Calibration for Multi-Camera Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18582) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18582) by David Voihanski and one other author presents a multi-camera homography calibration system that refines ground-plane geometry on the fly using live detection streams instead of offline 3D site calibration. The core contribution is a centroid-based projection optimization (PO) that runs asynchronously on lightweight, already-transmitted metadata, adding zero computational latency to the real-time tracker. Large-scale surveillance deployments often cannot perform offline calibration because privacy regulations forbid recording video, bandwidth limits prevent synchronizing hundreds of high-resolution streams, and covering huge sites with calibration targets is impractical. This approach removes those barriers, enabling privacy-safe, zero-overhead tracking that automatically adapts to camera movement or environmental change, which could make multi-camera tracking viable in far more real-world settings. The system starts from coarse manual homographies and continuously aligns ground-plane geometry via centroid-based projection optimization, feeding an optimized geometry into a multi-camera bird's-eye-view (BEV) tracker that fuses detections and unifies trajectories across zones. Because PO operates asynchronously on anonymous metadata rather than raw video, it adds no latency and remains resilient in dynamic environments where static, recorded-video calibration is impossible.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-camera tracking typically requires calibrating each camera's position and orientation so detections from different views can be mapped onto a shared ground plane, a process known as homography estimation. Traditional approaches rely on rigorous 3D site calibration, which is accurate but operationally heavy. Homography-based methods assume a planar ground and project detections onto a bird's-eye view, where trajectories from multiple cameras can be fused; this paper targets the weak-calibration and self-calibration regime where precise offline calibration is unavailable.

## [Bias-only test-time RL matches full-parameter TTRL with 76,000x fewer parameters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18587) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18587) introduces label-free bias-only test-time reinforcement learning (TTRL), which uses majority-vote pseudo-labels as rewards and optimizes only about 100K bias parameters while keeping the pretrained backbone frozen. On MATH-500 it reaches 76.67% accuracy, slightly exceeding a labeled bias-steering reproduction while using 76,000x fewer parameters than full-parameter TTRL, and the same procedure also improves vision-language and audio reasoning tasks such as MathVista, AI2D, LogicVista, and MMAU. This result suggests that substantial test-time adaptation can emerge from an extremely small, label-free optimization subspace, which could make TTRL far cheaper in memory and compute and broaden its use in resource-constrained settings. It also connects test-time reinforcement learning with parameter-efficient fine-tuning research, potentially influencing how future reasoning-adaptation methods are designed. The method freezes the backbone and updates only additive bias vectors, using majority-vote pseudo-labels as the reward signal; the learned steering vectors also transfer to 4,500 held-out MATH problems. The authors further analyze why this works, showing that majority-vote reliability improves with rollout consensus and that bias subspaces with greater accessible gradient energy exhibit stronger downstream trainability.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Test-time reinforcement learning (TTRL) lets models improve reasoning at inference time without labeled data, typically by generating multiple rollouts and using majority voting to estimate pseudo-labels for reward computation. Prior TTRL work usually updates a large fraction of model parameters, which is expensive. Bias-only fine-tuning, also known as BitFit or BEFT, is a parameter-efficient technique that updates only the additive bias terms in a network while freezing all weight matrices, and this paper combines the two ideas.

## [Robust MCTS Algorithm Bridges Sim-to-Real Gap in Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18599) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18599) introduces a robust variant of Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) that explicitly addresses transition dynamics and reward distribution ambiguities. The algorithm incorporates a robust power mean backup operator and carefully designed exploration bonuses, achieving a finite-sample convergence rate of O(n^{-1/2}) for value estimation at the root node, comparable to standard MCTS. MCTS has achieved great success in games like Chess, Go, and Shogi, but its reliance on perfect simulators limits real-world deployment where modeling mismatches are common. This work provides theoretical convergence guarantees and empirical robustness, potentially enabling more reliable planning in robotics, autonomous systems, and other safety-critical domains where sim-to-real transfer is essential. The algorithm uses a robust power mean backup operator to handle ambiguity and adds exploration bonuses to ensure finite-sample convergence at every node in the search tree. Empirical evidence shows robust performance even under significant ambiguity in reward distribution and transition dynamics, though the paper likely assumes bounded ambiguity sets and may not cover all types of model misspecification.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) is a heuristic search algorithm for decision processes that builds a search tree using random simulations, and it gained fame by combining with neural networks in AlphaGo. Sim-to-real transfer in reinforcement learning refers to the challenge of transferring policies trained in simulation to the real world, where dynamics often differ. Robust reinforcement learning aims to learn policies that perform well under model uncertainty, and this paper merges these ideas by making MCTS robust to ambiguities in the simulator.

## [PACT Benchmark Tests Enterprise AI Compliance Under Pressure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18605) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced PACT (Pressure-Applied Compliance Testing), a benchmark that evaluates whether enterprise LLM assistants follow compliance rules under pressure across twelve regulated domains and forty-eight multi-turn scenarios. Testing 22 common LLM models, they found that even the strongest assistants mis-apply rules on 6-10% of items, and ordinary user pressure raises violation rates by 65% on average. As enterprises deploy LLM agents in sensitive areas like hiring, healthcare, and finance, compliance with system-context rules becomes a first-order legal concern, and PACT provides the first systematic framework for measuring which models tend to violate rules under realistic pressure. The findings could influence model selection, guardrail design, and regulatory expectations for enterprise AI adoption. PACT pairs each standing rule against a rule-violating shortcut and applies a battery of pressures across different wordings and system-prompt modes, profiling models on six complementary metrics aggregated into a reliability-weighted PACTScore. Samples are constructed under strict LLM-as-judge auditing to be unambiguous, ungameable, and realistic enough to avoid eliciting evaluation-aware behavior.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Enterprise-grade LLM agents are increasingly deployed to assist employees with daily tasks, and they are typically given standing compliance rules in their system context. Prior evaluation frameworks largely measured whether models know rules, not whether they keep following them when breaking a rule becomes convenient due to deadlines, manager requests, or peer behavior. PACT is an arXiv preprint from TRACE AI Labs that shifts the focus to rule-following behavior under realistic workplace pressure.

## [GenStream Replaces Video Frames with Semantic Metadata and Generative Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18634) ⭐️ 7.0/10

GenStream, a new arXiv preprint, proposes a semantic streaming framework that transmits skeletal keypoints, camera viewpoint parameters, and a static 3D background model instead of dense video frames, letting a client-side generative model reconstruct photorealistic human figures and composite them into the scene. The authors report over 99.9% bandwidth reduction versus HEVC for the continuous data stream and partially validate the approach on Olympic figure skating footage. If validated, this paradigm could dramatically cut bandwidth for human-centric content such as sports, performance, and interactive media, where backgrounds are static and motion is limited to a few salient actors. It also points toward a post-codec streaming era built on semantic metadata and client-side generative rendering, with implications for volumetric avatars and personalized viewing. The framework shifts heavy computation to the client, which must run a generative model to synthesize humans, and the authors acknowledge challenges in generalization beyond the tested figure skating footage. The 99.9% bandwidth figure applies to the continuous data stream and is a partial validation rather than a full end-to-end deployment.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Conventional video codecs such as HEVC re-encode entire frames, treating foreground and background pixels uniformly and ignoring the semantic structure of a scene, which wastes bandwidth when backgrounds are static. Pose estimation extracts skeletal keypoints from people in video, and recent generative models can reconstruct 3D human bodies from limited input, making it feasible to send compact semantic metadata and regenerate pixels on the receiving end.

## [Beyond EER: Multi-Dimensional Evaluation of Speaker De-Identification Privacy Leakage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18673) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper proposes a holistic evaluation framework for speaker de-identification (SDID) that goes beyond the single Equal Error Rate (EER) metric, introducing five complementary metrics: EER, soft biometric leakage score, cumulative match characteristic re-identification analysis, canonical correlation analysis with Procrustes embedding alignment, and intelligibility via word error rate and semantic similarity. The authors evaluate five SDID systems from the IARPA ARTS program and demonstrate that these metrics capture independent dimensions of information leakage. Current SDID evaluations typically reduce privacy to a single biometric verification dimension, which can misrepresent a system's true privacy properties. This framework reveals that relying on EER alone may overlook critical leakage channels such as soft biometric inference and embedding-level re-identification, which matters for privacy-preserving speech technology and biometric data protection. The framework's five metrics include EER, soft biometric leakage score (SBLS), cumulative match characteristic (CMC) re-identification analysis, canonical correlation analysis (CCA) and Procrustes embedding alignment, and intelligibility via word error rate (WER) and semantic similarity. The evaluation covers five SDID systems from the IARPA ARTS program, including four performer systems and one baseline built by a Test & Evaluation partner.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Speaker de-identification (SDID) aims to conceal a speaker's identity in speech while preserving its utility, protecting against threats like speaker identification tools and forensic speech analysis. The IARPA ARTS program was launched to develop novel systems that modify spontaneous speech in real-time to protect individual privacy. Equal Error Rate (EER) is the point where the false non-match rate and false match rate are identical, and it has been the standard metric for evaluating speaker verification and de-identification systems. Soft biometrics are non-unique traits such as age range, dialect, sex, or speaking style that can be inferred from speech and may contribute to re-identification even after anonymization.

## [GS-MoE Architecture Boosts Rare Pathology Detection in Medical Imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18688) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Generalist-Specialist-MoE (GS-MoE), a two-branch Mixture-of-Experts architecture that couples a cross-modal generalist model with modality-specific specialists via domain-constrained feature fusion. On the RadImageNet dataset (1.35M images, 165 pathologies, three modalities), GS-MoE recovers detection of six low-prevalence pathologies where all baselines scored F1 = 0, with per-class gains up to +0.60 F1, while using ~53% fewer active parameters at inference than the strongest dense model. This work addresses a known limitation of pure MoE architectures in multimodal medical imaging, where expert routing can sacrifice cross-modal signals critical for rare disease detection. The approach could improve clinical decision support for low-prevalence conditions that are often missed, and the parameter efficiency makes it more practical for deployment. GS-MoE achieves an aggregate MCC of 0.770, slightly exceeding dense and specialist-only MoE baselines, while using ~53% fewer active parameters at inference. The domain-constrained feature fusion mechanism is key to balancing modality-specific specialization with cross-modal shared representations.

**Background**: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) is a machine learning technique where multiple expert networks divide a problem space into homogeneous regions, improving in-domain learning but potentially sacrificing cross-modal signals. RadImageNet is a large-scale multimodal radiology dataset with ~1.35 million annotated images from CT, MRI, ultrasound, and chest X-ray, used for pretraining and benchmarking. Rare pathologies are low-prevalence conditions that are often underrepresented in training data, making them hard to detect.

## [Echo Uses Trusted Back-Translation for Matching Decompilation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18706) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers present Echo, a matching decompilation system that uses compilation as trusted feedback to iteratively generate and refine source code candidates until their recompiled assembly matches the target binary. On function-level benchmarks it produces 2.43x more exact matches than the strongest baseline, and on the Mirai malware binary it matches 2.75x and 7.4x as many functions as GPT-5.6 and Codex respectively. Matching decompilation provides much stronger evidence of correctness than ordinary neural decompilation, which often produces plausible but unverifiable code, so Echo's gains could improve reverse engineering, malware analysis, and software security workflows. It also shows how compilation itself can serve as a reliable feedback signal for guiding large language models and program synthesis systems. Echo first uses a domain-specific model to generate candidate programs together with compilation configurations, then recompiles them, measures assembly-level similarity, and synthesizes promising code-configuration pairs. Remaining mismatches are progressively repaired through rule-based rewriting, neural refinement, and reasoning-based refinement, which is necessary because exact matching is hard for optimized binaries compiled with unknown settings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Decompilation is the reverse of compilation: recovering source code from a binary. Matching decompilation goes further by searching for source code that, when recompiled, produces byte-for-byte identical machine code to the target, which is why it is used in retro game decompilation projects and gives strong evidence of correctness. Neural decompilers can generate readable and recompilable code, but their output is hard to trust, and exact matching remains difficult for optimized binaries whose original compiler and flags are unknown.

## [PPO Critics Suffer Value Flattening; SP3O Fixes It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18708) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18708) identifies a systematic failure mode in PPO critics called Value Flattening, where critic predictions stay flat even though Monte Carlo state values change sharply across intermediate states. The authors propose SParse Proximal Policy Optimization (SP^3O), which applies value loss to only a few well-separated states per response, and show on Qwen3-Base that supervising just three states per response mitigates the problem and improves the learned policy across model sizes and evaluation suites. PPO is the workhorse algorithm behind RLHF for large language models, so a previously overlooked failure mode in its critic directly affects alignment and reasoning training pipelines. SP^3O offers a simple, low-cost fix that could improve value estimation and policy quality for practitioners without changing the overall PPO framework. The paper links Value Flattening to an implicit variance penalty in the critic loss and to redundant updates caused by temporally correlated states with similar gradients, and it reproduces the phenomenon in a controlled FrozenLake environment where it worsens as the state space grows. SP^3O mitigates both effects by supervising only a few well-separated states per response, though the abstract does not specify the exact selection criterion or computational overhead.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO), introduced by OpenAI in 2017, is a policy gradient reinforcement learning algorithm that updates the policy in small steps using a clipped objective to keep training stable. In RLHF for large language models, PPO typically pairs the policy with a critic that estimates state values to reduce the variance of policy updates. Monte Carlo state values are estimates obtained by averaging returns from multiple sampled continuations starting at a given state.

## [Agent Serving Should Read Tool Progress, Not Guess Durations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18849) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18849) argues that no estimate fixed before a tool call starts can predict its duration or even correctly rank calls, and proposes that running tool calls explicitly report their progress. A census of four public agent corpora shows a readable progress signal exists in most tool time, and a harness that recovers it without changing what the agent sees cuts p90 time to first token after a tool call by 20.7% (HBM only) and 20.8% (HBM + DRAM) versus LRU in a production engine. Agentic requests spend substantial wall-clock time waiting for tools while their KV cache occupies GPU memory, so better cache-retention decisions directly affect serving throughput and latency. This work offers an actionable alternative to duration prediction, potentially influencing how future LLM serving systems handle agentic workloads. The reported progress comes in two strengths—a fraction of work remaining, or an accurate signal that the end is near—and is treated as untrusted input from the tool. At KV cache decision points, the reported progress is several times to an order of magnitude more accurate than the best published predictors and remains accurate when the environment changes, with no measurable cost to the agent's benchmark score.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: LLM serving systems keep a KV cache—the stored key/value tensors from prior tokens—so they can avoid recomputing context, but this cache consumes large amounts of GPU memory. When an agentic request pauses to wait for a tool, the serving system must decide whether to keep that cache in GPU memory, evict it, or reload it later, and today it bases that decision on guesses such as the tool's name, its history, or a duration declared before the call. The paper shows these pre-call estimates are fundamentally unreliable and that the running tool itself already knows how much work remains.

## [GrainSpeech: Ultra-Compact Speech Synthesis for Microcontrollers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18856) ⭐️ 7.0/10

GrainSpeech introduces a fixed-receptive-field convolutional encoder and a Mel-specific gradient-variance supervision scheme, achieving high-quality speech synthesis with only 264.8K parameters and 17.9x real-time Mel generation on a microcontroller. A receptive-field-scaling study found that expanding self-attention beyond 15 phonemes yields no consistent gains, guiding the compact encoder design that reduces pitch, energy, and duration prediction errors by 36.0%, 17.3%, and 3.4% respectively. This work shows that compact acoustic models can match the quality of much larger models using less than 1.5% of their parameters, making on-device speech synthesis feasible for resource-constrained hardware like microcontrollers. It has direct implications for edge AI, embedded voice assistants, and low-power audio applications where memory and compute are severely limited. The paper reports that directly transferring image-domain gradient-variance supervision restores fine-scale variation but degrades predicted quality, motivating a Mel-specific formulation with axis-specific gradients, overlapping local statistics, and log-domain variance matching. GrainSpeech achieves UTMOS scores comparable to substantially larger models, and source code and demos are available at https://github.com/lab-emi/GrainSpeech.

**Background**: Speech synthesis systems typically use an acoustic model to convert text into intermediate features such as Mel-spectrograms, which are then turned into audio by a vocoder. ## [AURA Arbitration Layer Stabilizes Autonomous AI Agents in O-RAN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18857) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18857) demonstrates on a live O-RAN testbed that two independently correct AI agents — one protecting a latency SLA and one maximizing utilization for energy efficiency — jointly cause recurring unsafe oscillations in shared resource partitions. The authors propose AURA, a lightweight arbitration layer that admits agent actions only when they satisfy feasibility invariants, per-variable dwell times, and a deadband, and they prove the arbitrated system converges to a feasible operating point. As O-RAN control planes become agentic, with rApps from different vendors independently closing control loops over shared radio resources, this work exposes a fundamental safety gap in multi-agent RAN control and offers a practical, provably stable coordination mechanism. It could directly influence how operators and vendors deploy autonomous rApps in future O-RAN networks. Implemented on an OpenAirInterface (OAI) testbed with measured one-way latency and throughput, AURA reduces recurring shared-state excursions by more than an order of magnitude (from 8.4 to 0.4 PRB amplitude) and virtually eliminates cross-slice throughput starvation (from 40–55% to 0.3%), while leaving the protected slice's own latency compliance unchanged — a trade-off the convergence guarantee makes explicit.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: O-RAN (Open Radio Access Network) is an industry initiative that disaggregates traditional RAN functionality into modular, independently deployable microservices, including xApps in the near-real-time RIC and rApps in the non-real-time RIC. These applications close control loops at different time scales to dynamically optimize network performance. As vendors increasingly deploy autonomous AI agents as rApps, each optimizing its own objective, conflicts over shared radio resources can emerge at runtime — a problem existing conflict-mitigation mechanisms cannot handle because they assume a statically known application population.

## [Sparse Autoencoders Reveal Readout Gap in VLMs for Harmful Meme Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18860) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18860) uses sparse autoencoders, role-conditioned probes, and causal interventions to show that Gemma-3 and Qwen3.5 often internally represent evidence for harmful memes but fail to route it to their outputs. Sparse readouts substantially outperform native predictions across six harmful content benchmarks, with Qwen averaging 0.740 versus 0.432 native macro-F1 and Gemma improving from 0.532 to 0.714. The findings reframe harmful meme misclassification as a routing bottleneck rather than a pure representation failure, which could guide more effective safety interventions such as calibration-only routing and probe-distilled LoRA. This matters for AI safety and interpretability researchers working on multimodal content moderation, especially since the signal extends beyond English to Spanish and Hindi-English code-mixed data. Calibration-only routing recovers 93.3% of the mean gap, and probe-distilled LoRA improves native predictions, though shared multi-task adaptation causes negative transfer. A case study of Gemma-3-12B on Facebook Hateful Memes finds a distributed rank-32 image-prompt interaction reaching 0.756 versus 0.685 native macro-F1, and robustness controls show the signal is not explained solely by OCR and depends on paired visual evidence.

## [ASLEval Framework Reveals 46.9% Privacy Exposure Gap in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18864) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced ASLEval, an authorization-aware framework that measures 'privacy exposure displacement' in tool-using LLM agent sessions by pre-registering hidden target sets and measuring all declared visible exits. Across multiple enterprise-style environments, they found that an expected-outlet-only view misses 46.9% of exposure recovered by the visible-exit union, and attacker self-reports combine omissions with high false discovery. This work shows that current local evaluation proxies for agent privacy are fundamentally incomplete, potentially giving developers and auditors a false sense of security. It motivates new benchmarks that declare the complete visible boundary, ground claims in pre-specified targets and authorization, and report privacy alongside task utility. The study found that schema-aligned internal evidence usually precedes visible exposure at the request/probe level, and that reducing model-visible returns can change this path but may eliminate normal-task success. Independent human review supported the adjudication pipeline while identifying harder console and candidate cases.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

These local proxies lack common ground truth across outlets, reports, and tool paths, creating a mismatch the authors call 'privacy exposure displacement.' ASLEval addresses this by pre-registering hidden targets, measuring all declared visible exits, and reserving internal traces for diagnosis.

## [NeuroECG Adapts ECGFounder to Predict Neurological Outcomes After Cardiac Arrest](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18891) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose NeuroECG, a framework that adapts the pretrained ECGFounder ECG foundation model via task-specific gradual unfreezing on single-channel bedside ECG to predict neurological outcomes after cardiac arrest without EEG. On 412 ECG-available patients from the multicenter I-CARE database, the adapted backbone achieved the best ECG-only test AUROC of 0.7333, and combining deep ECG representations with static clinical covariates raised test AUROC to 0.8077 and AUPRC to 0.8970. EEG-based neurological prognostication after cardiac arrest is resource-intensive, so a low-cost bedside ECG alternative could broaden access to early prognostic information in intensive care settings. This work also demonstrates that large ECG foundation models pretrained on millions of recordings can be transferred to non-cardiac clinical prediction tasks, extending their value beyond cardiovascular diagnosis. The pipeline encodes multiple ECG segments per patient into segment-level deep features, aggregates them with quantile pooling at q = 0.24, and compresses them via PCA before combining with clinical covariates. The evaluation is limited to 412 patients from a single multicenter database, and the ECG-only AUROC of 0.7333 remains modest, so external validation and prospective testing are still needed.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Cardiac arrest often causes hypoxic-ischemic brain injury, and clinicians typically use electroencephalography (EEG) to assess neurological prognosis, but EEG requires specialized equipment and expert interpretation. ECG is routinely recorded at the bedside and is far cheaper, yet its prognostic value for brain outcomes has been underexplored. ECGFounder is a foundation model pretrained on over 10 million expert-annotated ECGs from the Harvard-Emory ECG Database, designed to be fine-tuned for downstream tasks. Gradual unfreezing is a transfer-learning strategy that first trains only the last layers and then progressively unfreezes earlier layers, while quantile pooling aggregates many feature vectors into a compact representation robust to outliers.

A new arXiv paper introduces DualViewEval, an agent benchmark compression method that jointly models outcome and process signals to select a small miniset of tasks and predict full-benchmark scores. Across five agent benchmarks and five baselines, it achieves 24x-40x compression on APEX-Agents and BFCL with only 20 tasks, reducing mean absolute error by 14.5%-28.2% and improving Kendall's tau by up to 7.2% on SWE-bench Verified. Agent benchmarks are far more expensive to run than conventional LLM benchmarks because they require multi-step execution and API calls, so this compression technique could substantially cut evaluation cost and time for agent developers. The selected minisets also expose capability differences between agents, offering compact diagnostic feedback for efficient agentic model development. The method identifies six complementary process signals from large-scale trajectories that are systematically associated with final agent performance, and combines them with outcome relations to learn an exact-size miniset. It was evaluated on five agent benchmarks including APEX-Agents, BFCL, and SWE-bench Verified, and the paper reports the best results on all datasets against five representative baselines.

## [HOPE: Higher-Order Pruning of Experts in MoE Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18916) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced HOPE (Higher-Order Pruning of Experts), a second-order pruning objective for mixture-of-experts language models that accounts for cooperative expert interactions rather than treating each expert independently. Evaluated on three frontier MoE models up to 122B parameters across math, instruction following, coding, and agentic benchmarks, HOPE achieved the best average rank of 1.58 out of 5 methods at 50% pruning, with gains up to +6.1% on agentic coding. MoE models achieve strong performance but suffer from huge parameter counts and memory bottlenecks, so better expert pruning directly translates into cheaper deployment. By showing that modeling expert interactions yields better pruning decisions, especially at aggressive compression rates and on complex agentic workloads, HOPE could influence how future MoE compression methods are designed. HOPE provably minimizes an upper bound on the error caused by pruning, and the authors show that the state-of-the-art first-order method REAP is a special case of HOPE where interaction terms are ignored. The advantage is most pronounced at high pruning rates and on challenging agentic workloads, where diverse expert combinations are invoked over long sequences.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) is an architecture in which multiple specialized sub-networks, called experts, are combined and only a subset is activated per input, allowing models to scale to very large parameter counts with limited compute. Expert pruning removes less important experts to shrink memory and inference cost, but existing methods like REAP score each expert independently and assume their contributions simply add up, ignoring the fact that experts in MoE models are used cooperatively.

## [Social Laws Extended to Stochastic Multi-Agent Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18929) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18929) extends the concept of social laws from deterministic, goal-based multi-agent settings to stochastic, reward-based environments. It introduces the notion of α-robustness, which measures the guaranteed utility each agent retains while pursuing its optimal single-agent policy under the assumption that all agents obey the social law, and presents a verification approach based on reducing the problem to a series of Markov decision processes (MDPs). Most real-world multi-agent systems operate under uncertainty and reward trade-offs, so extending social laws to stochastic settings could make coordination guarantees applicable to domains like robotics, autonomous traffic, and distributed resource allocation. The α-robustness metric and MDP-based verification provide a principled way to certify that a social law does not overly harm any individual agent, which is essential for adoption in safety-critical systems. The verification method reduces robustness checking to solving a series of MDPs, leveraging standard probabilistic model-checking techniques. However, empirical validation is limited to toy environments, so scalability to large, complex multi-agent systems remains an open question.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Social laws are pre-defined rules or conventions that restrict the actions available to agents in a multi-agent system, preventing conflicts and enabling coordination without explicit negotiation. Prior work on social laws largely assumed deterministic environments where agents have clear goals, but many real systems are stochastic and reward-driven, meaning outcomes are uncertain and agents optimize cumulative rewards. Markov decision processes (MDPs) are a standard mathematical framework for modeling sequential decision-making under uncertainty, and they are widely used for verification of probabilistic systems.

The paper introduces Dose-Aware Cold Diffusion (DACD), a physics-consistent reconstruction framework that explicitly models radiation dose as a continuous latent factor within a cold diffusion process. It integrates image-based dose-aware perception, multi-scale structural prior extraction, and dose-calibrated step allocation, and adds an iterative forward-backprojection correction to enforce projection-domain data consistency. Low-dose CT reconstruction methods often fail to generalize across continuous and unseen dose levels, producing artifacts and losing anatomical detail. DACD's ability to generalize across a continuous dose range, including unseen doses, could make AI-based CT reconstruction more clinically reliable and reduce the need for retraining per dose protocol. The method was evaluated on three public benchmarks—Mayo-2020, Mayo-2016, and LoDoPaB-CT—and reportedly outperforms state-of-the-art diffusion-based and physics-guided methods in quantitative accuracy and visual fidelity, especially under ultra-low-dose conditions. As an arXiv preprint, it has not yet undergone peer review and no community discussion is available.

Diffusion models are generative models that learn to reverse a degradation process; cold diffusion generalizes this idea by allowing arbitrary, even deterministic, image transforms instead of only Gaussian noise. Physics-informed learning incorporates known physical constraints, such as the CT forward-projection model, into neural networks to improve reliability.

## [Two-Pass Transcribe-Then-Review Fixes Multimodal Review Bottleneck](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18958) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18958) shows that single-pass multimodal review quietly fails, dropping roughly a third of source content and embellishing the rest, and that a two-pass decomposition — first transcribe, then review the transcript — restores faithfulness and coverage across a 21-source suite. The authors demonstrate the bottleneck is generation under load, not perception, since almost all dropped content reappears when the same model is simply asked to transcribe. This finding matters for anyone using multimodal models to review long recordings or documents, because it suggests a simple architectural change — splitting transcription and review into two same-weights passes — can substantially improve output faithfulness without retraining. It also reframes the problem: the issue is not that models cannot see or read the source, but that a single output budget cannot simultaneously support perception, reasoning, and long-form writing. The paper rules out modality as the cause (models read text and an image of the same text equally well) and shows that giving the single pass a much larger reasoning budget does not recover lost content, because the model spends that budget planning a review rather than writing the source down. Two failure modes remain for the two-pass approach: the review pass can run out of room on very long sources, and it can confabulate from memory once the grounding source is removed.

**Background**: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can accept text, images, audio, and video as input and generate natural-language output, making them attractive for reviewing long recordings or documents in a single call. Prior work has largely focused on improving perception and reasoning capabilities, but this paper identifies a generation bottleneck: the single output stream must simultaneously encode what was perceived, the reasoning over it, and the final written review. Task decomposition — breaking a complex job into sequential steps — is a common technique in LLM agent design, and this paper applies it to the review task.

A new arXiv paper introduces WordPolo, a word-finding game where models must discover an unknown target word using only semantic similarity distance scores (1 = correct, higher = further). The authors evaluated GPT-4.1, Llama 4, Claude 3.5 Haiku, Qwen 3, o4-mini, Deepseek-R1, humans, and a novel heuristic across 1,500 puzzles, finding solve rates ranging from 4% to 62% and introducing progression-based metrics that capture partial progress missed by accuracy alone. Traditional LLM benchmarks rely on dataset accuracy alone, which reveals nothing about the quality or faithfulness of a model's reasoning process. WordPolo makes iterative reasoning and adaptive search directly observable, showing that reasoning models can be hindered by overthinking and underthinking, and arguing for benchmarks that measure both process and outcome. Beyond solve rates, the paper introduces progression-based metrics that reveal models often make meaningful progress even when they fail to solve a puzzle, and notes that successful models exhibit human-like search strategies. The code and dataset are available at the project's demo site.

Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) such as o4-mini and Deepseek-R1 are LLMs trained to produce explicit multi-step reasoning traces, but standard benchmarks typically score only final answers. WordPolo combines these ideas, using semantic distance scores as the sole feedback signal so that a model's step-by-step search behavior becomes the object of evaluation.

## [MIRAGE Study Shows Conversation State Distorts Evidence Use in Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19059) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MIRAGE (Multimodal Interaction Retrieval, Attribution, and Grounding Evaluation) is a controlled study that varies only conversation state while holding evidence objects, questions, and scoring fixed, testing whether multimodal personal agents can determine answerability, recover the correct source, and answer from it. Across seven frontier and open-weight multimodal backbones, it finds that pre-compaction depth and post-compaction continuation form distinct, non-monotonic failure regimes rather than a single degradation curve. The findings show that outcome-only evaluation overestimates true historical evidence use, which matters for long-running personal assistants where continuity across dialogue, files, and workspace state is essential. It suggests agent evaluation should be conducted under conversation-state variation rather than inferred from final-answer correctness alone. Open-weight models rely heavily on context continuity and are reluctant to spontaneously switch to tool-mediated retrieval when provenance fails; retrieval pressure improves source attribution in deep pre-compaction states for tool-compliant models but consistently regresses after compaction, where stored evidence has already degraded.

Context compaction reduces the tokens used to represent an ongoing conversation while preserving state needed for future steps, but it can degrade the stored history agents rely on. MIRAGE isolates this variable to test whether agents truly ground answers in historical evidence or merely produce plausible outputs.

## [Probabilistic Linear Explanations Unify Sparse Explainability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19077) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.19077) introduces a unified framework for probabilistic explainability based on sparse, anchored linear models that applies to both binary classification and continuous regression. The authors prove that minimizing relevance error for such explanations is ClassNPPP-hard for neural networks, and propose a Mixed Integer Programming formulation plus a polynomial-time Iterative Hard Thresholding algorithm to solve it. This work bridges formal explainability and practical post-hoc methods like LIME and MAPLE, offering explanations that satisfy anchoring and sparsity constraints by construction while achieving lower relevance error. It could influence how explainable AI tools are designed for both classification and regression, especially in high-stakes domains requiring mathematically grounded justifications. The framework maps instances to the Boolean hypercube, strictly generalizing subset-based approaches by capturing both magnitude and direction of feature contributions under a prescribed sparsity budget k. The relevance error of any k-sparse explanation is bounded by its fidelity error up to a small local multiplicative factor, and the MIP formulation yields provably optimal empirical solutions with polynomial sample complexity.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Explainable AI seeks to justify individual predictions, but abductive explanations often involve too many features to be human-understandable, and probabilistic relaxations have been mostly limited to categorical classification. This paper addresses that gap by using sparse linear models anchored to the instance, which are easier to interpret and can handle regression. The complexity class ClassNPPP is a high-level complexity class beyond NP, indicating that finding optimal explanations for neural networks is computationally intractable in general.

## [Certified Recovery Updates Defend Quantum Error Correction Against Misleading AI Advice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19090) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.19090) identifies an ambiguity in passive syndrome records that lets an attacker exploit influence over an AI adviser to produce harmful quantum error-correction updates. The authors propose using additional calibration measurements on known encoded states to supply the missing sign information, plus a separate evaluator that accepts an update only when calibration uncertainty and a justified drift bound certify improvement over the current recovery. This work sits at the intersection of AI safety and quantum computing, showing that an AI adviser's recommendations in quantum error correction can be adversarially manipulated and that certified acceptance criteria can reject harmful advice while retaining beneficial updates. It matters for anyone building AI-assisted control stacks for fault-tolerant quantum hardware, where a single bad recovery update could silently degrade logical performance. In an odd-distance square toric code with error-free preparation, syndrome measurements, and recovery operations, opposite coherent X rotations produce identical passive syndrome-history distributions, yet a fixed phase correction helps at one sign and harms at the other; a terminal logical measurement on known encoded calibration states recovers the missing sign. The paper also derives sufficient limits on calibration age, and in matched simulations a validated channel-specific bound retains more beneficial updates than the general bound after accounting for evaluation time, while a separate surface-code experiment with stochastic circuit faults and drifting noise shows deterministic controllers achieve at least as many beneficial updates with the same observations.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Quantum error correction protects fragile quantum information by repeatedly measuring stabilizer operators to obtain a syndrome, from which a decoder infers and applies a recovery operation. The toric code and surface code are leading topological stabilizer codes, and in realistic fault-tolerant architectures an AI or classical adviser may recommend which recovery to apply. Certified robustness is a machine-learning concept that provides mathematical guarantees that a model's output cannot be changed by bounded adversarial perturbations, and this paper transplants that idea to quantum recovery selection.

## [Reference Choice Distorts Chest X-ray Report Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19093) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.19093) quantifies how variations in radiologist reporting practices affect the evaluation of AI-generated chest X-ray reports, introducing a radiologist-informed taxonomy and a rewriting method called ReRef. The authors also release MIMIC-CXR-Ext-ReRef, a radiologist-validated dataset of 120 original-alternative reference report pairs derived from MIMIC-CXR. The findings show that metric sensitivity to reporting style is large enough to change model rankings, meaning current evaluation pipelines may reward conformity to reporting conventions rather than clinical accuracy. This could affect how radiology report generation models are compared and selected for clinical deployment. On MIMIC-CXR with RadCliQ-v1, condensing discussion of normal findings in reference reports caused Libra to drop from first to second place while CheXOne rose from third to first among nine RRG models. The authors argue that many current metrics fail to decouple clinical interpretation from conformity to reporting practices.

This means two clinically equivalent reports can look very different textually, potentially penalizing correct AI outputs.

## [rMuscle Cuts VLA Robot Inference Latency via Muscle-Memory Caching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19104) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced rMuscle, a real-time Vision-Language-Action (VLA) inference framework that exploits cross-execution task similarity, including internal model states, through a dual-phase muscle-memory cache. It achieves 1.29–1.42X speedup on RTX 4090 and Jetson Thor across LIBERO, RoboTwin, and physical manipulation tasks while preserving original success rates on real robots. VLA inference latency directly affects robot responsiveness and motion smoothness, and this work targets a real bottleneck in deploying embodied AI in factories and other repetitive industrial settings. By reusing computation and weight accesses across similar executions, it could make real-time robotic control more practical on edge hardware. The framework combines a Context Cache that reuses visual-token outputs to reduce computation with an Action Cache that reuses neuron activation patterns to reduce weight accesses, keeping overhead low via online cache recomputation, sliding-window cache retrieval, and mask sharing across consecutive denoising steps. The reported speedups are measured on RTX 4090 and Jetson Thor, with success rates maintained on real-world robots.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models are multimodal foundation models that take a text instruction and an image observation, encode them into a latent representation, and generate low-level robot actions. They have become the dominant policy paradigm for robot learning, but their inference latency is a key obstacle to smooth real-time control. rMuscle draws an analogy to human muscle memory, where repeated movements become faster and more automatic, to cache and reuse computation across similar robot executions.

## [Affora: A Design System for Agent-Friendly Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19125) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.19125) introduces Affora, a design system that makes software interfaces readable by both humans and computer-use agents while preserving visual freedom. The work reports three controlled studies on component implementations, visual variation, and interaction-design principles, plus an evaluation on independently authored interfaces showing gains where agent-readable meaning is deficient. As computer-use agents increasingly operate software built for humans, interfaces that leave actions or task state unclear to machine readers cause failures and inefficiency. Affora offers a shared interface approach that connects user experience and agent experience, potentially influencing HCI and AI agent interaction design rather than requiring separate agent-only surfaces. The paper provides reusable implementations and executable checks, and finds that substantial visual variation remains possible when interaction meaning is preserved. Evaluation shows limited effects where existing deficits are absent or outside Affora's coverage, and a workflow case gives preliminary evidence of reduced interaction cost.

Existing interfaces and APIs are typically designed for either human users or traditional clients, forcing agents to infer missing context. Affora is a design system, meaning a set of reusable components and guidelines, that aims to make interface meaning explicit to both human and machine readers.

## [Generated Audio Loudness Shapes Force-Aware Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19137) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.19137) proposes a pipeline that jointly leverages generated video and audio to derive motion trajectories and corresponding desired-force profiles from a natural-language task prompt. The relative loudness of generated contact sounds is mapped to a bounded, time-varying force profile that is executed on a Franka Panda robot via a closed-loop force regulator. Kinematic-only video-to-trajectory methods fail in contact-rich tasks because they lack force information, so using generated audio as a proxy for contact force could enable zero-shot force-aware manipulation and serve as a data generation engine for training closed-loop policies. This matters for robot learning, where acquiring force-labeled demonstrations is expensive and contact-rich tasks remain a major bottleneck. The audio pipeline processes generated contact sound (SAM-Audio) and maps relative loudness to a bounded desired-force profile; when loudness is high, desired force rises to about 10N, and the regulator drives the virtual target into the surface, yielding measured forces of 11.4N. Execution uses a 1kHz fixed-stiffness impedance controller that tracks the desired force via virtual-target displacement on a Franka Panda.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Recent video generation models can synthesize plausible manipulation videos, and prior work learns robot trajectories from them, but these trajectories are purely kinematic and contain no force information. Contact-rich tasks such as pressing, wiping, or inserting require appropriate contact forces, so a force specification is essential. The Franka Panda is a 7-axis collaborative robot arm known for good force/torque control, making it a common platform for such research. This paper adds generated audio, whose loudness correlates with contact intensity, as a heuristic source of force profiles.
