## [ComPO: A Zeroth-Order Paradigm for LLM Preference Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19144) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes Comparison-based Preference Optimization (ComPO), a zeroth-order alignment method that extracts directional information from preference pairs via comparison oracles without directly optimizing a differentiable preference loss. It establishes a convergence guarantee for the offline scheme under smoothness, gradient sparsity, and oracle-latent-objective compatibility, and introduces an online variant using unlabeled policy generations for reverse-KL control against a reference policy. Direct preference alignment methods such as DPO are widely used but suffer from likelihood displacement, where probability mass shifts away from preferred responses with small likelihood margins. ComPO offers a gradient-free alternative with theoretical guarantees and demonstrated gains on Mistral, Llama, Gemma-2, Qwen3, and Gemma-3, potentially giving alignment researchers a more robust tool for preference fine-tuning. The convergence analysis relies on smoothness, gradient sparsity, and compatibility between the comparison oracle and a latent objective, while the online extension's performance guarantee requires local coverage and in-distribution pairwise reward accuracy. Experiments report improvements over existing direct alignment methods, including length-controlled win rates, with pair-level diagnostics consistent with mitigating likelihood displacement.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Zeroth-order optimization is a gradient-free approach that estimates descent directions using only function evaluations, useful when gradients are unavailable or unreliable. Preference alignment aims to steer LLMs toward human-preferred outputs, and direct methods like DPO optimize a differentiable preference loss on paired responses. Reverse KL divergence measures how one distribution differs from another and is often used to keep a fine-tuned policy close to a reference policy.

## [Search Procedure, Not Objective, Drives Tokeniser Quality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19145) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.19145) disentangles the two orthogonal axes of tokeniser design — optimisation objective (compression vs. log-likelihood) and search procedure (bottom-up merging vs. top-down pruning) — by introducing two new algorithms, BottomUpLL and TopDownComp, that complete the 2x2 design space. Training language models across model sizes, vocabulary sizes, and English/multilingual domains, the authors find that the search procedure, not the objective, is the dominant factor: bottom-up tokenisers consistently achieve lower bits-per-byte in most settings. This finding offers concrete, principled guidance for tokeniser construction, suggesting that researchers and practitioners should prioritise the search procedure when designing or selecting tokenisers rather than focusing on the optimisation objective. It could influence future tokeniser design across the NLP ecosystem, where BPE and UnigramLM remain the two dominant algorithms used by modern language models. The study evaluates models on bits-per-byte (BPB), a tokeniser-agnostic metric that normalises cross-entropy by byte count, and finds bottom-up tokenisers consistently win there; however, on the BLiMP grammaticality benchmark, no consistent relationship between design choice and performance emerges. The experiments vary model size, vocabulary size, and domain (English-only vs. multilingual), giving the conclusions broad coverage.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Tokenisation splits raw text into subword units before a language model processes it, and the two dominant algorithms are byte-pair encoding (BPE), which iteratively merges the most frequent character pairs, and UnigramLM, which uses a probabilistic model trained with EM and segments via Viterbi decoding. These algorithms differ along two axes: their optimisation objective (compression vs. log-likelihood) and their search procedure (bottom-up merging vs. top-down pruning), but prior comparisons confounded the two, making it unclear which axis caused observed differences. Bits-per-byte (BPB) is a tokeniser-agnostic evaluation metric that normalises cross-entropy by the number of bytes, enabling fair comparisons across different tokenisers.

## [Survey Maps EEG-to-Generative AI Decoding Across Image, Text, and Audio](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.12048) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv survey (2502.12048v4) systematically reviews 2017–2025 literature on decoding non-invasive EEG signals into images, text, and audio using generative AI. It catalogs datasets, architectures (GANs, VAEs, transformers, diffusion models), EEG feature-encoding techniques, evaluation metrics, and open challenges, while highlighting open-source datasets and baseline implementations. This survey consolidates a rapidly growing but scattered interdisciplinary field, providing a foundational reference for both newcomers and experts in brain-computer interfaces and computational neuroscience. By identifying methodological trends and open-source resources, it aims to accelerate reproducible research and systematic benchmarking in EEG-driven neural decoding. The review finds EEG-to-image models predominantly use encoder-decoder architectures based on GANs, VAEs, or diffusion models; EEG-to-text approaches increasingly leverage transformer-based language models for open-vocabulary decoding; and EEG-to-audio methods typically map signals to mel-spectrograms rendered by neural vocoders. Major limitations include small and heterogeneous datasets, limited cross-subject generalization, and the absence of standardized benchmarks.

ADS uses an agentic workflow that builds and tests each DomiKnowS component individually, and it supports optional human-in-the-loop intervention so experienced users can refine intermediate outputs. The paper reports that manually coding even a single DomiKnowS component takes about an hour, whereas ADS produces a complete program in 10-15 minutes.

## [Cross-Domain Mapping Boosts Human Creativity but Not LLM Creativity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.19087) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv study (2603.19087v3) tested cross-domain mapping — forcing creators to draw analogies from random remote sources — on human participants and seven LLMs designing features for ten daily products. Humans reliably benefited from the intervention, while LLMs generated more original ideas than humans on average but showed no overall benefit, with gains appearing only at high semantic distances and only for the highest-rated models. The findings challenge the assumption that the same creativity interventions work equally for humans and AI, which matters for prompt engineering, human-AI collaboration, and how we evaluate machine creativity. It suggests that simply prompting LLMs with random analogies is not a reliable originality boost, and that model capability and source remoteness are key moderating factors. The study used 140 Prolific participants, seven LLMs, ten consumer products, and 26 inspiration sources, with each creator either translating a randomly assigned source (e.g., cactus or GPS) into a product feature or proposing an innovation from an unmet user need. Humans tended to transfer surface features of the source, whereas LLMs transferred structural and functional properties, revealing systematically different transfer strategies.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Cross-domain mapping is a creativity technique in which a creator draws an analogy from an unrelated domain to inspire a novel idea, based on the idea that remote associations foster originality. Large language models are increasingly used for ideation tasks, but it remains unclear whether they are creative in the same way humans are and whether the same interventions increase creativity in both. Semantic distance refers to how far apart the source and target concepts are in meaning.

## [ADMITOR Certifies LLM Optimization Models Without Labels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15565) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced ADMITOR, a label-free admission gate that certifies LLM-generated optimization models by requiring cross-family agreement on the stated problem and on instances with resampled parameters, then applies a threshold fitted on solver-verified problems to accept, abstain, or escalate, with a finite-sample bound on the false-discovery rate. Inside a state-of-the-art skill learner, ADMITOR raised candidate-level admission precision to 0.927, versus 0.871 for majority vote and 0.726 for execution success, and its library achieved the highest macro accuracy of 58.4 across five public benchmarks. Experience-learning agents for optimization modeling reuse stored trajectories as skills, but a single wrong trajectory can be retrieved repeatedly and poison the library, and real problem streams provide no ground-truth answers to filter them. ADMITOR offers a principled label-free alternative with a statistical guarantee, which matters for researchers building LLM agents that must improve from unlabeled deployment streams. The gain comes from the accepted value being external to the learner and unanimous across families, while resampling never changed an accepted value on this stream and only reduced coverage. The finite-sample false-discovery bound holds on the calibration set but not on the benchmark stream; an audit traced most false certificates to benchmark texts that omit or round the numbers needed to reproduce the labeled answer, and a label-free check of extracted numbers against the text flags most of these cases.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Optimization modeling means translating a real-world problem into a formal mathematical program that a solver can execute, and LLM agents increasingly learn to do this by storing solved trajectories as reusable skills. Existing learners admit a trajectory only by matching a known optimum or label, which is unavailable in real ticket streams, while label-free substitutes such as execution success or single-instance agreement can admit wrong models. False-discovery rate is a standard statistical measure of the expected proportion of accepted hypotheses that are actually wrong, and a finite-sample bound guarantees it holds for a limited amount of calibration data.

## [Benchmarking LLM Judges for Voice-Agent Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24314) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2608.24314v2) benchmarks LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation for conversational voice agents by comparing human judgments with GPT-4.1 and GPT-5 on telecom and retail voice-agent conversations across conversational quality and safety dimensions. The same interactions were scored under three evaluation configurations (p0, p1, p2) to test whether automated judgments are sensitive to the evaluation setup and whether patterns generalize across configurations and judge models. The findings show that LLM-based evaluation can be an effective component of large-scale voice-agent assessment, but its reliability is metric- and configuration-dependent rather than uniform, which directly informs how teams should design hybrid evaluation pipelines. This matters for AI/ML practitioners building voice agents who need to decide which conversational attributes can be safely automated and which still require human oversight. Beyond aggregate agreement, the study examines metric-level correlations, evaluator consistency, and systematic human-LLM disagreement to identify which conversational attributes can be judged reliably by automation. It also highlights pipeline-level factors such as speech generation, streaming, and error propagation across ASR, reasoning, and tool-calling stages, motivating an end-to-end comparison of how human and LLM judges score the same interactions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: LLM-as-a-Judge is a technique in natural language processing where a large language model assesses the quality, relevance, or correctness of another model's output against predefined criteria, and it has become a common way to evaluate LLM-powered products at scale. Voice agents add complexity because their quality depends not only on the final text but also on speech generation, streaming, and errors that propagate through automatic speech recognition (ASR), reasoning, and tool-calling stages. This paper asks whether LLM judges can reliably replace or supplement human evaluators for such agents, and where human judgment remains necessary.

## [EvoUndo Enforces Recoverability for Self-Evolving LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28363) ⭐️ 7.0/10

EvoUndo, a new arXiv preprint (2608.28363v2), introduces a framework for representing, synthesizing, diagnosing, and independently verifying the recoverability of model-generated self-modifications across counterfactual states. Across 600 unseen one-shot self-evolution tasks, the authors identify 197 capability-improving mutations that fail recoverability verification, and while conventional repair recovers 0/197, an extended recovery calculus raises empirical oracle recovery to 191/197. This work highlights a safety gap in LLM agent self-evolution: a mutation that improves capability can leave persistent effects that cannot be safely reversed in other states. By showing that reliable self-evolution requires co-designing verification, state grounding, witness semantics, and recovery-language expressivity, it pushes agent research toward formal recoverability guarantees rather than iterative prompting alone. A protocol-locked 2x2 grounding-by-expressivity intervention separates two bottlenecks: exact state-address grounding raises recovery from 0/48 to 38/48 (79.2%) when the original language suffices, while extending the recovery language enables 142/143 (99.3%) recovery in the oracle-defined S1 stratum. On the primary gpt-oss-120b backbone, adding exact-address diagnostics to the richer language reduces recovery to 133/143 (93.0%), but a Qwen3.8-27B replication preserves the grounding and expressivity effects without this negative interaction, indicating the interaction is model-dependent.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: LLM agents increasingly modify their own prompts, tools, middleware, resources, and execution harnesses at runtime, a practice known as harness self-evolution. Such self-modification can improve task performance, but it also creates persistent state changes that may be unsafe or impossible to undo when the agent later operates in a different context. Recoverability verification asks whether a mutation made in one state can be safely reversed in counterfactual states, and the recovery language L0 is the original representation used to express and check such reversals.

## [Iris: 35B and 397B Search Agents Trained via SFT-RL Climbing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04304) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Iris-mini and Iris-pro, two search agents trained at the 35B-A3B and 397B-A17B scales, along with a data pipeline that reverse-constructs multi-hop questions from web hyperlinks and an RL training recipe against live search. With inference-time context management enabled, the models reach 82.2/84.8/86.9/52.3 and 88.6/85.1/92.9/56.4 on BrowseComp, BrowseComp-ZH, DeepSearchQA, and HLE, claimed as the strongest overall results among open-source search agents in their parameter ranges. This work matters because it offers a fully documented recipe—data construction, SFT, and RL against live search—for building capable search agents, a direction that is increasingly central to making LLMs useful for real-world information retrieval. If the promised weights and recipe are released, it could lower the barrier for the open-source community to reproduce and extend frontier-level search agents. The pipeline reverse-constructs multi-hop chains over an entity graph distilled from a seed page and its out-links, rewrites every non-answer entity into a descriptive reference so no clue can be resolved by string matching, and keeps only questions that a reference model fails closed-book yet solves once supporting evidence is supplied. Training alternates SFT and RL in a procedure called SFT-RL climbing, with the reward judge and observation summarizer served inside the training cluster and over-long rollouts interrupted at the request level and resumed from their committed prefix; all results come from a single ReAct agent with no sub-agents and no test-time verification.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-hop question answering is a long-standing NLP task in which a system must combine evidence scattered across multiple documents or reasoning steps to reach an answer, and it is typically harder than single-hop QA. Search agents are LLM-based systems that interleave reasoning with calls to a search tool, and they are commonly trained with reinforcement learning, the same family of techniques behind RLHF, where a reward signal optimizes the policy's behavior. The model scales cited here (35B-A3B, 397B-A17B) follow a Mixture-of-Experts design, where only a subset of expert parameters is activated per token, keeping inference cost far below the total parameter count.

## [LLMs Can Choose Alike While Computing Differently, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07478) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.07478v2) recorded internal activations from four open-weight LLMs — including dense and mixture-of-experts architectures and a matched base–instruct Qwen2.5 pair — during one-shot play of 144 strict ordinal 2×2 games. It found that incentive and choice were decodable in every model, but models differed in whether the represented incentive actually reached the decision, and the base and instruction-tuned Qwen2.5 models chose almost identically at baseline despite differing in whether incentive influenced choice. The result shows that similar behavioral outputs can rest on different internal computations, meaning behavioral benchmarks alone may not reveal whether a model is genuinely responsive to incentives. This has direct implications for AI alignment and interpretability, since post-training can reshape the path from represented incentive to decision while leaving behavior and decodable information largely intact. The study used a prespecified incentive traced from prompt through activations to choice, and found dense models mirrored the unadjusted human decline in strategic behavior as game complexity increased. Fixed decision cues were internally distinguishable but changed choices only selectively, and the authors note that strengthening an incentive shifted preference only in some models where it was tested.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Mechanistic interpretability aims to reverse-engineer the internal computations of large language models rather than only observing their outputs. Mixture-of-experts (MoE) models route inputs through multiple specialized sub-networks, while dense models activate all parameters, and base versus instruction-tuned variants of the same model (such as Qwen2.5) differ mainly through post-training. Strict ordinal 2×2 games are simple two-player, two-choice games where only the ranking of outcomes matters, making them a controlled testbed for studying strategic decision-making.

## [CFRC Enables Cheap Stateful Agent Handoffs Without Restarting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13800) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The arXiv paper introduces Commitment-Frontier Residual Completion (CFRC), a formal method for stateful agent handoffs that freezes a residual contract from accepted progress and admits execution only when the remainder is covered by live evidence. Across five environments and two same-provider model pairs, CFRC matches strong full-task agents' macro accuracy at just 22.0%–34.6% of their inference cost, with cross-provider transfer results as well. Multi-agent systems that route or cascade work across models often lose accepted choices, realized effects, or unfinished obligations when control transfers, forcing costly restarts. CFRC offers a principled, formally verified way to resume from the reached state, which could substantially cut inference costs for agent orchestration platforms while preserving correctness. CFRC enforces three ordering rules—target-before-proposal, whole-proposal-before-authority, and live-evidence-before-success—and establishes contract-relative partial correctness that extends to the original residual request under complete contract construction. The evaluation covers five environments and two same-provider model pairs, with additional cross-provider results demonstrating broader transfer.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: In multi-agent systems, a handoff occurs when one LLM agent transfers control to another specialized agent, and a good handoff must carry the goal, relevant evidence, completed work, open questions, and constraints. Routing and cascades reduce tool-agent cost by sending work to cheaper models, but stateful handoffs risk discarding accepted choices and realized effects, which is why the paper frames the problem as commitment-constrained residual completion. CFRC is the proposed solution that freezes a residual contract at the so-called commitment frontier and only executes when the remainder is fully covered.

## [Symbolic Replay Gives NetOps Agents Action-Level Safety Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14422) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.14422v2) constructs per-action ground truth for the network repair task in NetArena by symbolically replaying the emulated network and validating it against the environment at every turn, yielding the exact value of every action. From these action-level values the authors derive two pre-execution targets — whether an action reduces repair distance (progress) or increases it (harm) — and show across 10 agent models that verifiers using internal signals predict both more reliably than a baseline relying only on observable signals. Autonomous NetOps agents are increasingly expected to act as control-loop engines in datacenter fabrics, but they must abstain from high-risk actions that could cause or extend downtime — which requires knowing an action's impact before execution. This work fills a gap in NetOps agent benchmarks by supplying the missing per-action ground truth, offering a path toward safety feedback that lets agent harnesses abstain from risky actions and protect the target system. The ground truth is produced by a symbolic replay of the emulated network that is validated against the environment at every turn, so each action receives an exact value rather than a heuristic estimate. The evaluation covers 10 agent models, and the paper's stated perspective is to feed these internal signals back into an agent harness as safety feedback for abstention; the abstract is truncated, so full experimental details remain to be confirmed from the paper itself.

NetArena is a dynamic benchmark generation framework that evaluates LLM agents on real-world network tasks inside network emulators, and its network repair task asks an agent to diagnose and fix misconfigurations. Symbolic replay means re-executing a recorded or modeled sequence of events in a symbolic form to compute exact outcomes, which here is used to determine the true value of each candidate repair action.

## [Lightning Weave Composes Capabilities for Efficient Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14708) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers from the Jet AI projects (including Song Han and Han Cai) released Lightning Weave, a post-training framework that composes independently learned capabilities into a single student model via on-policy distillation. On Qwen3.5-4B, it raises HMMT 2025 accuracy from 59.2% to 64.0% with 10.7% fewer response tokens, and LiveCodeBench v5 accuracy from 41.7% to 54.2% with 9.6% fewer response tokens, with code released on GitHub. This work offers a practical new route to efficient reasoning by reusing existing specialist models instead of jointly optimizing competing accuracy and efficiency objectives from scratch. It could significantly reduce inference costs for reasoning models in mathematics and code while improving accuracy, and the released code makes the approach reproducible for the broader research community. Each acquired capability is represented as the policy shift (log-ratio) from the pre-post-training model to the resulting specialist, and Lightning Weave combines aligned log-ratio shifts at shared student token states using Tilted-Target DOPD to convert cached signals into a stable learning target. Because each anchor pair scores cached trajectories only once, subsequent student training does not require serving multiple live anchor models concurrently, and adjusting the relative strengths of anchor signals yields a strong empirical accuracy-efficiency Pareto frontier.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: On-policy distillation is a knowledge distillation technique where a student model samples its own trajectories and a teacher model provides dense token-level supervision on those trajectories, combining the state alignment of on-policy reinforcement learning with the dense supervision of distillation. Reasoning models often face a trade-off between accuracy and inference efficiency, since behaviors that improve one objective can hurt the other. Lightning Weave builds on this by treating independently post-trained specialists as teachers whose behavioral shifts can be extracted and composed into one student.

The paper introduces 'little m', an AI agent that combines a domain-specific knowledge repository with LLM-driven interaction to help formulate mathematical optimization models for industrial process control from messy real-world specifications. Through automated structural assessments and double-blind human evaluation, little m substantially outperforms state-of-the-art LLMs in generating semantically correct models, but the evaluations only assess formulation quality rather than solver feasibility, formal physical validity, or closed-loop industrial performance. ## [Graph-Based Interpretable NLI Using Atomic Propositions and ConceptNet](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16814) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.16814v2) introduces a fully graph-based natural language inference pipeline in which the classifier never directly reads the input text; instead, sentences are decomposed into atomic propositions, converted into ConceptNet triples via constrained decoding, and represented as three graphs per pair (premise, hypothesis, and a retrieved ConceptNet subgraph) that are fed into a fine-tuned 0.8-billion-parameter language model. The pipeline achieves 89.7% accuracy on SNLI, only 1.9 points below an identically trained text-based model, and combining graphs with text reaches 92.1% on SNLI. This work directly addresses the lack of auditable decision structures in LLM-based NLI systems, showing that interpretable graph representations can approach text-based accuracy with a much smaller model. It matters for explainable AI and NLP practitioners who need transparent, verifiable inference, and the finding that graphs and text are complementary suggests hybrid architectures as a promising direction. On ANLI, the pipeline matches published RoBERTa-large performance on rounds R2 and R3 (48.0% vs. 48.9% and 44.9% vs. 44.4%) but trails by 16 points on R1, yielding an overall gap of 9 to 14 points versus its text counterpart; the authors term this the "price of interpretability" and attribute it to representational limitations rather than data constraints. Ablation studies show graphs and text are complementary, with the combined modality achieving 92.1% accuracy on SNLI.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Natural language inference (NLI) is the task of determining whether a hypothesis sentence can be inferred from a premise sentence, typically classified as entailment, contradiction, or neutral; the SNLI corpus is a standard benchmark of 570,000 human-written English sentence pairs. Atomic propositions are minimal, irreducible units of meaning that cannot be broken down into simpler sentences, while ConceptNet is a knowledge graph of commonsense relations expressed as triples. Constrained decoding restricts a language model's token generation to enforce a specific output format, such as valid ConceptNet triples.

## [Paper Proves Theoretical Limits on Transfer Learning Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2006.12694) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The arXiv paper 2006.12694v2 proves several novel theoretical results on transfer learning, showing that improvement is bounded by the degree of probabilistic change and that transferred information must be carefully selected and dependent on the target problem. Transfer learning is widely used in practice but lacks well-developed theory; these results provide formal guidance on when and how transfer can help, affecting both researchers and practitioners who rely on pretrained models. The proofs build on the algorithmic search framework for machine learning, which measures a learning algorithm's performance by expected per-query probability of success, allowing the results to apply to a wide range of learning problems using transfer.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Transfer learning takes information and insight from one problem domain and applies it to a new domain, and it is commonly used in computer vision and natural language processing through pretraining. The algorithmic search framework is a theoretical model that views machine learning as a search over hypotheses and has been used to prove impossibility results. This paper extends that framework to analyze transfer learning.

<a id="item-190"></a>
## [LCA-UQ: Label-Confidence-Aware Uncertainty Estimation for LLM Hallucination Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.07255) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2412.07255v2) proposes Label-Confidence-Aware Uncertainty Quantification (LCA-UQ), a method based on Pointwise Kullback-Leibler (PKL) divergence that bridges global entropy from multiple stochastic samples and the local confidence of the candidate answer being evaluated. Empirical evaluations across popular LLMs and NLP datasets show that label sources significantly affect classification, and LCA-UQ outperforms existing entropy-based uncertainty estimation. Hallucination remains a major obstacle to deploying LLMs in safety-critical applications, and existing uncertainty quantification methods that rely only on sample entropy can produce biased classifications. By incorporating the confidence of the candidate answer itself, LCA-UQ offers a more reliable way to flag potentially invalid outputs, which could improve trustworthiness in high-stakes domains such as healthcare and scientific reasoning. The method uses Pointwise KL divergence to measure the discrepancy between the distribution of sampled outputs and the confidence assigned to a specific candidate answer, explicitly addressing the gap between global entropy and local confidence. The paper reports that label sources (i.e., how the candidate answer is defined) significantly impact classification outcomes, and that LCA-UQ captures nuances between sampling results and label sources better than prior entropy-based approaches.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Uncertainty quantification (UQ) for LLMs aims to estimate how likely a generated answer is to be correct, typically by measuring the entropy of multiple stochastic samples — the idea being that consistent answers indicate low uncertainty. Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence is a statistical measure of how one probability distribution differs from another, and the pointwise variant applies this comparison at the level of individual candidate answers. Hallucinations are fluent but factually incorrect LLM outputs, and detecting them reliably is essential for safe deployment.

## [Mem2Ego Enhances Vision-Language Models with Global-to-Ego Memory for Embodied Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.14254) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Mem2Ego introduces a novel vision-language model (VLM)-based navigation framework that adaptively retrieves task-relevant cues from a global memory module and integrates them with the agent's egocentric observations. The method surpasses previous state-of-the-art approaches on the HSSD and HM3D benchmarks and demonstrates strong performance on a real robot. This work addresses a key limitation in embodied navigation by combining global and ego-centric memory, improving spatial reasoning for long-horizon tasks. It could advance robotics and AI by enabling more efficient and reliable navigation in complex, unfamiliar environments. The framework dynamically aligns global contextual information with local perception, mitigating the loss of geometric information in language-based representations and the partial observability of first-person views. It is a preprint (arXiv:2502.14254v3) and has not yet undergone peer review.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Embodied navigation requires an agent to explore unfamiliar environments and reach goals using sensory inputs. Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have been used to provide commonsense and spatial reasoning, but existing LLM-based methods convert global maps into language descriptions, losing geometric details, while VLM-based methods rely solely on first-person views, leading to suboptimal decisions. Mem2Ego bridges these approaches by integrating global memory with egocentric observations.

## [CompArt Aligns Text-to-Image Generation with Principles of Art](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.12018) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose Aesthetic Alignment, a task that aligns text-to-image diffusion models to explicit compositional constraints defined by the Principles of Art (PoA) such as Balance, Rhythm, and Emphasis. They release CompArt, a dataset of 80,032 WikiArt images with captions and PoA analyses generated by a multimodal LLM, plus ArtDapter, a lightweight disentangled adapter that steers a pretrained T2I model along 10 PoA dimensions while preserving its semantic capability. Current T2I models handle semantic alignment well but offer poor control over how elements are composed, and prior aesthetic work treats aesthetics as a single preference-driven notion like "high quality" or "breathtaking." By operationalizing composition through established art-education principles, this work gives users a more interpretable and controllable interface for aesthetic steering, which matters for creative tools, design workflows, and any application needing reliable compositional control. CompArt contains 80,032 WikiArt images augmented with captions and PoA analyses produced by a multimodal LLM under structured prompting, and ArtDapter is described as lightweight and disentangled, enabling steering along 10 PoA dimensions. Experiments under a dual evaluation protocol show improved adherence to PoA controls over strong baselines, though the work is a specialized contribution rather than an immediate industry-changing result.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Text-to-image diffusion models generate images by iteratively denoising random noise conditioned on a text prompt, and they have become very good at producing what a prompt describes (semantic alignment). However, controlling how visual elements are arranged (composition) remains difficult, and existing aesthetic tuning usually collapses many qualities into a single preference score. The Principles of Art are a standard framework in art education—Balance, Rhythm, Emphasis, and similar concepts—used to describe how elements are organized into a coherent composition. Adapters are lightweight trainable modules inserted into a frozen pretrained network, allowing parameter-efficient fine-tuning without retraining the whole model.

## [BOOM Benchmark Exposes Weak OOD Generalization in Molecular ML Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.01912) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers from LLNL released BOOM, a chemically-informed benchmark for out-of-distribution (OOD) molecular property prediction, evaluating over 150 model-task combinations. They found that no existing model generalizes strongly across all tasks, with even the top-performing model showing an average OOD error 3x higher than in-distribution error. This benchmark provides the first systematic evaluation of OOD generalization in molecular property prediction, a critical capability for AI-driven drug discovery where novel molecules must be accurately assessed. The finding that chemical foundation models do not extrapolate well OOD highlights a major gap that must be addressed before ML can reliably accelerate real-world molecular design. The benchmark includes extensive ablation experiments showing how data generation, pre-training, hyperparameter optimization, model architecture, and molecular representation affect OOD performance. Models with high inductive bias can perform well on OOD tasks with simple, specific properties, but current chemical foundation models do not show strong OOD extrapolation. The benchmark is open-source at https://github.com/FLASK-LLNL/BOOM.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization refers to a model's ability to maintain accuracy when tested on data that differs from its training distribution, which is crucial for real-world applications where data inevitably shifts. In molecular property prediction, models are trained to map molecular structures to properties like solubility or toxicity, and discovering novel molecules requires accurate predictions for molecules unlike those seen during training. Chemical foundation models, such as Molformer and ChemBERTa, are transformer-based models pre-trained on large molecular datasets, but their OOD performance has not been systematically benchmarked until now.

## [LLMs Extract Probabilistic Knowledge to Parameterize Bayesian Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15918) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2505.15918v3) evaluates whether Large Language Models can generate probabilistic knowledge to parameterize Bayesian Networks, testing on eighty publicly available BNs spanning domains from healthcare to finance. The experiments show that querying LLMs about conditional probabilities of events yields meaningful results that outperform random and uniform baselines, as well as approaches based on next-token generation probabilities. This work bridges probabilistic graphical modeling and LLMs, offering a promising strategy for automatically constructing Bayesian Networks by combining LLM-extracted probabilistic knowledge with real-world data. It could significantly benefit automated knowledge acquisition and decision support systems, especially in domains where expert priors are expensive or data is scarce. The paper establishes the first comprehensive baseline for assessing LLM performance in extracting probabilistic knowledge, and explores using LLM-derived distributions as expert priors to refine distributions extracted from data, particularly when data is scarce. The evaluation covers 80 real-world Bayesian Networks and compares against random, uniform, and next-token generation probability baselines.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: A Bayesian Network is a probabilistic graphical model that represents a set of variables and their conditional dependencies via a directed acyclic graph, with each node associated with a conditional probability table. Parameterizing these networks traditionally requires domain experts to specify conditional probabilities, a task that grows exponentially with the number of parent nodes. Large Language Models have shown promise as factual knowledge bases, but their ability to generate probabilistic knowledge about real-world events remains understudied.

## [Contrastive 3D-CLIP grounding boosts text-to-CT generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.00633) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2506.00633v4) proposes a generation-oriented 3D-CLIP encoder trained with structured hard negatives that operate only at the text level, arguing that 3D vision-language alignment—not text encoder richness—is the primary bottleneck for semantic controllability in text-to-CT generation. The encoder conditions a fully end-to-end 3D latent diffusion model, and on the CT-RATE benchmark across 18 pathological conditions it achieves state-of-the-art image fidelity and factual correctness with less inference time and GPU memory than competing methods. This work reframes the text-to-CT problem by showing that grounding quality, not language model capacity, drives semantic controllability, which could redirect future medical image synthesis research toward better 3D vision-language alignment. If validated, it may enable more reliable synthetic CT volumes for diagnostics, data augmentation, and research, while being more computationally efficient than existing pipelines. The structured hard negatives are constructed exclusively at the text level, which increases contrastive difficulty without additional 3D memory cost and overcomes the small-batch constraints typical of volumetric encoders. The fully end-to-end latent diffusion model operates directly in 3D latent space, eliminating spatial artifacts and cross-slice inconsistencies introduced by super-resolution pipelines, and code is available at https://github.com/danielemolino/Text2CT.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Text-to-CT generation aims to synthesize 3D CT volumes from radiology reports, a task that could support diagnostics and data augmentation. Existing approaches typically condition diffusion models on text encoders pretrained with language-only or 2D vision-language objectives, which are linguistically expressive but lack volumetric grounding. CLIP is a widely used vision-language model trained on images and text, and extending it to 3D (e.g., CG3D) is an active research direction; contrastive learning with hard negatives is a standard technique for improving representation discrimination.

## [VPEngine: Shared-Backbone Multi-Head Inference for Robotic Vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.11584) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers released VPEngine, an open-source modular framework that runs multiple robotic vision tasks (depth, object detection, semantic segmentation) on a single shared DINOv2 foundation model backbone, with task-specific heads executing in parallel on the GPU. Using CUDA Multi-Process Service (MPS) and TensorRT optimization, it achieves up to 3x speedup over sequential execution and sustains at least 50 Hz end-to-end on an NVIDIA Jetson Orin AGX. Robots typically run separate models per perception task, which wastes GPU compute and memory on repeated feature extraction; VPEngine removes that redundancy and lets developers adjust per-task inference frequencies at runtime. This matters for edge and embedded robotics, where compute, memory, and power budgets are tight, and it lowers the barrier to deploying multi-task perception on real hardware. The framework is written in Python with ROS2 C++ (Humble) bindings, keeps a constant memory footprint, and avoids unnecessary GPU-CPU memory transfers by sharing image representations directly on the GPU. The reported 3x speedup and 50 Hz figures come from an example implementation with three heads on Jetson Orin AGX using TensorRT-optimized models, so real-world gains may vary with task count and hardware.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Foundation model backbones are reusable representation extractors (such as ViT or CNN backbones) that turn raw images into feature embeddings used by downstream tasks. In robotic vision, multi-task learning shares such representations across tasks like depth estimation, detection, and segmentation, but naive deployments run each model sequentially, repeating the expensive feature extraction step. CUDA Multi-Process Service (MPS) allows multiple GPU processes to share a single CUDA context, and TensorRT compiles models for faster inference on NVIDIA hardware such as the Jetson Orin AGX embedded platform.

## [Survey Traces Ultralytics YOLO Evolution from YOLOv5 to YOLO27](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.09653) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv survey (2510.09653v4) provides a comprehensive overview of the Ultralytics YOLO family, tracing architectural evolution, benchmarking, and deployment from YOLOv5 through YOLO27. It highlights YOLO27's scale-adaptive dual-architecture strategy, where compact YOLO27n/s models use streamlined CNNs with dual-scale prediction and NMS-free options, while YOLO27m/l adopt query-based transformer decoding, with YOLO27l adding an UltraViT backbone for global-context modeling. This survey consolidates a decade of rapid YOLO iteration into a single reference, helping practitioners and researchers compare accuracy, latency, and deployment trade-offs across generations. It also signals the field's shift toward NMS-free, transformer-augmented, and hardware-aware detection systems, which will influence how real-time vision models are built and deployed in robotics, agriculture, surveillance, and manufacturing. Preliminary COCO results for YOLO27 span 42.3–60.4 mAP at 640-pixel resolution with 0.62–2.32 ms TensorRT 11 FP16 latency, and YOLO27l reaches 61.2 mAP at 800 pixels. The review also covers YOLO26's DFL removal, Progressive Loss Balancing, Small-Target-Aware Label Assignment, MuSGD optimization, and NMS-free inference, alongside YOLO11, YOLOv8, and YOLOv5.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: YOLO (You Only Look Once) is a family of real-time object detection systems based on convolutional neural networks, first introduced by Joseph Redmon et al. in 2015; the name reflects that a single forward pass through the network produces predictions, unlike region-proposal methods such as R-CNN. Ultralytics maintains a widely used PyTorch-based implementation and ecosystem, and its releases from YOLOv5 onward have become a de facto standard for practical detection, segmentation, pose, and tracking tasks. Non-Maximum Suppression (NMS) is a post-processing step that removes duplicate detections, and recent YOLO versions aim to eliminate it for lower latency and simpler deployment.

## [ShotFinder Benchmark Enables Open-Domain Video Shot Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.23232) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced ShotFinder, a new benchmark and three-stage pipeline for open-domain video shot retrieval that formalizes editing requirements as keyframe-oriented shot descriptions with five controllable constraints: Temporal order, Color, Visual style, Audio, and Resolution. The benchmark contains 1,210 high-quality samples curated from YouTube across 20 thematic categories, generated by large models with human verification. This work fills a gap in multimodal retrieval research by providing the first systematic benchmark for open-domain video shot retrieval, which involves richer temporal structure and more complex semantics than text or static multimodal settings. The significant gap between model and human performance highlights a critical capability that multimodal large models have yet to overcome, guiding future research directions. The three-stage pipeline consists of query expansion via video imagination, candidate video retrieval with a search engine, and description-guided shot localization. Experiments on multiple closed-source and open-source models reveal clear imbalance across constraints: temporal localization is relatively tractable, while color and visual style remain major challenges.

**Background**: Open-domain video shot retrieval is a computational challenge defined as retrieving ranked, temporally localized video segments using free-form queries from diverse modalities. It employs CNNs, transformer-based models, and dense vector indexing to fuse visual, audio, and text features for effective shot segmentation and ranking. Existing research has mainly focused on text or static multimodal settings, leaving video shot retrieval lacking systematic benchmarks and analysis.

## [VG2S: Variational Graph-to-Scheduler Framework for Job Shop Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.00408) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces VG2S, a Variational Graph-to-Scheduler framework that applies variational inference to the Job Shop Scheduling Problem (JSSP) for the first time, deriving a probabilistic objective based on the Evidence Lower Bound (ELBO) combined with maximum entropy reinforcement learning. By mathematically decoupling representation learning from policy optimization through a variational graph encoder, the method achieves superior zero-shot generalization on large-scale benchmarks such as DMU and SWV compared to state-of-the-art DRL baselines and traditional dispatching rules. Job shop scheduling is a critical NP-hard combinatorial optimization problem in manufacturing, and this work demonstrates that principled probabilistic inference can significantly improve training stability and generalization of DRL-based schedulers. The framework's ability to generalize zero-shot to unseen, larger problem instances could reduce the need for costly retraining and make learned schedulers more practical for real-world production environments. The VG2S framework uses a variational graph encoder to learn robust structural representations of scheduling instances, and the ELBO-based objective with maximum entropy RL encourages exploration while improving robustness against hyperparameter variations. Experiments show particular advantages on large-scale and challenging benchmark instances like DMU and SWV, though the approach is evaluated primarily in simulation and may face challenges in dynamic real-world shop floors.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: The Job Shop Scheduling Problem (JSSP) involves scheduling a set of jobs, each consisting of ordered operations that must be processed on specific machines, with the goal of minimizing the total completion time (makespan). It is a well-known NP-hard combinatorial optimization problem with wide applications in manufacturing and supply chains. Variational inference is a technique for approximating intractable posterior distributions in Bayesian models, while maximum entropy reinforcement learning augments reward maximization with an entropy bonus to encourage exploration and improve robustness. Graph neural networks are often used to represent scheduling instances as graphs, where operations and machines form nodes and edges.

## [HALT: Lightweight LLM Hallucination Detection via Log-prob Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.02888) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced HALT (Hallucination Assessment via Log-probs as Time series), a lightweight hallucination detector that uses only the top-20 token log-probabilities from LLM generations as a time series, processed by a GRU model with entropy-based features. They also released HUB (Hallucination detection Unified Benchmark), consolidating prior datasets into ten capabilities, and reported that HALT is 30x smaller than the fine-tuned modernBERT-base encoder Lettuce while outperforming it with a 60x speedup on HUB. Hallucinations remain a major obstacle for LLMs in safety-critical domains, and HALT offers an efficient detection approach that works with proprietary LLMs without needing internal weights, hidden states, or attention maps. Its compatibility with closed-source models and strong domain generalization could make hallucination detection more practical for real-world deployments. HALT relies only on output log-probabilities rather than surface-form text, distinguishing it from black-box approaches, and it avoids the need for hidden states or attention maps unlike white-box methods. The HUB benchmark covers both reasoning tasks (Algorithmic, Commonsense, Mathematical, Symbolic, Code Generation) and general purpose skills (Chat, Data-to-Text, Question Answering, Summarization, World Knowledge).

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models can generate plausible but incorrect or unsupported content, known as hallucinations, which is especially risky in safety-critical applications. Log-probabilities reflect how confident a model is in its token predictions, and treating them as a time series allows sequential models like GRUs to detect patterns associated with hallucination. GRUs are a type of recurrent neural network designed for sequential data, making them well-suited to this task.

## [PACT-WAM Cuts Robot Manipulation Tokens by 75% with Joint Action-Visual Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.15882) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PACT-WAM introduces a world-action model that jointly generates a 16-step action trajectory and temporally corresponding visual forecasts via conditional flow sampling, using hierarchical history encoding to retain 16 observations at 256 tokens per view — 75% fewer than dense encoding. Without Proposal Review (PR), it achieves 98.6%, 92.3%, and 78.0% success on LIBERO, RoboTwin 2.0, and real-world Piper tasks, rising to 99.5%, 93.4%, and 86.7% with PR. This work addresses a key bottleneck in world-action models — the high computational cost of dense temporal representations — by showing that compact encoding can preserve control performance while drastically reducing token usage. If validated, such efficiency gains could make joint action-visual prediction more practical for real-time robot manipulation and broader deployment on resource-constrained platforms. The model uses a shared flow module with two modality-specific heads under transition-wise causal attention, and a TiTok-VAE decoder reconstructs multi-view future images from visual latents. Ablations confirm that hierarchical history allocation and joint action-visual generation improve control success, while forecast-guided execution via PR reveals trade-offs between success rate and proposal-generation cost.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: World-action models (WAMs) are robot policies that explicitly leverage world modeling — predicting future states or representations of the environment — to support or jointly perform action prediction, as opposed to vision-language-action (VLA) models that rely on VLM backbones. Conditional flow sampling is a generative technique that learns a velocity field to transform a simple distribution into a target conditional distribution, enabling efficient sampling of actions and visual forecasts. TiTok-VAE refers to a VAE variant that tokenizes images into compact latent representations, which here helps decode future images from a small number of visual tokens.

## [MINT Transfers MRI Biomarkers to Speech for Early Alzheimer's Screening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.23994) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose MINT (Multimodal Imaging-to-Speech Knowledge Transfer), a three-stage framework in which an MRI teacher model defines a compact embedding space for CN-versus-MCI classification and a residual projection head aligns speech representations to that space via a combined geometric loss. On ADNI-4, the aligned speech model achieves performance comparable to speech baselines, and multimodal fusion improves over MRI alone (0.973 vs 0.958), enabling imaging-free inference. Structural MRI biomarkers for Alzheimer's require costly infrastructure that limits population-scale screening, whereas speech is non-invasive and easily collected. MINT is the first demonstration of MRI-to-speech knowledge transfer for early Alzheimer's screening without imaging at inference, potentially enabling low-cost, scalable cognitive impairment screening. The framework uses a frozen MRI classifier for imaging-free inference, and ablations identify dropout regularization and self-supervised pretraining as important design choices. The reported fusion result of 0.973 versus 0.958 for MRI alone comes from the ADNI-4 dataset.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Alzheimer's disease is a progressive neurodegenerative disorder in which mild cognitive impairment (MCI) often precedes dementia, making early detection important. Structural MRI provides validated biomarkers but is expensive and not widely accessible, while speech offers a non-invasive alternative but lacks biological grounding when used alone. MINT builds on knowledge distillation, where a teacher model transfers knowledge to a student model, here transferring MRI-derived biomarker structure to speech representations.

## [Domain-aware Fourier Features Boost Physics-Informed Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02948) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2603.02948v2) proposes Domain-aware Fourier Features (DaFFs) for positional encoding in Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs), encoding domain geometry and boundary conditions directly into the input representation. The approach eliminates explicit boundary condition loss terms and loss balancing schemes, and is paired with an LRP-based explainability framework that extracts relevance attribution scores for the input space. PINNs are notoriously difficult to train and interpret, so removing boundary-condition loss terms and loss balancing while achieving orders-of-magnitude lower errors and faster convergence could substantially simplify workflows for the scientific machine learning community. The added LRP-based interpretability also addresses a long-standing weakness of PINNs, potentially making them more trustworthy for physics and engineering applications. Unlike Random Fourier Features (RFFs), DaFFs encapsulate all domain-specific characteristics such as geometry and boundary conditions, simplifying optimization and reducing training computational cost. LRP analysis shows PINN-DaFFs yield more physically consistent feature attributions, whereas PINN-RFFs and vanilla PINNs display scattered, less physics-relevant patterns.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) embed partial differential equations (PDEs) into the loss function so that a neural network learns solutions consistent with physical laws, but they often require careful tuning of boundary condition terms and loss weights. Fourier features are a positional encoding technique that maps input coordinates through sinusoidal functions to help neural networks overcome spectral bias and learn high-frequency details. Layer-wise Relevance Propagation (LRP) is an explainable AI method that attributes a model's prediction back to its input features by propagating relevance scores through the network layers.

## [AuthorMix: Modular Style Transfer via Layer-wise LoRA Adapter Mixing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.23069) ⭐️ 7.0/10

AuthorMix introduces a lightweight, modular authorship style transfer framework that trains style-specific LoRA adapters on a small set of high-resource authors and then combines them via reinforcement-learned layer-wise mixing to rapidly adapt to new target authors using only a handful of examples. It ranks first on the combined style-meaning score among all baselines, including GPT-5.1, and is the only method best-or-tied on every dimension in human evaluation. This work addresses two long-standing limitations of authorship style transfer — inflexibility of single-model approaches and poor meaning preservation — by showing that modular, composable adapters can outperform even very large proprietary models on low-resource targets. It suggests a practical path toward personalized, few-shot style adaptation that could benefit NLP researchers and applications in education, cultural preservation, and content generation. The framework trains individual style-specific LoRA adapters and learns layer-wise mixing weights via reinforcement learning, which the authors find substantially outperforms static adapter-wise mixing. Adaptation to a new target author requires only a handful of target-style training examples, and the paper identifies layer-specific, gradient-based mixing as a promising direction for future work in model merging.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Authorship style transfer is the task of rewriting text to match a target author's writing style while preserving the original meaning. Existing methods typically train a single large model on large corpora to cover all target styles at once, which is costly, inflexible for new targets, and often trades away meaning preservation. LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) is a technique that freezes a pre-trained model's weights and injects small trainable low-rank matrices into each layer, enabling cheap, modular fine-tuning for specific tasks or styles.

## [AMIGO Benchmark Tests Agentic VLMs on Multi-Image Hidden-Target Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.28662) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced AMIGO (Agentic Multi-Image Grounding Oracle Benchmark), a long-horizon benchmark where an oracle privately selects a target image from a gallery of visually similar images, and the model must recover it by asking a sequence of attribute-focused Yes/No questions under a strict protocol that returns Yes/No/Unsure feedback and penalizes invalid actions with Skip. The benchmark is instantiated with the 'Guess My Preferred Dress' task and evaluates open-source VLMs across metrics covering identification success, evidence verification, efficiency, protocol compliance, robustness to controlled feedback perturbations, and trajectory-level diagnostics. Most existing evaluations of vision-language models focus on single-image, single-turn correctness, leaving a gap in assessing long-horizon, multi-turn interactive grounding. AMIGO addresses this gap and shows that final-answer accuracy alone overstates evidence-grounded performance, which matters for anyone building or deploying agentic VLMs that must act through extended interactions. The benchmark stresses three capabilities: question selection under uncertainty, consistent constraint tracking across turns, and fine-grained discrimination as evidence accumulates. Results show models can guess correctly without verification-passing evidence, waste turns through invalid questions, or fail to preserve the upload protocol, and that model scale alone does not guarantee reliable long-horizon interactive grounding.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) connect visual perception and language, and are increasingly used as agents that act through extended interactions rather than single responses. Multi-image grounding refers to locating or identifying a target across multiple images, a harder problem than single-image grounding because the model must compare and reason over several visual inputs. AMIGO builds on these ideas by turning hidden-target identification into a sequential question-asking game with strict protocol rules.

## [LLM Writing Assistance Is Erasing Native Language Signals in Academic English](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08568) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2604.08568v4) constructs two native language identification (NLI) datasets of academic abstracts from arXiv and the ACL Anthology, covering eight L1 groups across three eras: pre-neural-network, pre-LLM, and post-LLM. Evaluating LLM-fine-tuned NLI classifiers, the authors find a consistent decline in NLI performance over time, and a rewriting experiment with recent LLMs produces an even larger drop than the historical trend. The findings suggest that LLM-based writing assistance may be homogenizing academic English and erasing the subtle L1 fingerprints that NLI relies on, which matters for NLP researchers, sociolinguists, and anyone concerned about linguistic diversity in scholarly communication. It also implies that NLI as a task may become substantially harder as LLM-assisted writing spreads. The decline is more pronounced from the pre-NN era to the pre-LLM era than from the pre-LLM era to the post-LLM era, indicating that homogenization began gradually with neural approaches rather than suddenly with LLMs. The paper's rewriting experiment with recent LLMs shows a larger NLI performance drop than the progression across eras alone, suggesting further homogenization may lie ahead.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Native language identification (NLI) is the task of determining an author's native language (L1) from their writing in a second language (L2), based on recurring language-use patterns specific to L1 groups; it has applications in second-language teaching and forensic linguistics. Linguistic homogenization refers to distinct languages or dialects becoming more similar or being replaced by a dominant one, a concept borrowed from sociology. The ACL Anthology is a major open archive of computational linguistics and NLP publications, while arXiv is a widely used preprint server, making both suitable sources for longitudinal study of academic English.

## [Tutorial Bridges Deep Learning and Operations Research for Sequential Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11507) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv tutorial (2604.11507v2) presents an operations research/management science (OR/MS) perspective on deep learning for sequential decision making under uncertainty, organizing the field around three themes: predict-then-optimize and decision-aware learning, learning-based decision generation under constraints, and deep reinforcement learning for sequential and combinatorial problems. It argues that deep learning complements optimization rather than replacing it, bridging neural architectures such as transformers, LLMs, and deep RL with OR/MS mathematical foundations. As AI shifts from prediction toward decision-making in complex, uncertain environments, this tutorial offers researchers and practitioners a unified framework that combines the adaptability and scalability of deep learning with the mathematical rigor of OR/MS for constraints, recourse, and uncertainty. Its impact spans supply chains, service systems, healthcare, agriculture, energy, environmental sustainability, and autonomous operations. The tutorial is a synthesis and overview rather than a new algorithmic breakthrough, and it explicitly frames its contribution as an OR/MS-centered perspective that connects decision-making foundations to modern neural architectures. It emphasizes three central themes and positions these developments as part of a broader shift from predictive AI toward decision-capable AI.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Sequential decision making under uncertainty involves choosing actions over time when outcomes are uncertain, and it is often formalized using frameworks such as Markov decision processes (MDPs). Operations research and management science (OR/MS) has long provided the mathematical foundations for such problems, including optimization, constraints, and recourse. Deep learning, especially deep reinforcement learning, allows agents to learn decisions directly from large, unstructured inputs without manually engineering the state space, which has driven applications in robotics, games, transportation, finance, and healthcare.

## [VISTA uses validation-informed self-distillation to prevent trajectory deviation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.12044) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced VISTA, an online self-distillation framework that addresses a previously under-explored optimization failure called Trajectory Deviation, where models abandon high-generalization states during training. VISTA uses a validation-informed Marginal Coverage score to identify expert anchors—earlier model states with specialized competence—and integrates a coverage-weighted ensemble of these anchors online, improving robustness and generalization while reducing storage overhead by 90%. This work highlights a subtle failure mode where strong validation accuracy masks the loss of generalizable features, which could affect any deep learning model trained with standard procedures. By mitigating this issue without large storage costs, VISTA offers a practical path to more robust models across benchmarks, potentially influencing how practitioners monitor and regularize training. VISTA identifies expert anchors via a validation-informed Marginal Coverage score and regularizes the loss landscape by integrating a coverage-weighted ensemble of these anchors online. A lightweight implementation reduces storage overhead by 90% without performance loss, though the abstract is truncated and lacks full experimental details.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Self-distillation is a technique where a neural network leverages its own predictions or internal representations to refine learning without an external teacher, often improving accuracy and robustness. Trajectory Deviation is a newly termed optimization failure where models discard previously learned latent features as training proceeds, without triggering classical overfitting signals. Marginal Coverage is a validation-informed score that measures how well model states cover distinct data regions, used here to select expert anchors.

## [HINTBench: New Benchmark Audits Intrinsic Risks in AI Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.13954) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced HINTBench, a benchmark of 596 agent trajectories (400 synthetic risky, 136 synthetic safe, 30 reconstructed real-world risky, and 30 reconstructed real-world safe, averaging 24.0 steps) for auditing intrinsic non-attack risks under benign conditions. It supports three tasks—risk detection, risk-step localization, and intrinsic failure-type identification—and experiments show strong LLMs perform well on trajectory-level detection but the best model scores below 37 Strict-F1 on fine-grained risk-step localization. Existing agent-safety evaluation has focused mainly on externally induced risks like prompt injection and malicious tools, leaving intrinsic failures under benign conditions underexplored. HINTBench establishes intrinsic risk auditing as an open challenge, highlighting that current LLMs and off-the-shelf guard models transfer poorly to this setting, which matters for anyone deploying long-horizon autonomous agents. The benchmark's annotations are organized under a unified five-constraint taxonomy, and the dataset mixes synthetic and reconstructed real-world trajectories. A notable caveat is that existing off-the-shelf guard models, evaluated under their native prompts, transfer poorly to this setting, and the abstract lacks detailed quantitative results beyond the Strict-F1 figure.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: AI agents are systems that autonomously execute multi-step tasks, and their safety has traditionally been tested against external attacks such as prompt injection, malicious tools, or environment manipulation. Intrinsic risk refers to unsafe trajectories that arise from internal failures even under benign, non-adversarial conditions, where errors remain latent and propagate across long-horizon execution. HINTBench is a guard-oriented evaluation task designed to detect, localize, and diagnose such failures in agent trajectories.

## [Schema Key Wording Acts as Hidden Instruction Channel in Constrained Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.14862) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2604.14862v3) presents the first systematic study showing that the wording of schema keys in constrained decoding acts as an implicit instruction channel, not just a structural constraint. Experiments on GSM8K and Math500 across seven language models show that changing only schema-key wording can substantially affect accuracy, with both positive and negative effects, and that prompt-level and schema-level instructions interact non-additively. The findings suggest that schema design is part of instruction specification in structured generation, not merely output formatting, which could reshape prompt engineering and schema design practices for anyone building JSON-mode or grammar-constrained LLM pipelines. It also implies that developers may inadvertently help or hurt model accuracy simply through their choice of field names. The paper formulates structured generation as a multi-channel instruction problem where task signals can be placed in prompts, schema keys, or both, and provides a projection-aware analysis giving a sufficient condition under which an unconstrained expected-score advantage of an instructional key is preserved after grammar projection. The evidence is substantially stronger on GSM8K than on Math500, indicating the effect is task-dependent.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Constrained decoding forces large language models to produce outputs that satisfy a formal schema such as JSON by restricting token probabilities during generation, guaranteeing syntactic validity. Prior work has largely treated schemas as pure structural constraints, overlooking that schema-key tokens also enter the autoregressive context and may guide generation. GSM8K and Math500 are widely used grade-school and competition-level math reasoning benchmarks used to measure LLM accuracy.

## [Shadow Mask Distillation Compresses KV Cache for Memory-Efficient RL Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06850) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2605.06850v2) introduces Shadow Mask Distillation (SMD), a method that compresses the KV cache during reinforcement learning rollouts while correcting the resulting off-policy bias. SMD uses a dual-track loss to align the training signal from the compressed rollout with the full-context policy, enabling stable GRPO/PPO post-training under aggressive KV cache compression. Long-context reasoning tasks in RL post-training suffer from a severe memory wall due to the KV cache footprint during rollouts, and naive compression causes complete learning collapse with zero reward. SMD offers a practical path to memory-efficient alignment of long-context LLMs, potentially lowering the hardware barrier for RLHF and RLAIF workflows. The paper theoretically formalizes why importance reweighting, used in prior work such as Sparse-RL, is fundamentally unstable for long-context tasks due to high gradient variance and sample inefficiency. SMD instead applies an auxiliary mask to distill full-context behavior into the compressed rollout, aligning the two distributions closely enough for stable optimization.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning from human or AI feedback (RLHF/RLAIF) has become a key paradigm for unlocking LLM reasoning, and algorithms like PPO, GRPO, and Online DPO all require generating exploratory trajectories (rollouts) during training. For long-context tasks, storing the Key-Value (KV) cache for these rollouts consumes enormous GPU memory, creating a memory wall. KV cache compression is nearly lossless for standard inference, but in RL the sampler sees a sparse context while the learner updates on the full dense context, producing an off-policy bias that RL's instability amplifies dramatically.

## [TC-LIA Detects Mirage Reasoning in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00435) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Text-Conditioned Layer-wise Internal Alignment (TC-LIA), an unsupervised, model-agnostic method that detects mirage reasoning in vision-language models by tracking question-image alignment across layers of a frozen CLIP ViT-H/14 encoder. On 19,004 samples across ten VQA domains, fourteen state-of-the-art VLMs showed base mirage rates of 57.3-75.0%, which TC-LIA alone reduced to 7.5% with 83.5% classification accuracy, and an ensemble reached up to 88.4% accuracy with a 6.4% mirage rate. Mirage reasoning is a significant failure mode where VLMs produce confident answers without visual evidence, undermining trust in multimodal AI. This work offers a practical, training-free detection method that could improve VLM reliability and safety in real-world deployments, especially for researchers in AI safety and VLM evaluation. TC-LIA is purely unsupervised with fixed projections and scoring weights, requiring no labels or training, and it summarizes patch-text alignment using final similarity, late-layer top-k alignment, early-to-late gain, and slope. When combined with blank/noise detection, domain routing, and VLM self-assessment, the ensemble's supervised training is optional, and an ensemble trained on a single backbone transfers well to unseen backbones, staying within 1.2% accuracy points of per-backbone training across thirteen held-out VLMs.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) combine visual and textual understanding, often using a CLIP (Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training) encoder to align images and text. Mirage reasoning occurs when a VLM answers a visual question correctly but without actually using the image, relying instead on dataset biases or language priors. Detecting such cases before releasing an answer is crucial for trustworthy multimodal systems.

## [Libra: Adaptive Runtime for Efficient Agentic RL Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03077) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers present Libra, an adaptive runtime for agentic RL post-training that combines a Causality-Guided Bucket Scheduler for intra-stage scheduling with cross-stage coordination that dynamically reallocates workers between rollout and training. Evaluated on a 48x NVIDIA A800 GPU cluster and a 160x Ascend 910B3 NPU cluster across three agentic benchmarks, Libra achieves up to 4.2x higher throughput and up to 2.7x faster reward convergence. Agentic RL post-training is becoming a standard way to turn LLMs into capable tool-using agents, but its long-tailed rollout workloads and shifting resource demands between rollout and training create major efficiency bottlenecks. Libra's adaptive scheduling and non-blocking worker reallocation could meaningfully reduce GPU idle time and speed up training for researchers and practitioners building agentic LLM systems. The Causality-Guided Bucket Scheduler routes requests across execution buckets with different parallelism configurations to reduce delays caused by rollout stragglers, while cross-stage coordination moves workers between rollout and training through a non-blocking protocol without interrupting ongoing training. The system was validated on both NVIDIA A800 GPUs and Ascend 910B3 NPUs, showing cross-hardware generality.

**Background**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has become a standard post-training paradigm for shaping large language models (LLMs) into capable agents. In agentic RL, the rollout stage generates trajectories while invoking external tools, producing long-tailed and non-stationary workloads where a small fraction of trajectories dominates rollout makespan. Rollout and training also differ in compute patterns, memory demands, and sensitivity to sequence length, and as the policy evolves, shifts in the workload distribution further change their relative resource demands.
