## [PACT: Provenance-Aware Fusion Prevents False Corroboration in Human-Robot Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01662) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.01662v2) introduces PACT (Provenance-Aware evidence Conservation and Typed action admission), a multi-view fusion method that separates evidence magnitude from countability using a supplied provenance partition. Under singleton fidelity and insertion non-amplification, the coordinatewise meet is proven to be the unique pointwise greatest admissible within-component rule, and experiments show that duplicating prompt outputs within a camera from multiplicity one to eight leaves all 720 typed responses per checkpoint unchanged. This work addresses a fundamental flaw in multi-view and multi-modal fusion: repeated inference over the same observation can inflate confidence without adding independent evidence. For human-robot collaboration, where action admission decisions must be trustworthy, PACT provides formal guarantees that agreement only counts as corroboration when it comes from genuinely separate sources. The method relies on a supplied provenance partition and assumes singleton fidelity, insertion non-amplification, commensurability, and separate-component additivity; component budgets add only under these stated assumptions. In four of 12 replicated-source tests on HandWritten, false refinement lowered macro-averaged negative log-likelihood and Brier score while increasing normalized common-support area under the risk-coverage curve (ncsAURC), and in the controlled handover benchmark removing the adversarial-consensus condition left a 0.056 reduction in ncsAURC for provenance-partition aggregation relative to singleton aggregation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: In multi-view learning and human-robot collaboration, systems often fuse predictions from multiple cameras, prompts, or models to make decisions. A common assumption is that if several views agree, the agreement provides independent corroboration, but this fails when the views are derived from the same underlying observation. Provenance refers to the origin and derivation history of each piece of evidence, and PACT treats provenance as part of the fusion state so that repeated outputs can refine a prediction without multiplying the support available for action admission.

## [Calendar-SPCA embeds daily, weekly, annual cycles into sparse PCA for interpretable electricity profiles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06060) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces Calendar-Structured Sparse Principal Component Analysis (Calendar-SPCA), a structured representation-learning method that embeds known daily, weekly, and annual calendar geometry directly into a low-dimensional factorization. Evaluated on the GoiEner and Low Carbon London public smart-meter datasets, at rank 15 it retains 96.92% and 82.90% of the explained variance of rank-matched PCA, with mean loading sparsities of 61.95% and 81.50%. Electricity consumption profiles carry multiple simultaneous periodicities, and standard PCA loadings are dense and hard to interpret; Calendar-SPCA produces sparse, locally coherent factors aligned with calendar axes, which can help utilities and researchers extract interpretable consumption patterns from smart-meter data. It is a solid technical contribution to time-series analysis and interpretable machine learning, though its application remains niche. The method represents the feature domain as the Cartesian product of cyclic calendar axes and combines an L1 loading penalty with graph total variation to encourage sparsity and local coherence. A factorial experiment characterizes the effects of sparsity and calendar coherence and examines robustness across sample size, latent dimensionality, and repeated fits, with comparisons against classical sparse PCA and sparse PCA with total variation (SPCA-TV).

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Principal component analysis (PCA) is a widely used dimensionality-reduction technique, but each principal component is a linear combination of all original variables, making results hard to interpret. Sparse PCA adds sparsity constraints (e.g., via lasso or elastic net) so components load on only a few variables, improving interpretability. Total variation is a mathematical measure of how much a function or signal changes locally, and graph total variation extends this to values defined on graph nodes, encouraging piecewise-constant or locally smooth solutions. Smart meters record fine-grained electricity consumption, and long-term profiles exhibit daily, weekly, and annual cycles that motivate calendar-aware models.

A new arXiv paper introduces context segmentation, a two-level agentic framework that splits complex exploitation tasks into contextually isolated sub-problems to reduce context bloat. Evaluated on the picoCTF dataset with memory-constrained gemma-4 models, the E4B model solved 18.52% of tasks that standard agentic execution failed to complete while using fewer tokens than brute-force retries. The work highlights a dual-use risk: locally deployed open-weight SLMs can bypass proprietary API guardrails and perform advanced cybersecurity tasks, so understanding and mitigating their capabilities matters for AI safety. It also offers a practical context-management technique that could improve long-horizon agentic performance beyond cybersecurity. The framework uses a dual-agent design that separates high-level strategic reasoning from low-level execution clutter, and the code is released at https://github.com/9xeb/context-segmentation. Results are limited to the picoCTF dataset and memory-constrained gemma-4 models, and the abstract is truncated, so broader generalization remains unverified.

## [New Training Criterion Enables Deep Boltzmann Machines for Statistical Data Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14934) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces observed-block multi-prediction, a discriminative training criterion for Deep Boltzmann Machines (DBMs) that restricts the multi-prediction objective to targets each row actually observes, making it well-defined for any missingness pattern and reducing to the original criterion when rows are complete. It is evaluated on a consumer purchase panel and public-domain census microdata, showing that the DBM's advantage comes almost entirely from conditioning on one outcome block when predicting the other, contributing +0.19 and +0.36 percentage points and being positive in all 40 cells. This work enables DBMs to be applied to statistical data fusion, where two panels share covariates but observe disjoint outcome blocks and no row observes both outcomes, a setting that previously ruled out discriminative training. It also provides evidence that generative pre-training contributes little to the DBM's advantage, which could simplify future model design and training for fusion tasks. The method is evaluated over grids in sample size and covariate width spanning 40 cells and 200 runs per method; the fine-tuned DBM is the best method in 37 of 40 cells against baselines tuned on validation and given the same conditioning. The conditioning term is positive in all 40 cells, never decays as panels grow (flat on one dataset, growing on the other), and requires neither a second hidden layer nor more inference.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Deep Boltzmann Machines are a type of deep generative model within the family of Boltzmann machines, which are stochastic Ising models and Markov random fields trained via energy-based learning. Multi-prediction training is a discriminative criterion that requires ground truth for held-out targets, making it inapplicable when no row observes both outcome blocks. Statistical data fusion combines two panels that share a block of covariates but observe disjoint outcome blocks, and in its traditional form no row observes both outcomes at once.

## [Geospatial Metadata Boosts Cross-Disciplinary Dataset Discoverability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16498) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper analyzes Harvard Dataverse and finds that datasets with more missing geospatial and thematic metadata receive fewer downstream citations and have fewer resolvable connections to other datasets. Only 0.3% of research datasets include a bounding box, and after embedding datasets in a metadata knowledge graph, the authors find datasets are twice as likely to connect across scientific disciplines through shared geospatial metadata as through keywords. The findings suggest that geographic metadata is a more reliable basis for cross-disciplinary interoperability than keyword vocabularies, which often remain discipline-specific, and they highlight how voluntary metadata curation directly affects citation impact and data reuse. This matters for repository managers, funders, and researchers who rely on FAIR data principles to make datasets findable and reusable across fields. The study trains and fine-tunes a small language model on Harvard Dataverse datasets, and through geospatial metadata enrichment it increases the share of datasets from different disciplines connected through metadata elements from 58.5% to 63.2%. Most geospatial records represent archival points rather than complete geographic shapes, and missing metadata fields represent lost information that reduces interoperability.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Research data repositories are essential infrastructure for scientific inquiry and for ensuring datasets follow FAIR (Findable, Accessible, Interoperable, and Reusable) principles, a set of guidelines first published in 2016 to improve the management of digital assets. Harvard Dataverse is the world's largest general-purpose research repository, hosting hundreds of thousands of citable datasets across every discipline, but researchers generally provide geospatial and thematic metadata voluntarily, so incomplete records are common given limited curation resources. A bounding box is a geographic metadata field that defines the spatial extent of a dataset using coordinates, and it is a key element for enabling geographic search and linking.

A new arXiv preprint (2609.17171v2) introduces Adaptive Derivative-Ordered Random Explanation (ADORE), a unified post-hoc interpretability framework that uses first- and second-order derivatives to capture nonlinear feature interactions and feature-sample contributions. The authors report that ADORE outperforms LIME and SHAP on tabular, text, and image data, and have released it as an open-source Python package on GitHub. Interpretability is critical in high-stakes domains like healthcare and finance, where opaque model decisions can have serious consequences. By unifying global feature importance and local sample-level explanations in one framework, ADORE could give practitioners a more complete and computationally efficient alternative to widely used tools like LIME and SHAP. ADORE quantifies feature impact by capturing both magnitude and direction, and identifies critical samples influencing model decisions. It achieves scalability through randomized singular value decomposition (SVD) and dynamic sparsity detection, though as an arXiv preprint it has not yet undergone peer review or community validation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 17, 04:00

**Background**: Post-hoc interpretability methods explain a trained model's behavior after training, and are typically model-agnostic tools such as LIME and SHAP. These methods often struggle with nonlinear feature interactions, computational cost, and fragmented global versus local analyses. ADORE addresses these gaps by using first- and second-order derivatives, analogous to gradients and Hessians used in optimization, to model nonlinearity within a single analytical framework.
